# Preface

Hey there, and welcome to the first edition of *Computational Intelligence for Connective Cognition Networks: Advances and Applications*! This book feels like it’s arriving at just the right time—a moment when computational intelligence is hitting its stride, blending clever algorithms, sprawling networks, and ideas that actually matter to people’s lives. It’s changing how we see the world and how we tackle its messier problems. To me, “connective cognition networks” is all about that sweet spot where machines start thinking a bit more like us and networks tie it all together, letting us take on challenges across all sorts of fields.

What you’ll find in these pages is a mix of fresh ideas and real-world grit. We’ve got chapters that dig into everything from sharpening ECG analysis with some fancy modeling and machine learning tricks to poking around the wild possibilities of quantum networking. It’s a broad sweep—think genetic algorithms sprucing up images, security setups for drones, or figuring out what’s fake in photos and videos. Each piece here nudges the limits of what smart tech can do. And it doesn’t stop there—we’re also looking at how AI can make roads safer, sort out multilingual documents, sniff out fake news, and even help teachers prep better lessons with big language models. It’s all about the potential to shake things up.

This book didn’t just happen on its own—it’s the work of an amazing crew of researchers, hands-on experts, and big thinkers who’ve poured their know-how into it. They’ve not only pushed the theory behind connective cognition networks further but also shown how it can solve real problems out there in the world. As editors, we’ve tried to pull all these threads into something that makes sense together—something that’s deep but not intimidating, so whether you’re a pro in this stuff or just curious, there’s something here for you.

Putting this together has been quite a ride, full of “aha” moments and back-and-forth chats. I can’t thank the authors enough for bringing their A-game, or the reviewers for catching things we missed and making it all sharper. And a big shoutout to the publishing folks who stuck with us to get this out the door.

We hope Connective Cognition Networks: Advances and Applications lights a spark—maybe it’ll teach you something new, get you thinking, or even push you to question where all this tech is headed. Whether you’re a researcher, a pro in the field, or a student dipping your toes in, we want you to see what’s happening now and maybe catch a peek at what’s coming next. These tools and ideas are weaving new links between data, machines, and how we think—opening doors we’re only starting to peek through.

So, dive in, flip through, and come along with us on this journey into connective cognition networks. We’re excited to have you here!

# Acknowledgments

Pulling together *Computational Intelligence for Connective Cognition Networks: Advances and Applications* has been a team effort, and I’m genuinely grateful for everyone who’s had a hand in it. This book wouldn’t be what it is without a whole bunch of folks who’ve chipped in with their time, talent, and encouragement.

First off, a massive thank you to all the authors who’ve poured their hearts and minds into these chapters. You’ve brought such a range of ideas and expertise to the table—everything from ECG analysis to quantum networking and beyond. It’s been a real privilege to see your work come together and shape this collection. Your willingness to share, tweak, and polish has made this something special.

I also want to give a big nod to our reviewers. You’ve been the unsung heroes here, diving into drafts with sharp eyes and thoughtful feedback. Your suggestions didn’t just catch the little stuff—they helped us sharpen the big picture, too. Thanks for keeping us on our toes.

Finally, a shoutout to the broader research community and everyone out there pushing the boundaries of computational intelligence. You’re the reason a book like this even makes sense—your curiosity and drive light the way for projects like ours And to my family and friends—thanks for putting up with me disappearing into this project. Your support, whether it was a kind word or just letting me ramble about networks and AI, kept me going.

This has been a wild ride, and I’m so thankful to everyone who’s ridden along with us. Here’s to you all—cheers for making *Connective Cognition Networks* happen!

# About the Editors

**Kirti Aggarwal** is working as a senior assistant professor in the Department of Computer Science & Engineering, Jaypee Institute of Information Technology, Noida, India. She received her Ph.D. degree in Computer Science & Engineering from Jaypee Institute of Technology, Noida in September 2023. She has more than 10 years of academic experience. Her research interests include social networking, nature inspired computing, optimization algorithms, DBMS, discrete mathematics, computer networks, compiler design, and java programming. She has published papers in International Journal and Conferences.

**Anuja Arora** is working as a professor in the Computer Science & Engineering Department of Jaypee Institute of Information Technology, Noida, India. She has academic and research experience of 19 years and industry experience of 1.5 years. She received her Ph.D. degree in Computer Science from Apaji Institute of Mathematics & Applied Computer Technology, Banasthali University, Banasthali, India in December 2013. She is a Senior IEEE Member, ACM Member, SIAM Member, INSTICC, and Life Member of IAENG. She has published more than 100 research papers in peer-reviewed international journals, book chapters, and conferences. Three students have been awarded Ph.D. under her supervision and three are in process. Her research interest includes deep learning, artificial neural network, social network analysis and mining, sustainable computing, data science, machine learning, data mining, web intelligence, web application development and web technologies, software engineering, software testing, and information retrieval systems. Dr. Arora participated in many international conferences as an organizer, session chair, and member of national advisory and International Program Committees. She is an editorial board member of numerous IGI, Inderscience, and Bentham international journals. She is the reviewer of many reputed and peer-reviewed IEEE transactions—TKDE, TNSM, IEEE Transaction of Cybernetics, etc. She is also the reviewer of various international Journals.

**Zahid Akhtar** received his Ph.D. in electronic and computer engineering from the University of Cagliari, Italy. He is currently an assistant professor at the Department of Network and Computer Security, State University of New York (SUNY) Polytechnic Institute, USA. Prior to that, he was a research assistant professor with the University of Memphis, USA, and a postdoctoral fellow with the INRSEMT, University of Quebec, Canada, the University of Udine, Italy, Bahcesehir University, Turkey, and the University of Cagliari. His research interests include computer vision and machine learning with applications to cybersecurity, biometrics, affect recognition, image and video processing, and audiovisual multimedia quality assessment.

**Alessandro Bruno** earned a Ph.D. degree in computer engineering from DINFO, Palermo University. He is now a tenure-track assistant professor with the Department of Business, Law, Economics, and Consumer Behaviour at IULM University, in Milan, Italy. Before joining IULM University, Alessandro was an assistant professor at Humanities University (Milano, Italy). He worked as a lecturer in computing with the Department of Computing and Informatics, Bournemouth University in 2021 and 2022. He covered postdoc positions at NCCA (National Centre for Computer Animation) in the UK, INAF IASF (Italian National Institute for Astrophysics), University of Palermo, and IZS (Istituto Zooprofilattico Sperimentale della Sicilia). He was a research visitor at Mullard Space Science Laboratory (MSSL), University College London (UCL) in the imaging group led by Professor Jan-Peter Muller. He is the author of more than 50 International articles and serves as a associate editor for International journals. Alessandro is currently the principal investigator of a project funded by the NGI-Search consortium named HeReFaNMi (Health-Related Fake News Mitigation). His research interests include computer vision, artificial intelligence, and image analysis. He has mostly dealt with visual attention and visual saliency, biomedical imaging, crowd behavior analysis, image and video forensics, remote sensing, and human–computer interaction.

# Chapter 1Enhancing ECG analysis through parametric quartic spline modeling and machine learning classification

Alka Mishra, Surekha Bhusnur, Santosh Kumar Mishra, and Pushpendra Singh

DOI: [10.1201/9781003569619-1](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003569619-1)

## **1.1** Introduction

Electrocardiogram (ECG) signals play a vital role in diagnosing various cardiac abnormalities and monitoring heart health. These signals, which represent the electrical activity of the heart, provide valuable insights into the heart’s functioning and can aid in the detection of conditions such as arrhythmias, ischemia, and myocardial infarction \[[1](#ch11.xhtml#r001), [2](#ch11.xhtml#r002)\]. As such, accurate analysis and interpretation of ECG signals are paramount in clinical practice.

Traditionally, ECG signals are analyzed visually by trained medical professionals to identify abnormalities. However, this process can be time-consuming and subjective, leading to variability in diagnosis \[[3](#ch11.xhtml#r003)\]. To overcome these limitations, there has been a growing interest in the development of automated methods for ECG analysis, leveraging advancements in signal processing and machine learning techniques. One area of focus in ECG research is the development of accurate models that can replicate the complex morphology of ECG signals \[[4](#ch11.xhtml#r004)\]. These models are essential for various applications, including algorithm development, medical device testing, and education.

In recent years, significant progress has been made in developing methods for generating ECG signals. Many of these methods are based on trigonometric, Fourier, and Gaussian techniques. While these methods offer simplicity and computational efficiency, they often fall short in accurately capturing the complexity and variability of real ECG data and representing certain physiological features \[[5](#ch11.xhtml#r005)\], which limits their applicability in contexts requiring high fidelity and realism \[[6](#ch11.xhtml#r006), [7](#ch11.xhtml#r007)\]. Some approaches using neural networks for ECG modeling have also been proposed. These models excel at learning complex patterns and capturing intricate relationships in ECG data. However, they come with drawbacks such as high complexity and computational requirements, lack of interpretability, the need for large datasets, and susceptibility to overfitting \[[8](#ch11.xhtml#r008)–[11](#ch11.xhtml#r011)\].

Studies have also explored the parametric representation of ECG signals. Dolinsky et al. introduced a model based on elementary trigonometric and linear functions, which was further implemented by Gerasimov and Pedonova \[[12](#ch11.xhtml#r012), [13](#ch11.xhtml#r013)\]. In contrast, Mishra et al. focused on the critical need for precise ECG signal analysis and classification, emphasizing the role of ECG in diagnosing cardiac electrical abnormalities. They highlighted the challenges posed by noise and artifacts in actual ECG recordings, which complicate performance assessment \[[14](#ch11.xhtml#r014)\]. Parametric spline-based methods have emerged as promising approaches for ECG modeling, offering the flexibility to capture the intricate details of ECG waveforms with a minimal number of control points. In addition to ECG modeling, there is a need for robust classification algorithms capable of accurately identifying different cardiac rhythms and abnormalities from ECG signals \[[15](#ch11.xhtml#r015)–[17](#ch11.xhtml#r017)\].

ECG classification using machine learning involves the automated categorization of ECG signals into various classes based on their waveform characteristics. This process begins with the acquisition of high-quality ECG data, followed by preprocessing to remove noise and artifacts. Relevant features are then extracted from the signals, capturing important waveform characteristics such as temporal intervals and morphological features \[[18](#ch11.xhtml#r018)–[21](#ch11.xhtml#r021)\]. Machine learning models, including support vector machines, neural networks, and decision trees, are selected and trained using labelled ECG data to classify signals into different categories, such as normal and abnormal rhythms. Model performance is evaluated using metrics like accuracy and sensitivity, and optimization techniques are applied to improve classification accuracy. Once trained and validated, the optimized model can be deployed in clinical settings to assist healthcare professionals in diagnosing cardiac conditions and providing timely interventions \[[22](#ch11.xhtml#r022), [23](#ch11.xhtml#r023)\].

## **1.2** Methodology

A parametric quartic spline is a mathematical curve-fitting technique used to interpolate data points in a smooth and continuous manner. In the context of ECG modeling, it involves creating a mathematical representation of the ECG waveform based on a set of control points \[[24](#ch11.xhtml#r024)\]. Here’s how it works:

1.  **Control Points Selection**: Control points are strategically placed along the ECG waveform to capture important morphological features such as peaks, valleys, and inflection points. The number and placement of control points are crucial as they determine the accuracy of the spline curve in representing the original ECG signal.
2.  **Quartic Polynomial Interpolation**: A quartic polynomial is used to interpolate between adjacent control points. This generalized polynomial function is of the form:

*f*(*x*) = *a**x*4 + *b**x*3 + *c**x*2 + *d**x* + *e*(1.1) 

1.  where in (1.1) 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐, 𝑑 and *e* are coefficients determined by the interpolation process.
2.  **Curve Smoothing**: The spline curve is smoothed to ensure continuity and minimize oscillations. This is achieved by adjusting the tension at each control point, effectively controlling the curvature of the curve.
3.  **Parameterization**: Parameters such as knot vectors and tension values are adjusted to fine-tune the spline curve and improve its fidelity to the original ECG waveform. This allows for greater control over the interpolation process and ensures that the generated curve closely matches the underlying cardiac activity.
4.  **Synthetic ECG Signal Generation**: Once the spline curve is optimized, it is used to generate synthetic ECG signals by interpolating between the control points. These synthetic signals can mimic both normal and abnormal ECG patterns, providing a valuable tool for research, algorithm development, and educational purposes \[[24](#ch11.xhtml#r024)–[26](#ch11.xhtml#r026)\].

Overall, parametric quartic splines offer a flexible and efficient method for ECG modeling, allowing for the creation of realistic synthetic signals that closely resemble real-world data.

### **1.2.1** Breakdown of the essential requirements for ECG modeling

Synthetic ECG generation serves several valuable purposes in the field of biomedical engineering and cardiology:

*   **Data Augmentation:** Synthetic ECG generation allows researchers to augment existing datasets by generating additional samples with varying characteristics. This augmented dataset can enhance the robustness and generalizability of machine learning models trained on limited data.
*   **Anomaly Detection:** By generating synthetic ECG signals with known anomalies or abnormalities, researchers can train machine learning models to detect and classify these conditions accurately. This enables the development of automated diagnostic tools for early detection of cardiac abnormalities.
*   **Model Validation:** Synthetic ECG signals provide a controlled environment for testing and validating ECG analysis algorithms and models. Researchers can simulate various physiological scenarios and evaluate the performance of their algorithms under different conditions.
*   **Privacy Preservation:** Generating synthetic ECG data can help address privacy concerns associated with sharing real patient data. Synthetic datasets can be used for algorithm development and testing without compromising patient privacy or confidentiality.
*   **Education and Training:** Synthetic ECG signals are valuable for educational purposes, allowing students and healthcare professionals to practice ECG interpretation skills in a risk-free environment. Synthetic datasets can simulate a wide range of cardiac conditions, providing learners with diverse learning experiences.
*   **Research Exploration:** Synthetic ECG generation opens up opportunities for exploring novel research questions and hypotheses in cardiology and biomedical engineering. Researchers can investigate the impact of specific ECG features on diagnostic accuracy or study the effectiveness of different signal processing techniques \[[14](#ch11.xhtml#r014)–[16](#ch11.xhtml#r016)\].

Overall, synthetic ECG generation plays a crucial role in advancing research and innovation in cardiology, facilitating the development of more accurate diagnostic tools, predictive models, and personalized healthcare solutions.

## **1.3** Result

To apply the spline method for modeling ECG signals, we utilized real ECG data obtained from the PhysioNet database. PhysioNet, managed by the MIT Laboratory for Computational Physiology, offers a repository of freely available medical research data \[[27](#ch11.xhtml#r027)\]. Thirteen critical points crucial for ECG signal analysis were selected for modeling based on clinical significance \[[14](#ch11.xhtml#r014), [15](#ch11.xhtml#r015)\]. These points were identified according to the unique characteristics of ECG signals, and their locations by star marks are depicted in [Figure 1.1](#ch11.xhtml#f1). [Figure 1.1](#ch11.xhtml#f1) visually illustrates the comparison between the original ECG signal and the modeled ECG signal. This comparison vividly demonstrates the close alignment between the modeled signal and the original signal. In the figure, the real ECG signal is denoted by star markers, while the modeled ECG is depicted by a solid line. This graphical representation underscores the fidelity of the modeled signal in faithfully following the original ECG waveform.

![Figure 1.1 Location of critical points and original ECG signal along with the modelled ECG signal.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C001_001.jpg)

**Figure 1.1** Location of critical points and original ECG signal along with the modelled ECG signal.[⏎](#ch11.xhtml#f1b)

The visual comparison of original signal and modelled signal is also shown in [Figure 1.1](#ch11.xhtml#f1). It indicates that how modelled signal closely follow the original signal. The real ECG is represented by star marker and modelled ECG represented by a solid line. This process was iterated for both normal and abnormal ECG signals, resulting in the creation of a comprehensive ECG signal database tailored for machine learning classification purposes. The database comprised 168 instances. Subsequently, three distinct machine learning techniques were employed: K-nearest neighbors (KNN), Naïve Bayes, and AdaBoost. Each technique was applied to the prepared dataset to assess its classification performance. The classification accuracies obtained were as follows: 94% for KNN, 99% for Naïve Bayes, and 98% for AdaBoost.

The Orange software platform was utilized to implement these machine learning techniques. Renowned for its user-friendly interface designed for data analysis and machine learning tasks, Orange facilitated seamless execution of the methods. Notably, among the employed techniques, Naïve Bayes demonstrated superior performance. This was evidenced by its higher classification accuracy compared to KNN and AdaBoost. To provide further insight into the performance of each method, confusion matrices were generated. These matrices, presented in [Tables 1.1](#ch11.xhtml#t1), [1.2](#ch11.xhtml#t2), [1.3](#ch11.xhtml#t3), and [1.4](#ch11.xhtml#t4), offer a detailed breakdown of the classification outcomes, shedding light on the strengths and weaknesses of each approach.

## **1.4** Conclusion

The pivotal role of synthetic ECG generation in propelling advancements in cardiology research and innovation. By harnessing this technique, researchers can propel the development of more precise diagnostic tools, predictive models, and personalized healthcare solutions tailored to individual patient needs. The focus of the presented work has been on ECG modeling utilizing a parametric quartic spline method, complemented by machine learning classification applied to the resultant database. Through this approach, the study aimed to enhance our understanding of ECG signals and improve automated classification capabilities.

Machine learning techniques, including KNN, Naïve Bayes, and AdaBoost, were employed to analyze the derived ECG database. These methods demonstrated considerable potential in automating classification tasks, thereby streamlining diagnostic processes. Notably, among the three techniques, Naïve Bayes emerged as particularly promising, exhibiting superior accuracy compared to its counterparts. Overall, the findings underscore the effectiveness of combining ECG modeling with machine learning classification techniques. This integrated approach holds significant promise for revolutionizing cardiac healthcare by enabling more accurate diagnoses, predictive insights, and tailored treatment strategies. As such, continued exploration and refinement of these methodologies are imperative for driving future advancements in the field of cardiology.

# Chapter 2Quantum networking paradigm

Preety Aneja

DOI: [10.1201/9781003569619-2](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003569619-2)

## **2.1** Introduction

Our universe shows a fascinating behaviour at the microscopic level, and there are many occurrences in nature which are hard to be explained. Have we tried to think why a quantum computer can perform better as compared to a typical computer? And what are the unique features it possesses? All these questions have their answers hidden in understanding the fundamentals of nature. The branch of physics, known as quantum mechanics ([Konishi and Paffuti 2009](#ch12.xhtml#r054)), explains the behaviour and dynamics of particles at the microscopic level.

As the research and advancements in quantum mechanics grow rapidly, the existence of quantum computers, quantum networks and now, the quantum internet has become possible. There are significant advantages of quantum computing over traditional or classical computing. Quantum computing harness the fundamental concepts of quantum mechanics like entanglement, superposition, etc (Wineland 2013), which offer significant advantages over traditional computing methods to solve the complex problems like logistics, drug designing, banking, statistical science, secure communication, etc. The realization of quantum computers has become possible only with the remarkable developments in designing quantum software, quantum hardware research, quantum algorithm creation, etc.

The fundamental unit of information in classical computers is a binary digit. Each binary digit can exist in two states, that is either 0 or 1. On the other hand, the basic unit of quantum information in case of quantum computers is a qubit. The qubits can be prepared in various physical systems such as electronic energy states of atoms, optical degrees of freedom of photons, spin states of electrons or nuclei, etc. ([Bennett and DiVincenzo 2000](#ch12.xhtml#r030); [Bouwmeester and Zeilinger 2000](#ch12.xhtml#r032)). The properties of qubits are generally based on physical properties of the particle, such as the spin of an electron, and hence it can differ in various ways. It is known that an electron can have either up spin or down spin, but in quantum mechanics, there is a probability for an electron to exist in a state which has a spin in both directions simultaneously. This concept is known as the superposition of two states in quantum mechanics. This concept sounds strange to our general observation in accordance with classical physics. However, this type of peculiar behaviour shown by particles would be helpful in various ways. For instance, qubits can store much more information than conventional bits. A classical bit can take values as 0 and 1 only. With two bits, there exist four possible combinations: 00, 01, 10 or 11. While in quantum computing, a qubit can take value 0 and 1 simultaneously due to superposition property. Thus, with two qubits, all four combinations (00, 01, 10, 11) can exist at once. This property in quantum computing has increased the amount of information stored in qubits tremendously.

The operations performed by quantum computers are even faster than the fastest supercomputers. Quantum computing provides enhanced security in communication systems also. But then the question arises, what makes the transmission of quantum information possible? Quantum information, in the form of qubits, travels from one quantum processor to other processor over the long distances. This is possible through quantum networks which form an essential part of quantum communication. Quantum computers can provide enhanced security and could be potential threats to current encryption methods ([Klima et al. 2018](#ch12.xhtml#r053)). As the information travels through qubits, any attempt of tampering with the information can affect the quantum states of the particles involved. Thus, quantum networks offer unprecedented capabilities in secure communication.

Further, quantum memories play a crucial role in quantum networks and hence quantum communication which preserves the unique properties of qubits. These technologies are advancing and evolving rapidly in revolutionizing the realm of computation, data processing and transmission and cybersecurity. The goal of achieving quantum supremacy is becoming reality in today’s era since various companies and research institutions have already designed and developed quantum computers with over 50 qubits. The computation time taken by these quantum computers to solve complex problems has been reduced in seconds which otherwise would be in years if solved by traditional computers. The future of quantum computing and hence quantum networking is very bright.

This chapter provides a fresh perspective on the quantum networks by examining the various quantum elements like quantum computers, qubits, quantum circuits algorithms, etc. as an interconnected system rather than isolated components so as to make a cohesive quantum network. This chapter highlights the integration of various quantum technologies to transform existing communication frameworks and provides a roadmap to achieve quantum internet with enhanced security over classical internet. [Section 2.2](#ch12.xhtml#h1-006) covers the detailed discussion on fundamentals of quantum networks, focussing on quantum computing and the unique properties of superposition, entanglement, interference etc. [Section 2.3](#ch12.xhtml#h1-007) discusses about the quantum network paradigms like QKD, quantum teleportation, etc. and their pivotal role in future communication systems. [Section 2.4](#ch12.xhtml#h1-008) covers the applications and current state of art studies of quantum networking. [Sections 2.5](#ch12.xhtml#h1-009) and [2.6](#ch12.xhtml#h1-010) will discuss the opportunities and challenges of integrating quantum computing in quantum-based network paradigms. The last section concludes the chapter and explores the future directions.

## **2.2** Fundamentals of quantum networks

Quantum networks are systems designed to transmit and process information based on the principles of quantum mechanics rather than classical physics. This section explores the essential components and principles behind quantum networks, setting the groundwork for understanding how these systems enable secure communication, distributed computing, and advanced quantum sensing as follows:

### **2.2.1** Quantum computing and qubits

The principles of quantum mechanics form the basis of quantum computing to perform computation in a way that is different from classical computing at the fundamental level. The operation of quantum computers is dependent on the quantum effects like superposition and entanglement in a similar way as the operation of classical computers relies on the behaviour of electrons in semiconductor materials ([Langlois 2002](#ch12.xhtml#r056)). In traditional digital computing, the fundamental unit of information is a binary digit which can take two values, 0 or 1. In quantum computing, the fundamental unit is called qubit ([Figure 2.1](#ch12.xhtml#f2)) which may take value ‘0’ or ‘1’ or can exist in the superposition of these two states simultaneously ([Nielsen and Chuang 2010](#ch12.xhtml#r062); [Nadj-Perge et al. 2010](#ch12.xhtml#r060); [Hendrickx et al. 2020](#ch12.xhtml#r047)). Quantum computing has drastically revolutionized the field of computation technology by exploiting the unique principles of quantum mechanics so as to surpass the specific tasks performed by classical computers. Quantum computing has many applications in various domains such as cryptography, materials science, healthcare, logistics, climate modelling, etc.

![Figure 2.1 From qubits to quantum computer.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C002_001.jpg)

**Figure 2.1** From qubits to quantum computer.[⏎](#ch12.xhtml#f2b)

### **2.2.2** Superposition

In quantum mechanics, the superposition is a unique property which is exhibited by the qubits. This means a qubit can exist simultaneously in the superposition of states ‘0’ or ‘1’ unlike the classical bits which can only take the values 0 and 1 at once ([Figure 2.2](#ch12.xhtml#f3)). Mathematically, this property can be represented as

![Figure 2.2 Illustration of a bit and qubit.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C002_002.jpg)

**Figure 2.2** Illustration of a bit and qubit.[⏎](#ch12.xhtml#f3b)

|*ψ*⟩ = *a*|0⟩+ *b*|1⟩, 

where |*ψ*⟩ is a wave function, *a* and *b* are complex numbers representing the probability amplitudes of the respective states ([Nielsen and Chuang 2010](#ch12.xhtml#r062)). The magnitude squared of these probability amplitudes will give us the probability of the qubit being either in a particular state and must satisfy the no

|*a*|² + |*b*|² = 1. 

Thus, quantum computers can store and process a vast amount of information due to this unique property of superposition as a huge computational space becomes available. For a system with *N* number of qubits, the computational domain increases exponentially as 2N. The exponential growth shows that even with a small number of qubits, a large amount of information can be stored and processed by the quantum computers. This feature of quantum computers has enabled them to solve many complex problems like factoring large numbers, simulation of quantum systems, etc., in contrast to classical computing methods.

### **2.2.3** Entanglement

Entanglement is another unique property exploited in quantum computing ([Bennett and DiVincenzo 2000](#ch12.xhtml#r030)). Qubits can exist in an entangled state unlike the classical bits, which are totally independent of each other. This entangled state of qubits creates a direct link between two qubits globally even if the two qubits are physically apart. This means if one of the two qubits is probed, the other will get affected due to entanglement. The measurement on one entangled qubit will instantaneously influence the state of other qubit even if these qubits are far apart from each other. Thus, the qubits in the entangled state will have a correlation which classical bits do not exhibit. The entangled system of the qubits must be described as a whole rather than independently ([Figure 2.2](#ch12.xhtml#f3)). Mathematically, the entangled state of the two qubits is represented as

|*ψ*⟩ = *a*|00⟩ + *b*|01⟩ + *c*|10⟩ + *d*|11⟩, 

where *a, b, c* and *d* are complex probability amplitudes. This interconnection of multiple qubits over long distances become a valuable asset in quantum computing which has applications in dense coding, quantum teleportation, quantum key distribution (QKD) and quantum simulations ([Gill 2021](#ch12.xhtml#r042)). The correlation among entangled qubits cannot be explained by classical physics and, thus, can lead to more complex and faster computations.

### **2.2.4** Quantum interference

Quantum states can possibly exhibit the interference phenomena in a similar way as the waves interfere, which leads to maxima and minima ([Kipnis 2012](#ch12.xhtml#r052)). Quantum interference can lead to the amplification of certain outcomes while cancelling the other analogous to constructive and destructive interference in wave theory. The principle of quantum interference is useful in quantum algorithms to enhance the probability of correct solutions as compared to the probability of incorrect solutions, which get diminished. This property can enhance the computational power and efficiency over classical technology.

### **2.2.5** Measurement

The last stage in quantum computation is measurement, thus ultimately collapsing the qubit from a superposition state into one of the definite states, 0 or 1. The process of measurement ultimately leads to a classical output from a quantum system by destroying the superposition state of qubits to a definite state. Correct outcome is typically guaranteed by the quantum algorithms but stochastic nature of the process can even lead to the non-sampling of the correct outcome. Thus, some classical technique is required after data processing to obtain the final correct output from raw results with quantum computer.

### **2.2.6** Quantum gates and circuits

The quantum versions of classical gates used to carry out the quantum computation are called quantum gates. Quantum gates operate on superpositions of states unlike classical gates and thus enabling quantum parallelism. Quantum gates operate on qubits through unitary operations to manipulate the state of qubits while preserving quantum properties like superposition, entanglement etc. Quantum gates are reversible and are able to perform reverse calculations and complex computations. These gates can be single-qubit as well as multi-qubits. Examples include single-qubit gates such as Pauli-X (analogous to the classical NOT gate), Pauli-Y, Pauli-Z, Hadamard (H) and Phase (S) gates; two-qubit gates, such as the Controlled-NOT (CNOT) gate, which flips the state of a qubit, thus enabling operations that are impossible with classical gates alone. A sequence of quantum gates can perform complex algorithms and thus forms a quantum circuit.

### **2.2.7** Quantum algorithms

Quantum circuits form the foundation for implementing quantum algorithms, which are then applied to one or more qubits to solve specific problems. Some of the examples of quantum algorithms are Shor’s algorithm for factorization of integers, Grover’s algorithm for searching databases, etc. Both these algorithms provide faster speedups in comparison to classical algorithms ([Shor 1999](#ch12.xhtml#r070); [Grover 1996](#ch12.xhtml#r045)).

## **2.3** Quantum-based networks paradigms: the next frontier in quantum communication

Although universal quantum computers or simulators are still in development, efforts are being made by researchers in linking these quantum computers to build quantum networks. Quantum based network paradigms are an emerging field at the intersection of quantum physics and information technology to bring a transformative impact in computation, communication and data transmission with unparalleled security and computational power. Each paradigm has a unique role, mechanism, applications, challenges and advantages. The successful implementation of these paradigms will play a crucial role in the future of communication networks and information processing at the global level. Quantum networks can be better understood by discussing its elements as follows:

*   Quantum networks are facilitated with quantum processors with minimum one qubit. A small quantum computer is known as a quantum processor which can perform quantum logic gates on qubits. The application is run on these quantum processors with one qubit known as end nodes. It might be possible to have quantum processors with more than one qubit as per the requirement of the application.
*   Communication lines are required for the transfer of qubits and telecom wires can be utilized for this purpose.
*   In quantum networks, qubits are delivered to the required processors with the help of optical switches in order to optimizing the communication process.
*   Fourth, long distance transmission of qubits is possible through quantum repeaters so as to avoid losses (Gu et al. 2024).

Key aspects and technologies in this domain include:

### **2.3.1** Quantum key distribution

QKD network is a typical example of a quantum network which does not require entangling the quantum nodes. Unlike classical cryptographic methods, QKD is based on information theoretical security utilizing fundamental laws of quantum mechanics and thus making it secure against any future advancement in computational power ([Xu et al. 2020](#ch12.xhtml#r076); [Zhang et al. 2018](#ch12.xhtml#r078)). In a QKD network, quantum channels (optical fibres or free space links) are used for the distribution of quantum keys between two parties. These keys are used to encrypt and decrypt messages transmitted through the communication channel. Any attempt at eavesdropping is detectable due to the disturbance of quantum states in the act of quantum measurement. [Bennett and Brassard (1984](#ch12.xhtml#r031)) proposed BB84 protocol which uses four polarization states of photons for encoding bits. Later on, more and more protocols were proposed and developed like E91, etc ([Zhang et al. 2024](#ch12.xhtml#r079)).

### **2.3.2** Quantum teleportation

It is a process in which the quantum state of a particle is allowed to be transmitted directly from one location to another, without physically transporting the particle itself with the help of classical communication ([Olmschenk et al. 2009](#ch12.xhtml#r064)). This quantum network uses entangled quantum nodes in which the qubits can be processed and transmitted directly. This paradigm creates a robust and versatile network offering incomparable security and computational power ([Devulapalli et al. 2024](#ch12.xhtml#r037)).

### **2.3.3** Cloud-based quantum computing

Cloud-based quantum computing provides access to quantum emulators and processors over the internet ([Soeparno and Perbangsa 2021](#ch12.xhtml#r071)). As a result, users can perform quantum computations instead of using local quantum hardware. This paradigm of quantum networking has broadened the access of quantum computing resources and thus speed up more research and advancements in this field ([Golec et al. 2024](#ch12.xhtml#r044)).

### **2.3.4** Entanglement-enabled communication

Entanglement-enabled communication makes use of qubits in entangled state to establish communication links with enhanced security. Any attempt at interference or eavesdropping is detectable as ensured by this network paradigm which in turn provides a robust foundation for secure data transmission ([Nötzel 2020](#ch12.xhtml#r063)).

### **2.3.5** Quantum repeaters

Quantum repeaters can extend the range of quantum communication by correcting for losses and errors due to decoherence or noise without directly measuring the quantum information ([Azuma et al. 2023](#ch12.xhtml#r029)). These are used for enabling the reliable transmission of quantum information over longer distances without significant loss or degradation of signal. In local area networks or even in some metro area networks with short distances, quantum repeaters are not required as the qubit fidelity can be maintained easily. However, quantum repeaters become essential to implement as the quantum networks scale up to cover some larger geographic area over a country, region such as wide area networks (WANs), some larger metro networks etc. The working of quantum repeaters involves the breaking of the transmission path into shorter segments so that qubit fidelity can be maintained over these segments. Pairs of entangled qubits are created across each segment. Entanglement between adjacent segments is created and then entanglement is swapped over the longer distances to maintain the purity and integrity of quantum states. Quantum memory is a core component of quantum repeaters as it can store and release the qubit at a later time. This will enable the synchronization of entanglement over different segments (Azuma et al. 2023).

### **2.3.6** Quantum internet

The internet is playing an integral role in connecting people across globe and hence, become an important part in our daily lives. However, the current internet infrastructure is more prone to cyber-attacks posing a threat to sensitive information ([Aslan et al. 2023](#ch12.xhtml#r028); [Carlo and Obergfaell 2024](#ch12.xhtml#r034)). Quantum internet can solve this problem. The quantum internet is a theoretical concept of interconnected quantum computers and other quantum devices such that information can be sent and received using quantum signals ([Wehner et al. 2018](#ch12.xhtml#r074)). Many complex problems could be solved with the help of quantum internet in co-existence with the classical internet. The motivation behind quantum internet comes from the transformative impact of classical internet on our society. The development of quantum internet can have profound implications for security, scientific discovery and computing. The quantum internet can span various domains, including metrology, distributed quantum computing and quantum-enhanced secure communication potentially revolutionizing areas such as financial risk analysis, data encryption and material science ([Cacciapuoti et al. 2019](#ch12.xhtml#r033)).

## **2.4** Practical applications and state-of-art studies in quantum networking

Till date, no one has managed to develop a large-scale and sustained quantum network, but there are many advancements in this direction as discussed in [Table 2.1](#ch12.xhtml#t5).

## **2.5** Opportunities of quantum computing in quantum-based network paradigms

Integrating quantum computing into quantum-based network paradigms is a groundbreaking development that has the potential to revolutionize information processing. Quantum computing can significantly enhance the capabilities of quantum networks, leading to more secure and efficient communication. Let’s discuss some of the opportunities:

1.  *Enhanced computational power*: Quantum computing can perform complex calculations at faster speeds than classical computers ([Farhi et al. 2014](#ch12.xhtml#r041)). This characteristic feature of quantum computers can bring transformative impact on industries such as cryptography, optimization and drug discovery.
2.  *Enhanced Security*: Quantum networks are based on the fundamental principles of quantum mechanics ensuring that any attempt to intercept information can be readily detected. Quantum networks like QKD had greatly enhanced the security in communication networks ([von Burg et al. 2021](#ch12.xhtml#r073)). Quantum computing has enabled more sophisticated encryption methods which are virtually unbreakable by classical means to improve the security features.
3.   *Efficient information processing*: Quantum algorithms can optimize network protocols and have the tendency to reduce delay in the data transmission ([Gisin et al. 2002](#ch12.xhtml#r043); [Djordjevic 2012](#ch12.xhtml#r039)). This will overall increase the capacity of quantum networks to transmit data with minimum delays. Quantum computing and hence quantum networks can lead to more efficient processing and transmission of data.
4.  *Quantum Sensors*: Quantum networks aim to interconnect the quantum devices over a long distance and hence find applications from medical imaging to environmental monitoring with enhanced sensing capabilities ([Landman et al. 2022](#ch12.xhtml#r055)).
5.  *New paradigms in network design*: The advancements in quantum physics had led to the development of novel network architectures based on it. For instance, communication systems are greatly affected by the development of quantum repeaters and quantum routers which can extend the communication range and hence, reliability of quantum communication.
6.  *Scientific Research*: Quantum networks can expedite the collaborative research by enabling shared access to quantum resources and linking quantum laboratories. Overall, quantum-based network paradigms hold the potential to revolutionize communication and computation that are unattainable with classical technologies.

## **2.6** Challenges

The role of quantum computing in quantum-based networks is exciting. There exist challenges also as discussed below:

1.  *Quantum decoherence*: The decay of quantum information as a result of interaction with the environment is called quantum decoherence ([Schlosshauer 2019](#ch12.xhtml#r068)). The quantum states are very much prone to decoherence on interaction with environment. This can lead to the loss of information so the main challenge is to protect the purity of quantum states by developing advanced isolation techniques and materials.
2.  *Quantum and Classical integration*: The integration of quantum networks with the existing classical networks so as to create hybrid systems brings new challenges ([Shi and Malaney 2023](#ch12.xhtml#r069); [Reilly 2019](#ch12.xhtml#r067)). The integration includes designing interfaces and protocols for the seamless interaction between quantum and classical systems ([DiAdamo et al. 2022](#ch12.xhtml#r038)).
3.  *Atmospheric Interference*: Free-space quantum communication is greatly affected by weather conditions and atmospheric turbulence.
4.   *Security*: Quantum networks need to be protected against the attacks by continuously monitoring the security protocols, developing safe cryptographic methods and doing rigorous testing.
5.  *Development of efficient quantum repeaters*: The quantum repeaters are required to extend the range of quantum communication but robust and efficient repeaters are difficult to build that can perform entanglement swapping and preserve the purity of quantum states without loss of information. Quantum repeaters rely on quantum memories to store and retransmit entangled states. However, current quantum memories struggle with limited coherence times (the duration they can reliably hold quantum states) and low storage efficiency, which restricts network scalability ([Dusanowski and Thompson 2023](#ch12.xhtml#r040)). Further, in large quantum networks, scaling up of numerous repeaters and synchronization between these repeaters is a complex challenge.
6.  *Cost and scalability for large-scale integration*: Small quantum networks, while scaling up to larger practical quantum networks require advancements in technology. This includes developing scalable architectures, standardized protocols and modular quantum network components which requires investment and infrastructure also. Quantum hardware is still in development, and real-world problems can be solved only with much more powerful hardware ([Monroe and Kim 2013](#ch12.xhtml#r058)).
7.  *Interdisciplinary collaboration*: Research and development in quantum networks requires the expertise in various fields such as quantum physics, computer science, engineering and thus fostering interdisciplinary collaboration.
8.  *Standards and protocols*: The development of quantum networks lacks the standardized protocols and regulations for quantum communication.

## **2.7** Conclusion and future directions

Quantum computing and hence quantum networks have many real-world applications. With the advent of quantum computing which leverages the principles of quantum mechanics, there occurs a paradigm shift in classical computing since quantum computing enable computations that are infeasible with classical methods. The foundation of quantum computing is based on the qubits and quantum gates, which in turn, offers the unprecedented speed and efficiency in solving complex problems. Thus, quantum-based network paradigms represent a transformative shift in computation, communication technology and information processing to achieve enhanced security, speed and computational power. The potential of quantum computing is exciting as well as challenging also. There exist many challenges with quantum networks such as preservation of entanglement over the long distance, technologies for integrating quantum nodes with existing classical infrastructure, and errors due to decoherence and noise. But the ongoing research and development efforts in this field promise to unlock the full potential of quantum networks to make quantum communication as the cornerstone of global connectivity. Key directions include hybrid quantum-classical protocols to integrate quantum systems with classical infrastructure, standardized communication protocols for interoperability, quantum machine learning ([Karthikeyan et al. 2024](#ch12.xhtml#r050)) and AI-powered network optimization ([Khang and Rath 2024](#ch12.xhtml#r051)) to adapt dynamically to network conditions. It is emphasized that the international collaboration and standards development to realize a global quantum internet infrastructure is urgently needed. A more secure, interconnected, and technologically advanced future can be embarked by embracing quantum-based network paradigms.

# Chapter 3Genetic algorithm-based framework for optimizing medical image enhancement

Kirti Aggarwal and Meenal Jain

DOI: [10.1201/9781003569619-3](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003569619-3)

## **3.1** Introduction

Enhancing the clinical capabilities of medical image enhancement is critical for image analysis. The goal of this work is to apply genetic algorithms (GAs) to produce enhanced images. The basic idea entails feeding the initial image through GAs to produce a clear and high-quality reproduced image, which is then enhanced further. This work aims to create a framework for creating the finest possible images by using a cutting-edge algorithm that considers all restrictions to produce better outcomes. For accurate and efficient picture enhancement, it employs the GA. We began with reading an image and preparing the fitness function. This randomly generated image is continuously evolved through many generations, using crossover and mutation, and GA until a superior solution is found. With a dual-peaked intensity distribution, this can enhance the visual impression of a region of interest (ROI) in picture data.

Before processing, image enhancement enhances the quality and information content of the original data. Traditional technique includes contrast enhancement, spatial filtering, and density slicing. In contrast enhancement or stretching, a linear transformation is applied to expand the range of grey levels. Spatial filtering improves linear features that occur naturally, such as fault. Density slicing segments the continuous range of grey tone into a series of density intervals, with each interval represented by a specific color or symbol.

The demand for medical image improvement has expanded in recent years in order to assist clinicians in making accurate diagnoses; hence, medical image processing provides relief to enhance medical images for proper illness diagnosis \[[1](#ch13.xhtml#r080)\]. One of the primary goals of medical image processing has always been to improve image quality. The clarity of images for human viewing is improved by image enhancement.

Enhancement techniques include removing blur and noise from an image, increasing contrast, and revealing details \[[2](#ch13.xhtml#r081)\]. While capturing an image, some information is lost, resulting in a blurry or less informative image that is not always clear. Medical images are poorly lighted, and many key parts of the image are not visible, resulting in visual blur and contrast that is difficult to notice.

Medical research data contains a plethora of information that, in some situations, cannot be simply examined, if at all, using traditional statistical approaches. Physicians who could benefit from using algorithms to solve difficult medical problems are unaware of their existence. As a result, we wanted to use the GA and its medical applications. The employment of the GA in several medical specialties, including radiology, has intriguing implications. This work presents the GA’s uses in illness screening, diagnosis, treatment planning, pharmacovigilance, prognosis, and healthcare management, as well as allowing physicians to imagine how they may apply this metaheuristic approach in their own practice. [Figure 3.1](#ch13.xhtml#f4) represents the flow cycle of GA. The algorithm is defined in detail in [Section 3.3](#ch13.xhtml#h1-014).

![Figure 3.1 Flow cycle of genetic algorithm.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C003_001.jpg)

**Figure 3.1** Flow cycle of genetic algorithm.[⏎](#ch13.xhtml#f4b)

During this research work, we will conduct extensive research on how GAs work and how they can be used in optimizing the process of image enhancement. We aspire to create a new path for the healthcare sector where the solutions can be achieved using GA. The image enhancement problem can be characterized by several factors, which make the parameter selection problem difficult.

This chapter presents a framework that applies GAs within the context of connective cognition networks to optimize image enhancement across various domains. By harnessing the power of computational intelligence, the framework connects interdisciplinary knowledge—from artificial intelligence to image processing—into an integrated system designed to enhance the quality of images. GAs, as a core component of computational intelligence, are utilized to address the complex optimization challenges involved in image enhancement, providing adaptable and scalable solutions. The chapter highlights how these algorithms mimic evolutionary processes to iteratively improve image quality, reflecting the adaptive nature of connective cognition networks that link diverse cognitive processes and expertise. This approach contributes to advancements in computational intelligence by showcasing how connective cognition can be leveraged to develop innovative solutions for image enhancement, ultimately improving visual outcomes across different applications.

## **3.2** Literature survey

The majority of extant image enhancement approaches are empirical or heuristic methods that are tied to specific types of images and try to enhance the contrast level of images that have deteriorated throughout the acquisition process. An optimization problem is determining the ideal level of greyscale that enhances each different input image. Some approaches employ Swarm Intelligence techniques. Particle swarm optimization (PSO) is used in multiobjective histogram equalization to improve contrast while preserving brightness. Some works use the utilize similar candidate solution encodings and histogram mapping strategies, all within an optimization framework focused on the Artificial Bee Colony (ABC) algorithm.

In the realm of image processing, nature-inspired optimization strategies are critical. It improves image enhancement, restoration, image segmentation \[[3](#ch13.xhtml#r082)\], image edge detections, image generation, image fusion, picture pattern recognition, image thresholding, and other functions. So far, numerous optimization strategies for various image processing applications have been proposed. Evolutionary algorithms \[[4](#ch13.xhtml#r083)\] draw inspiration from biological processes like reproduction, mutation, recombination, and selection. The optimization technique is essential for identifying the most accurate or optimal solutions from a set of possibilities. In scenarios involving multiple individuals, each person may have their own best solution, with the global best representing the top solution among all local bests. Evolutionary algorithms, leveraging fitness functions, have won many challenging problem-solving tasks, and the field that employs these algorithms as problem-solving tools is known as evolutionary computation. The fitness function is a key component of evolutionary computation and enhancing the objective function will result in ideal solutions.

Fireflies are insects that have the inherent ability to illuminate in the dark through flashing and flickering. This biological characteristic inspired the development of the firefly optimization algorithm \[[5](#ch13.xhtml#r084)\]. Firefly optimization algorithm was used to enhance fuzzy clustering for CT/MR Images \[[6](#ch13.xhtml#r085)\]. The process of segmentation involves isolating the desired area of interest. Anatomical organs and irregularities such as tumors, cysts, and other anomalies are important in medical imaging for clinicians to diagnose disorders for telemedicine applications. Classic segmentation methods include thresholding, region growth, and edge detection. Clustering, an unsupervised learning approach, groups similar data points together, while fuzzy partitioning integrates comparable pixels based on fuzzy membership value. However, the conventional Fuzzy C-Means (FCM) algorithm is insensitive when it comes to cluster centroid initialization, leading to frequent issues with local minima. Therefore, the optimization process becomes more important during the initialization of cluster centroids, boosting the FCM algorithm’s performance. For CT/MR medical picture segmentation, firefly optimization is used with the FCM method in this study.

The nonlinear tensor diffusion filter is used to remove artefacts and denoise the image during the preprocessing stage, effectively reducing computation time by processing fewer pixels overall. When combined with FCM, the firefly optimization produces satisfactory outperforming FCM when paired with other methods such as Cuckoo Search \[[7](#ch13.xhtml#r086)\], ABC \[[8](#ch13.xhtml#r087)\], and simulated annealing methods \[[9](#ch13.xhtml#r088)\]. Cluster validity performance criteria are utilized to determine the optimal number of clusters. In the storage and transmission of medical data, image compression is crucial. For medical photos, lossless compression methods are often used, although lossy vector quantization (VQ) algorithms are employed in scenarios where the reconstructed image quality remains high with an optimal compression ratio. The bat optimization algorithm \[[10](#ch13.xhtml#r089)\] is inspired by bats’ habit of using echolocation to locate prey and navigate through obstacles. In the VQ algorithm, the bat optimization process is used to find the best codebook design. For abdominal CT images, the BAT-VQ compression technique was compared against classical VQ, contextual vector quantization (CVQ), and JPEG lossless systems.

Beni and Wang \[[11](#ch13.xhtml#r090)\] invented the swarm intelligence algorithm, It is made up of agents or individuals who have a local interest in one another and the environment. They obey individual rules; there is no centralized control system, thus they act independently. Local agent interaction will result in global behavior, which implies global intelligence. Mirjalili et al. \[[12](#ch13.xhtml#r091)\] introduced this algorithm, based on the leadership and hunting styles of grey wolves. Grey wolves are called apex predators because they are at the top of the food chain. The group members adhere to a highly tight social dominance hierarchy. The first level is alpha, and they are the leaders who make judgments about hunting, sleeping, and other matters. The beta level is the second. These individuals serve as subordinates to the alpha decision-making level. When the alphas become too old or die, the beta candidates will take their position. The third level is known as delta, and it dominates omega. The lowest level of the hierarchy is Omega. Both alpha and beta wolves will use delta wolves as subordinates. The fittest solution in GWO will be alpha, followed by beta, and finally delta. Everything else will be considered Omega. As a result, alpha, beta, delta, and omega will be used to direct hunting.

The ABC optimization method is based on honey bee behavior and was proposed by Karaboga \[[13](#ch13.xhtml#r092)\]. Three groups of bees are present in this mode. Employed bees, onlooker bees, and scout bees are the three types. Employed bees go on a food hunt and report back to the hive. From the information provided by the employed bees, the observer bees will select the best food source. i.e., each employed bee will have information about a certain food source. Scout bees are employed bees whose food source has been rejected. Kennedy and Eberhart \[[14](#ch13.xhtml#r093)\] proposed PSO, developed by observing the behavior of fish and birds while they move in groups. The PSO will iterate using the specified parameters until it finds a better solution for the candidate. By taking into account inertia, personal influence, and social impact, a particle, also known as a candidate, can enhance its position. PSO is most commonly used in control systems for functional optimization and optimum control. PSO is used to solve nonlinear optimization issues since it is simple to implement and has fewer parameters. It is adaptable to various uses.

The key findings from our research are that the individuals cannot be created or deleted in PSO. Individuals just move around a terrain that tracks their fitness over time. This is similar to a flock of birds or other animals communicating. Second, fitness proportionate selection and genetic recombination occur in GAs. The PSO equivalents of this are best selection and particle movement. Both methods must be fine-tuned to function in certain problem domains. To achieve faster convergence PSO techniques require normalization of the input vectors. GAs can handle both continuous and discrete values. In terms of computational resources, the GA is quick, simple to deploy, and cost-effective. The ACO is greedier, but it produces superior results, especially when dealing with huge problems. Each method has its own set of mechanics and representational elements. It’s crossover and schemata in GAs. Edge-based structure and edge weights are used in ACO. ## **3.3** Genetic algorithm

GAs are fundamentally inspired by Charles Darwin’s natural selection process, where they accept input and generate output by selecting from various potential solutions. GAs were initially employed by John Holland \[[15](#ch13.xhtml#r094)\]. The GAs are intended to mimic natural system processes that are required for evolution. To find the best answers, GA searches efficiently in global spaces. GA is more successful at enhancing contrast and generates natural-looking photos. A GA is a search technique inspired by the principles of natural selection mechanism and genetic system. These algorithms are highly parallel and flexible, merging the concept of “survival of the fittest” in string structures with a systematic yet random exchange of information. This approach results in a search strategy that mimics some of the creativity found in human problem-solving. They effectively use previous data to predict and explore new search areas with projected improved outcomes, iteratively applying genetically inspired operators to probable solutions.

**Algorithm 1: Pseudo of the Genetic Algorithm**

*g* = 0 /\*Initialize Generation counter\*/  *P* = *I**n**i**t**i**a**l**i**z**e* *p**o**p**u**l**t**a**i**o**n*/\**R**a**n**d**o**m* *g**e**n**e**r**a**t**i**o**n* *o**f* *p* *i**n**d**i**v**i**d**u**a**l**s* \*/ *R**e**p**e**a**t* *U**N**T**I**L* *e**n**d* *c**o**n**d**i**t**i**o**n*   
*F* = *R**e**p**r**o**d**u**c**s**t**i**o**n*(*P*)/\**S**e**l**e**c**t**i**o**n* *o**f* *p* *p**a**r**e**n**t**s* *t**h**r**i**u**g**h* *a* *r**o**u**l**e**t**t**e*\*/  
 *S* = *C**r**o**s**s**o**v**e**r*(*F*)   
*M* = *M**u**t**a**t**i**o**n* (*S*)/\**A**l**t**e**r**a**t**i**o**n* *o**f* *t**h**e* *i**n**d**i**v**i**d**u**a**l* *b**i**t**s* *w**i**t**h* *p**r**o**b**a**b**i**l**i**t**y* *m**c*\*/  
   
*P* = *U**p**d**t**a**e* *p**o**p**u**l**a**t**i**o**n*(*M*)/\**M* *i**s* *c**o**p**i**e**d* *t**o* *P*(*P*\=*M*)\*/  
 *g* = *g* + 1/\**I**n**c**r**e**m**e**n**t* *o**f* *t**h**e* *G**e**n**e**r**a**t**i**o**n* *c**o**u**n**t**e**r* \*/± 

Unlike traditional search strategies, GAs deal with numerous points at once rather than a single point. GAs have been shown to give reliable search in complex spaces both theoretically and experimentally. Pattern recognition and image processing present numerous challenges that require executing efficient searches within complex spaces to identify the optimal solution. GAs use three fundamental procedures on a restricted number of strings to discover the global near-optimal answer. Reproduction/selection, cross-over, and mutation are the three operators.

### **3.3.1** Parent selection strategies

#### **3.3.1.1** Tournament selection

One of the selection strategies used in GAs is tournament Selection. In this, the top candidates are selected from the present generation. After this, the selected best candidates are advanced to the following generation. In a K-way tournament selection, top k-individuals are selected and they compete against each other \[[16](#ch13.xhtml#r095)\]. Among the selected candidates only the best candidate is selected and passed on to the next generation. Numerous tournaments are conducted, allowing us to finalize the pick of candidates, those will advance to the following generation.

This selection strategy has a feature called selection pressure, that provides a probabilistic evaluation of a candidate’s chance of being chosen for a tournament. If the tournament size is large, weaker candidates will have less likelihood of selection, as they must compete against stronger opponents. The pace of convergence of the GA is determined by the selection pressure parameter. The higher the selection pressure, the higher the rate of convergence. Over a wide range of selection pressures, GAs can uncover best or near-best solutions.

#### **3.3.1.2** Roulette wheel selection

In roulette wheel selection, the circular wheel is divided into segments representing individuals. Where each segment size is proportional to the fitness of the individual, as represented in [Figure 3.2](#ch13.xhtml#f5). The wheel is rotated and a specific point on the perimeter of the wheel is selected as indicated in the figure. The segments that comes in front of that specific point is chosen as a parent \[[17](#ch13.xhtml#r096)\]. Similar process is followed for choosing the next parent. When the wheel is revolved, an individual with better fitness will have more probability of stopping in front of the designated point, as it has the larger segment on the wheel. Hence, likelihood of selecting an individual is directly related to his fitness value.

![Figure 3.2 Roulette wheel selection diagram.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C003_002.jpg)

**Figure 3.2** Roulette wheel selection diagram.[⏎](#ch13.xhtml#f5b)

#### **3.3.1.3** Ranking selection

In rank selection, the population is first ranked, and then each chromosome’s fitness is assigned based on its position in that ranking. The worst chromosome receives a fitness value of 1, the second-worst gets a fitness of 2, and so forth, with the best chromosome receiving a fitness value equal to N, where N is the total number of chromosomes in the population \[[18](#ch13.xhtml#r097)\]. Following that, all chromosomes have a chance to be chosen. Premature convergence can be avoided using rank-based selection techniques. However, because it sorts the populations based on fitness value, it can be computationally expensive. However, because the best chromosomes do not differ as much from the others, this strategy may result in slower convergence.

### **3.3.2** Crossover

Crossover is a genetic characteristic that involves the joining of two chromosomes (also known as parents) to form a new chromosome (also called offspring). If the new chromosome inherits the best qualities from both parents, the result of crossover may be better than both parents \[[19](#ch13.xhtml#r098)\] as shown in [Figure 3.3](#ch13.xhtml#f6). During evolution, crossover happens according to a user-defined crossover probability. The new offspring will inherit certain characteristics from one parent and others from the other. Assume first parent is 10001011 and second parent is 10011111; after completing the crossover, the outcome can be 10011111, which has some part of the first parent and some part of the second parent.

![Figure 3.3 Crossover.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C003_003.jpg)

**Figure 3.3** Crossover.[⏎](#ch13.xhtml#f6b)

### **3.3.3** Mutation

After the crossover is completed, mutation may occur. Which is done to avoid all population solutions falling into a local best of the current problem. The encoding as well as the crossing influences the mutation. When encoding permutations, for example, the mutation could mean switching two genes \[[20](#ch13.xhtml#r099)\]. The new offspring are randomly altered by mutation as shown in [Figure 3.4](#ch13.xhtml#f7). We can encode binary data by altering a few randomly selected bits from 1 to 0 or 0 to 1. The mutations for the parent 10001011 could be 10001111 and for the parent 10011111 could be 10010111.

![Figure 3.4 Mutation.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C003_004.jpg)

**Figure 3.4** Mutation.[⏎](#ch13.xhtml#f7b)

### **3.3.4** Formulation of objective function and fitness function

The objective function is used to determine a solution’s worth. The output of this objective function is then turned into a fitness value that is utilized to determine the quality of a solution. Because GA is an optimization tool, the objective function is often a maximization or minimization function that includes all variables. The fitness function was designed to create a bimodal histogram in the grey level intensities, allowing for automated image processing steps to evaluate the quality of the individuals throughout the optimization. Individuals with a histogram with two well-separated normal distributions and an equal distance from the optimal threshold are favored by the fitness function we utilize.

GAs have numerous benefits over classical optimization algorithms. These algorithms are very versatile and are capable of handling a wide range of objective functions. The objective function may be stable or dynamic, linear or nonlinear, continuous or discontinuous, or even affected by random noise. The ability of GAs to tackle complex problems and their inherent their inherent parallelism are the two most notable advantages. Because multiple offspring in a population operate independently, the population (or any subset) can hunt in multiple directions at the same time. This capability of GAs makes the parallelization of algorithms very easy, and it also simplifies the modification of different parameters and even groups of encoded strings. However, GAs have some drawbacks as well. The selection criteria for the new population, the design of objective function, the choice of population size, and the selection of key factors like mutation and crossover rates must be handled with utmost care. Any misstep in the choices of these parameters can make it difficult for the algorithm to converge optimally and resulting in ineffective outcomes. Despite these limitations, GAs are nevertheless extensively used nonlinear optimization strategies today.

As a result, GAs is being used extensively in the field of engineering over the past decade. GAs are now being seen as the powerful tool for design optimization. This research work explores the different applications of evolutionary algorithms to solve complex optimization problems, challenges, and managing uncertainty in typical industrial scenarios. Here, the GA is utilized as an optimization mechanism in the supply chain planning process under uncertainty to determine the best production, inventory level, and distribution strategies.

## **3.4** Methodology

This research work aims to create a framework for creating the finest possible images by using an algorithm that considers all restrictions to produce better outcomes. For accurate and efficient picture enhancement, it employs the GA. It takes a picture as input and then enhances it. We start by interpreting an image and setting up the fitness feature. The GA begins with a randomly produced image that is similar in shape to the input image. This randomly generated image is continuously evolved utilizing crossover and mutation, as well as GA, until it achieves a better solution than the original.

### **3.4.1** Data representation

The initial step in solving a GA optimization problem is to consider the best way to describe the data. The chromosome (i.e. solution) is accepted as a 1D row vector by GA, but the input image is not one-dimensional. If the image is binary or grayscale, it may be 2D. If the input image is color, there may be more than two dimensions. If the color is RGB, there are three dimensions, one for each channel. A 1D vector must be used to represent data with more than one dimension. How can we turn multidimensional (MD) data into a 1D vector?

Starting with the simplest scenario, transforming a 2D image (or 2D matrix) into a 1D vector necessitates merging the two dimensions into a single dimension as shown in [Figure 3.5](#ch13.xhtml#f8). The matrix has numerous rows, and all of these rows must be merged into a single row. This is accomplished by stacking the several rows together. The following diagram depicts this. The illustration depicts a three-row, three-column image/matrix. There are 3 × 3 = 9 elements in all.

![Figure 3.5 Data representation of input image.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C003_005.jpg)

**Figure 3.5** Data representation of input image.[⏎](#ch13.xhtml#f8b)

The vector length will undoubtedly be 9 when converted to a 1D vector. The first three elements of this vector will be drawn from the image’s first row. The vector’s next three items will come from the second row, and the vector’s last three elements will come from the last row.

### **3.4.2** Fitness function

The fitness or objective function is a key segment of the GA, as it determines the quality of each image iteration based on a set of quantitative indices. As greyness and spatial ambiguity measurements for image enhancement and segmentation challenges, the proposed technique uses entropy (H), compactness (COMP), index of area coverage (IOAC), and their combinations as quantitative indices for evaluating picture quality. The average amount of fuzziness in the greyness of an image (X) is the degree of difficulty (ambiguity) in identifying whether a pixel should be considered black (dark) or white (bright) based on global information. Compactness and IOAC take local information into consideration and indicate the degree of fuzziness in an image’s shape and geometry (spatial domain). As a result, the concept of minimizing these ambiguous measurements can be used to build a fitness (assessment) function.

During each iteration, the fitness function calculates a composite score by applying a weighted formula that integrates these indices. Higher scores indicate better-defined image features and clearer contrasts, guiding the GA to select and breed images that move closer to an optimal, high-quality output. By evolving images towards a bimodal histogram with distinct peaks in intensity, the fitness function drives the enhancement process, ensuring the GA consistently favors improved clarity and contrast.

## **3.5** Experimentation and results

GA is utilized in a range of complicated problems; a second level job must be used to regulate their parameters for efficiency’s sake. As previously stated, the rate of mutation and crossover is known to have a significant impact on the efficiency and behavior of GA. Some researchers utilize tuning of the different parameters to select them, such that it identifies the best value of the parameters before the execution and then run the GA with these. Alternatively, other researchers have proved that utilizing control parameters (deterministic, adaptive) is more efficient in generating best or near-best results.

According to certain studies, high crossover rates of 80–95% and modest mutation rates of (0.1–1) should be used. The researcher advises utilizing a high mutation e.g. 50% with crossover and a small population in another study. Without any feedback from the search process or user involvement, the deterministic control parameter alters the parameter value during the execution. As a result, this kind of feature setting should be utilized to try to figure out which behavior the parameter should deal with in order to be effective and to discover the best or near-best answer. Using high crossover rates with low mutation rates, along with a large population size, promotes diversity within the population. However, in smaller populations, higher mutation rates are employed to introduce diversity and enhance search quality. By adjusting crossover and mutation rates, GA efficiency can be improved through a balanced approach to population diversity.

### **3.5.1** Qualitative and quantitative results

[Figures 3.6](#ch13.xhtml#f9) and [3.7](#ch13.xhtml#f10) show the input image and the enhanced image generated after applying the enhancement process using different numbers of generations and different crossover rate. The similar approach can also work on images other than those are related to medical field, as shown in [Figures 3.8](#ch13.xhtml#f11) and [3.9](#ch13.xhtml#f12). As shown in these figures, increasing the number of generations and adjusting crossover rates significantly impacted the quality of the enhanced images. With an optimal combination of 500 generations, a 0.4 crossover rate, and a 1% mutation rate, the images achieved the highest clarity and contrast enhancement. These optimized parameters resulted in images where critical medical features were highlighted, allowing for easier anomaly detection in clinical settings.

![Figure 3.6 Input image versus enhanced image: generations 500, crossover rate 0.7, and mutation rate 1%.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C003_006.jpg)

![Figure 3.7 Input image versus enhanced image: generations 600, crossover rate 0.4, and mutation rate 1%.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C003_007.jpg)
