The implementation of an image forgery detection system employing CNNs may encounter several problems. Here are some important points to think about:

*   Dataset bias: Bias in the training dataset may have an impact on how well the CNN model performs. For instance, the model may become biased towards one class or the other if the training dataset contains an excessive amount of legitimate or altered photos. A diverse array of photos must be gathered, and the classes must be as evenly distributed as feasible, to overcome this problem.
*   Limited data availability: For rare instances of picture forgeries, it can be difficult to compile a sizable collection of labelled images. The CNN model may overfit to the training dataset if the dataset that is trained with is too small, and it may perform badly on fresh data. This problem can be solved by using data augmentation techniques to fictitiously expand the dataset and strengthen the model’s robustness.
*   Computing power: Using a sizable dataset to train a CNN model might demand a lot of processing power, such as powerful GPUs and lots of RAM. This can be costly and might call for specialized gear. Cloud computing platforms can be utilized to rent compute resources on an as-needed basis to solve this problem.
*   Interpretability: It can be difficult to grasp how CNN models generate their predictions because of their difficulty in interpretation. This might be a problem in situations where explainability is crucial. To visualize which areas of the image are most crucial for the model’s predictions, techniques like saliency maps and gradient-based attribution can be employed to solve this problem.
*   Assaults from the opposite direction: CNN models are susceptible to assaults from the opposite direction, in which a minor perturbation is injected into the input image to make the model predict incorrectly. In applications where security is crucial, this can be a problem. This problem can be solved by using methods like adversarial training and input preprocessing to strengthen the model’s defense against hostile attacks.

## **5.5** Result analysis

The test dataset can be divided into multiple groups depending on the type of counterfeit (e.g., copy-move, splicing, or retouching) to know the system’s capacity to find out various forgeries. This allows for a distinct assessment of the system’s performance for each category. A confusion matrix shown in Figure 5.12 illustrates the performance of a classification model on a test dataset with known true values. It provides a visual representation of the algorithm’s effectiveness.

![Figure 5.12 Confusion matrix.](html/images/9781032942346_C005_012.jpg)

**Figure 5.12** Confusion matrix.

*   **Accuracy**: The overall accuracy of the model, which is the ratio of correctly predicted instances (both true positives and true negatives) to the total instances is calculated. The accuracy of 87.0% indicates that the model is generally performing well.

$\\begin{matrix} {~\\text{Accuracy} = \\frac{\\text{TP} + \\text{TN}}{\\text{TP} + \\text{TN} + \\text{FP} + \\text{FN}} = \\frac{370 + 357}{370 + 357 + 46 + 62}} \\\\ {= \\frac{727}{835} \\approx 0.870\\left( {\\text{or~}87.0\\%} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 

*   **Precision (for Real):** The ratio of correctly predicted real images to the total predicted real images. The precision of 88.9% indicates that when the model predicts an image as real, it is correct 88.9% of the time.

$\\text{Precision} = \\frac{\\text{TP}}{\\text{TP} + \\text{FP}} = \\frac{370}{370 + 46} \\approx 0.889\\left( {\\text{or~}88.9\\%} \\right)$ 

*   **Recall (Sensitivity or True Positive Rate, for Real)**: The ratio of correctly predicted real images to the total actual real images. The recall of 85.6% indicates that the model correctly identifies 85.6% of the real images.

$\\text{Recall} = \\frac{\\text{TP}}{\\text{TP} + \\text{FP}} = \\frac{370}{370 + 62} \\approx 0.856\\left( {\\text{or~}85.6\\%} \\right)$ 

*   **F1 Score (for Real)**: The harmonic mean of precision and recall. F1 Score of 87.2% strikes a balance between precision and recall, offering a unified metric that accounts for both false positives and false negatives.

$\\begin{matrix} {F1 \\cdot \\,\\text{Score} = 2 \\times \\frac{\\text{Precision} \\times \\text{Recall}}{\\text{Precision} + \\text{Recall}}} \\\\ {= 2 \\times \\frac{0.889 \\times 0.856}{0.889 + 0.856} \\approx 0.872\\left( {\\text{or~}87.2\\%} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ 

*   **Specificity**: The ratio of correctly predicted fake images to the total actual fake images. The specificity of 88.6% indicates that the model correctly identifies 88.6% of the fake images.

$\\text{Specificity} = \\frac{\\text{TN}}{\\text{TN} + \\text{FP}} = \\frac{357}{357 + 46} \\approx 0.886\\left( {\\text{or~}88.6\\%} \\right)$ 

*   **False Positive Rate (FPR)**: The ratio of incorrectly predicted real images to the total actual fake images. The false positive rate of 11.4% shows the proportion of fake images incorrectly classified as real.

$~\\text{FPR} = \\frac{\\text{FP}}{\\text{FP} + \\text{TN}} = \\frac{46}{46 + 357} \\approx 0.114\\left( {\\text{or~}11.4\\%} \\right)$ 

*   **The loss and accuracy curves for training and validation loss:** The training loss and validation loss graphs provide critical insights into the performance and generalization ability of your CNN model for forgery image detection.

As shown in Figure 5.13, the training loss starts at 0.6 which indicates the high error on the training data. This is expected as the model starts learning from scratch. The training loss decreases steadily over epochs while reaching 0.43. This indicates that the model is learning from the training data and improving its performance. Validation loss starts at 0.49 and decreases to 0.45, indicating that the model is initially learning and generalizing well to unseen data. The validation loss continues to decrease to 0.44, suggesting further improvement in the model’s generalization. However, the slight increase and subsequent return to 0.45 may indicate that the model is struggling to maintain its performance or is overfitting to the validation data.

![Figure 5.13 Accuracy and loss graph.](html/images/9781032942346_C005_013.jpg)

**Figure 5.13** Accuracy and loss graph.

The training accuracy starts at 0.6 and increases significantly to 0.85. This indicates that the model is learning from the training data and improving its ability to correctly classify samples. After reaching 0.85, the training accuracy stabilizes around this value, fluctuating slightly between 0.84 and 0.85. This suggests that the model has learned most of the patterns present in the training data and is performing consistently. The validation accuracy starts relatively high at 0.9, indicating that the model initially generalizes well to unseen data. The validation accuracy decreases to 0.86 and then to 0.84 before returning to 0.86. This suggests that the model may be struggling to generalize to unseen data, possibly due to overfitting or the presence of outliers or noisy data points in the validation set.

## **5.6** Future work

This research work presents a reliable and comprehensible CNN-based model for image forgery detection. Despite of great accuracy, CNN-based models can be challenging to comprehend and subject to hostile attacks. One of the primary limitations lies in the black-box nature of deep learning models, making it difficult to understand the decision-making process behind forgery detection. Future efforts should focus on improving the explainability and interpretability of CNN models through visualization techniques, attention mechanisms, and interpretable architectures.

Additionally, robustness against adversarial attacks remains a crucial area for improvement. Adversarial manipulations can deceive CNN models by introducing imperceptible perturbations, leading to misclassification. Enhancing model resilience through adversarial training, defensive distillation, and anomaly detection techniques can significantly improve reliability in forensic applications.

Furthermore, cross-domain generalization is essential for deploying CNN-based forgery detection models in real-world scenarios. Models trained on specific datasets often struggle to generalize across different forgery techniques and image sources. Leveraging domain adaptation, transfer learning, and meta-learning can help CNNs adapt to diverse datasets and maintain high detection accuracy.

Another promising direction involves integrating CNNs with hybrid approaches, combining deep learning with traditional forensic techniques. Fusion models—leveraging both deep feature extraction and handcrafted forensic features—can improve robustness and interpretability.

Lastly, the ethical and legal implications of image forgery detection must be considered. As forensic AI models become more advanced, ensuring bias-free decision-making, transparency, and compliance with legal standards will be critical for their adoption in law enforcement and media forensics.

Future research can concentrate on creating more resilient models that are more interpretable and less vulnerable to attacks.

*   Increasing the diversity and quality of training data: The training data’s quality and diversity have a significant impact on the accuracy of CNN-based models. To enhance the effectiveness of these models, future research can concentrate on creating datasets that are larger and more varied.
*   Integration of numerous modalities: In addition to looking at photos, other types of modalities, such audio and text, can also be used to detect image forgeries. Future research can concentrate on merging many modalities to create more thorough counterfeit detection systems that can examine various media types.
*   CNN-based models can be computationally expensive and may not be appropriate for deployment on mobile or low-power devices, making them unsuitable for mobile and low-power device modelling. Future research can concentrate on creating models that can operate on these gadgets without sacrificing accuracy.
*   Creating CNN-based models to detect video forgery is more difficult than detecting image forgery, despite the fact that CNN-based models have been successful at doing so. Future research can concentrate on creating CNN-based algorithms that can recognize video piracy, including deepfake videos.
*   Creating models for real-time forgery detection: Real-time image fraud detection is essential for many applications, including social networking platforms and video streaming. Future research can concentrate on creating CNN-based models that can be incorporated into these systems and can instantly detect forgeries.
*   Making multimodal forgery detection models multimodal forgery detection entails spotting fakes that were produced by fusing various media, such audio and visual. Future research can concentrate on creating CNN-based algorithms that can recognize multi-modal forgeries and offer more thorough analysis.

# Chapter 6The future of road safety integrating computational intelligence with network paradigms and AI innovations

Shruti Kalra, Ruby Beniwal, and Monika

DOI: [10.1201/9781003569619-6](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003569619-6)

## **6.1** Introduction

Road safety is a significant concern in a world where driving is an everyday activity for many individuals. Driver drowsiness is one of the primary risks on the road, leading to numerous accidents and, in severe cases, fatalities \[[1](#ch16.xhtml#r142)–[5](#ch16.xhtml#r146)\]. To tackle this issue, the paper titled “Driver Drowsiness Detection Using Machine Learning” aims to mitigate drowsiness-related accidents through an intelligent detection system. This study leverages machine learning (ML) technology, which learns from data to develop a system capable of identifying early signs of drowsiness in real time. The ultimate objective is to enhance road safety by preventing accidents before they happen. Artificial intelligence (AI) has become a transformative force across many sectors, with its application in road safety holding significant promise. Integrating AI into transportation systems introduces innovative solutions to improve safety, reduce accident rates, and save lives. With the rising number of vehicles and the increasing complexity of traffic systems, traditional road safety approaches are often insufficient. AI offers powerful tools to address these challenges effectively. A primary way AI supports road safety is through predictive analytics. By analyzing extensive datasets, including traffic patterns, weather conditions, and historical accident data, AI can predict potential high-risk areas. This predictive capability enables authorities to implement preventive measures, such as adjusting traffic signals or dispatching law enforcement to accident-prone zones, thereby reducing the probability of accidents before they occur \[[6](#ch16.xhtml#r147), [7](#ch16.xhtml#r148)\]. Driver assistance systems represent another crucial application of AI in road safety. Advanced driver-assistance systems (ADAS) use AI to enhance driving safety and improve the driving experience. Features such as adaptive cruise control, lane-keeping assistance, and automated emergency braking help drivers maintain safe distances, stay within lanes, and respond promptly to potential hazards. These systems serve as an additional layer of safety, compensating for human error—a leading factor in road accidents \[[8](#ch16.xhtml#r149)\].

AI has also proven highly beneficial in traffic management. Intelligent traffic management systems can process real-time traffic data to enhance traffic flow, reducing both congestion and accident risk. By dynamically adjusting traffic signals based on live conditions, AI helps create smoother traffic movement and prevents bottlenecks that often contribute to collisions. Additionally, these systems can redirect traffic in response to accidents or hazardous conditions, further promoting road safety. The rise of autonomous vehicles marks a major advancement in AI’s application to road safety. Self-driving cars, powered by AI, can make real-time safety-focused decisions. These vehicles maintain consistent, safe driving behaviors, communicate with other vehicles to prevent collisions, and handle unexpected scenarios with precision. By removing the human error factor, autonomous vehicles have the potential to significantly reduce accident rates on the roads. AI is also essential in driver monitoring. AI-equipped systems can observe drivers for signs of fatigue, distraction, or impairment, and alert them if they become inattentive or engage in risky behaviors. In critical situations, these systems can even take control of the vehicle to prevent accidents due to human factors. This proactive approach to driver monitoring supports drivers in staying alert and in control, contributing to safer driving conditions \[[9](#ch16.xhtml#r150)–[11](#ch16.xhtml#r152)\].

Incident detection and response is another area where AI enhances road safety. AI can rapidly detect accidents and other incidents on the road, providing real-time alerts to emergency services along with critical information about the situation. This capability leads to faster response times, which can be crucial in saving lives and mitigating the impact of accidents. Infrastructure monitoring is yet another application of AI in road safety. AI can monitor the condition of roads, bridges, and tunnels, identifying issues such as potholes or structural weaknesses that could pose a danger to drivers.

Proactive maintenance based on AI assessments helps prevent accidents caused by poor infrastructure conditions.

This chapter specifically addresses driver drowsiness, a major cause of road accidents worldwide, through a comprehensive real-time detection and prevention system. Utilizing Raspberry Pi and ML, the system integrates computer vision, deep learning, and physiological signal analysis. Live video footage of the driver’s face is processed using Open CV and dlib to detect facial features indicative of drowsiness. ML models analyze these features to identify signs such as prolonged eye closure and yawning. Upon detection, the system issues alerts to ensure driver safety and prevent accidents. This chapter offers a practical solution for enhancing road safety by effectively combating driver fatigue, showcasing the transformative power of AI and computational intelligence (CI) within network paradigms for a safer future on the roads. [Figure 6.1](#ch16.xhtml#f28) illustrates how AI enhances road safety through various interconnected components. It begins with data collection using sensors, cameras, and GPS. The collected data undergoes processing using ML, big data analytics, and edge computing. Real-time monitoring tracks traffic flow, driver behavior, and weather conditions. Predictive modeling forecasts accidents and traffic trends. Decision-making is facilitated by autonomous vehicles and smart traffic lights. Driver assistance systems like lane keeping, automatic braking, and adaptive cruise control enhance safety. Traffic management is optimized with dynamic traffic signals and route optimization. Incident response includes emergency alerts and accident response coordination, ensuring comprehensive road safety \[[12](#ch16.xhtml#r153), [13](#ch16.xhtml#r154)\]. We aim to generate an intelligent system that determines the physiological signals, facial expressions, and eye movements, all of which serve as keys to detecting driver drowsiness and fatigue. Early detection would lead to immediate alerting of the driver, thereby providing the best approach to avoiding potential accidents. For wider applicability, we will design the system to seamlessly connect with in-vehicle technology, permitting its use with every kind of vehicle. The hardware implementation will make use of a system based on hardware such as the Raspberry Pi, Pi camera module, and relay to provide real-time detection and alerts. We also understand that driving conditions can vary greatly—sometimes it’s sunny, sometimes it’s rainy, and road conditions can differ. Therefore, our system will be adaptable to different situations. Additionally, it must be easy to use. We are focusing on making the interface friendly and straightforward, so both drivers and operators find it easy to interact with \[[14](#ch16.xhtml#r155)–[16](#ch16.xhtml#r157)\].

![Figure 6.1 AI enhances road safety through various interconnected components.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_001.jpg)

**Figure 6.1** AI enhances road safety through various interconnected components.[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f28b)

The objectives of this research are as follows:

*   Enhance Driver Alertness and Safety: Implement AI-driven real-time monitoring systems to detect signs of driver drowsiness, such as prolonged eye closure and yawning, and provide immediate alerts to prevent accidents caused by fatigue.
*   Improve Emergency Response: Develop AI systems that swiftly detect drowsiness-induced incidents, providing real-time data to emergency services for faster response times, ultimately minimizing the severity of accidents and improving road safety outcomes.
*   Implement computer vision techniques to analyze facial features and eye movements for early detection of driver fatigue.
*   Investigate the integration of hardware components such as Raspberry Pi and camera modules to enhance the portability and efficiency of the detection system.

## **6.3** Methodology

The flowchart in [Figure 6.2](#ch16.xhtml#f29) illustrates a comprehensive process for detecting driver drowsiness, designed to be implemented in real-time systems to monitor driver alertness and take preventive actions to avoid accidents caused by drowsiness. The process begins with the initialization of the system, starting all necessary hardware and software components required for monitoring and processing driver behavior. The first operational step involves capturing the driver’s facial features using a camera, usually installed on the dashboard or the steering column, ensuring it has a clear view of the driver’s face. The camera captures continuous video footage, which is then processed in real time. Once the driver’s face is captured, the system collects raw data, including video frames and other sensor inputs if available. These data are essential for subsequent analysis and processing. The collected data undergoes image processing techniques to enhance the quality of the captured frames, including noise reduction, contrast adjustment, and other preprocessing techniques to ensure the data is suitable for feature extraction. Specific facial features are detected from the processed images, including the eyes, mouth, and head position. Advanced algorithms, such as Haar cascades or deep learning-based methods, are used to identify and track these features accurately.

![Figure 6.2 Comprehensive process for detecting driver drowsiness.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_002.jpg)

**Figure 6.2** Comprehensive process for detecting driver drowsiness.[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f29b)

The detected features are then analyzed to extract relevant metrics, including the blink rate, yawning frequency, and head nodding patterns, which are critical indicators of driver drowsiness. The extracted features are analyzed to determine the driver’s state, with high yawning frequency and low blink rates being common indicators of drowsiness. This analysis is performed using pretrained ML models that can distinguish between normal and drowsy behavior. For continuous improvement, the system uses collected data to train and validate ML models, which are crucial for accurately predicting drowsiness based on real-time data. The trained models are deployed in real time to monitor the driver’s state continuously, performing ongoing analysis to detect any signs of drowsiness. A decision node evaluates the results from the analysis stage, determining if the driver exhibits a high yawning rate or low blinking rate, which indicates drowsiness. If the driver is determined to be awake, the system continues monitoring without triggering any alerts or actions. However, if the driver is found to be drowsy, the system initiates a series of preventive actions.

These actions include stopping the engine and displaying a warning to alert the driver. The system also continues monitoring for drowsiness to ensure the driver’s safety. Additionally, the warning system includes multiple alert mechanisms, such as audio alerts, visual alerts, and haptic feedback, to effectively alert the driver and prevent potential accidents. The integration of these components creates a robust drowsiness detection system that leverages real-time data collection, image processing, feature extraction, ML, and alert mechanisms to enhance road safety by preventing accidents caused by driver drowsiness.

[Figure 6.3](#ch16.xhtml#f30) illustrates a detailed map of 68 facial landmarks commonly used in facial recognition and analysis. These landmarks are pivotal in various applications such as facial recognition systems, emotion detection, drowsiness detection, and other areas of computer vision. Understanding the specific roles and positions of these landmarks can provide insights into their significance and usage in technological implementations. The facial landmarks are distributed across key facial features and are numbered from 1 to 68. Starting from the left side of the face to the right, the landmarks are strategically placed to capture the contours and critical points that define the human face. A point 1–17 outlines the shape of the face, starting from the left jawline, moving down to the chin, and then up to the right jawline. These points are essential in determining the overall shape of the face and are often used in facial contouring tasks. Points 18–27 correspond to the eyebrows. Points 18–22 define the left eyebrow, while points 23–27 define the right eyebrow. These points help in capturing the arch and orientation of the eyebrows, which are significant for facial expression analysis and emotion detection. The nose is defined by points 28–36. Point 28 is at the top of the nose bridge, and the points progress downward to point 36, which is located at the bottom of the nose. Points 31–35 specifically outline the nostrils. The precise detection of these points is crucial for tasks that involve nose shape analysis or nasal feature extraction. Points 37–48 delineate the eyes. The left eye is marked by points 37–42, while the right eye is marked by points 43–48. These points include the upper and lower eyelids and the corners of the eyes. Accurate localization of these points is vital for gaze tracking, blink detection, and overall eye analysis, which are used in applications like drowsiness detection and human–computer interaction.

![Figure 6.3 Detailed map of 68 facial landmarks.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_003.jpg)

**Figure 6.3** Detailed map of 68 facial landmarks.[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f30b)

The mouth is defined by points 49–68. The outer lip line is marked by points 49–60, and the inner lip line is defined by points 61–68. These landmarks are used to capture the shape and movements of the lips, which are important for tasks such as lip reading, speech recognition, and emotion detection. Each of these landmarks is identified through advanced image processing and ML techniques. The process typically involves detecting the face within an image and then applying algorithms to locate each landmark precisely. Commonly used methods include Haar cascades, convolutional neural networks (CNNs), and ensemble regression trees. The utility of these landmarks extends beyond static image analysis. In video analysis, tracking these points over time allows for dynamic facial expression recognition, real-time emotion analysis, and monitoring of facial movements. For instance, in drowsiness detection systems, continuous monitoring of eye landmarks (points 37–48) can help determine the blink rate and eye closure duration, which are indicators of drowsiness. Moreover, the accuracy of these landmark points plays a crucial role in the effectiveness of various applications. Misalignment or incorrect detection can lead to errors in the interpretation and analysis of facial features. Therefore, the algorithms used for landmark detection are designed to be robust and precise, even under different lighting conditions, facial orientations, and expressions.

To gauge the system’s effectiveness, evaluating it with key performance metrics—such as accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score—is essential. Each of these metrics offers distinct perspectives on the model’s success in accurately distinguishing between classes.

The accuracy metric indicates the portion of correct predictions out of all predictions made, expressed as

$\\text{Accuracy} = \\frac{\\text{True~Positives} + \\text{True}}{\\text{Negatives~Total~Predictions}}$(1) 

where True Positives (TP) refers to instances where the model correctly predicts positives, True Negatives (TN) represents correct negative predictions, False Positives (FP) represents incorrect positive predictions, and False Negatives (FN) represents missed positive cases.

Precision measures the ratio of accurate positive predictions to the total number of instances predicted as positive, calculated as

$\\text{Precision} = \\frac{\\text{True~Positives}}{\\text{True~Positives} + \\text{False~Positives}}$(2) 

Recall, also known as sensitivity, measures the proportion of actual positives correctly identified by the model, defined by

$\\text{Recall} = \\frac{\\text{True~Positives}}{\\text{True~Positives} + \\text{False~Negatives}}$(3) 

The F1-score, a harmonic mean of precision and recall, combines these two metrics to provide a balanced evaluation, particularly useful when both false positives and false negatives are important:

$F\_{1}\\,\\text{Score} = 2 \\times \\frac{\\text{Precision} \\times \\text{Recall}}{\\text{Precision} + \\text{Recall}}$ 

Evaluating these metrics on both training and test datasets is crucial for a complete assessment, as it allows for comparison of the model’s performance on known versus new data. This practice helps identify issues such as over-fitting or under-fitting, providing a comprehensive view of how well the model generalizes to unseen cases.

## **6.4** Results and discussion

The plots in [Figures 6.4](#ch16.xhtml#f31), 6.5, 6.6, and [6.7](#ch16.xhtml#f34) deliver a detailed examination of the model’s effectiveness in identifying various states of drowsiness, such as yawning, nonyawning, closed eyes, and open eyes. [Figure 6.4](#ch16.xhtml#f31), titled “Precision, Recall, and F1-score by Category”, displays precision, recall, and F1-score metrics for each category: yawn, nonyawn, closed eyes, and open eyes, as well as the macro average. In this figure, precision, illustrated by blue bars, reflects the model’s capability to limit false positives by indicating the proportion of true positives out of all positive predictions. Precision values are stable across categories, with scores near 0.97 for yawn, nonyawn, and open eyes, while closed eyes has a slightly lower precision of 0.96. Recall, depicted with green bars, assesses the model’s ability to identify all actual positives, calculated as the ratio of true positives to total actual positives. Recall reaches its peak for nonyawn at 0.98, slightly below for yawn at 0.98, and lowest for closed eyes at 0.95.

![Figure 6.4 Precision, recall, and F1-score for each category (yawn, nonyawn, closed eyes, open eyes) and the macro average (macro average).⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_004.jpg)

**Figure 6.4** Precision, recall, and F1-score for each category (yawn, nonyawn, closed eyes, open eyes) and the macro average (macro average).[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f31b)

![Figure 6.7 Model’s accuracy and loss change over training epochs.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_007.jpg)

**Figure 6.7** Model’s accuracy and loss change over training epochs.[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f34b)

The F1-score, shown in red bars, merges precision and recall via their harmonic mean, providing a balanced metric that accounts for both false positives and false negatives. F1-scores are close to both precision and recall values, indicating balanced performance across categories. The macro average metric reflects an overall balance in performance, with precision, recall, and F1-score all around 0.97. [Figure 6.5](#ch16.xhtml#f32) presents an overview of the model’s accuracy and loss metrics. The accuracy plot on the left indicates an accuracy of 0.96, meaning that 96% of instances are correctly classified. The loss plot on the right quantifies prediction errors, showing a value of 0.09, which denotes a low error rate. Together, these plots summarize the model’s strong accuracy and minimal prediction error.

![Figure 6.5 Overall accuracy and loss of the model.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_005.jpg)

**Figure 6.5** Overall accuracy and loss of the model.[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f32b)

The “Precision by Category” plot (refer [Figure 6.6](#ch16.xhtml#f33)) shows precision scores for yawn, nonyawn, closed eyes, and open eyes, consistently high across categories, ranging from 0.96 to 0.97. The “Recall by Category” plot highlights recall scores, with nonyawn having the highest recall at 0.98 and closed eyes having the lowest at 0.95. The “F1-score by Category” plot combines the insights from precision and recall, showing F1-scores for each category, consistent with the precision and recall values, demonstrating a balanced performance. These plots emphasize the system’s reliability in detecting different states, with minor variations in recall for closed eyes.

![Figure 6.6 Precision scores for yawn, nonyawn, closed eyes, and open eyes, consistently high across categories, ranging from 0.96 to 0.97.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_006.jpg)

**Figure 6.6** Precision scores for yawn, nonyawn, closed eyes, and open eyes, consistently high across categories, ranging from 0.96 to 0.97.[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f33b)

The plot in [Figure 6.7](#ch16.xhtml#f34), ”Model Accuracy and Loss over Epochs,” illustrates how the model’s accuracy and loss change over training epochs. Accuracy, shown with a solid blue line, steadily increases from around 0.85 to 0.96 over 10 epochs, indicating that the model improves its predictive capabilities as it learns from the training data. Loss, depicted with a dashed red line, decreases from around 0.35 to 0.09 over the same period, showing that the model is effectively minimizing errors during training. The dual-axis plot helps visualize the trade-off between accuracy and loss during the training process; as accuracy increases, loss decreases, indicating successful learning and optimization.

[Figure 6.8](#ch16.xhtml#f35) displays the model’s accuracy across 200 training epochs. Accuracy serves as a key metric, showing the proportion of instances correctly classified relative to the total. This plot includes two distinct lines: a blue line for training accuracy and an orange line for validation accuracy. At the beginning of training, both accuracy values increase rapidly, illustrating the model’s quick adaptation in making accurate predictions based on the training data. As training progresses, these accuracy curves begin to level off, indicating that the model is approaching optimal performance, with minimal improvement from further training. The close proximity of the training and validation accuracy lines suggests the model generalizes well to unseen data—an essential quality in ML models. Minor variations in the validation accuracy line are expected due to changes in the validation data, but overall, the high accuracy values point to a model that is both effective and reliable in its classifications.

![Figure 6.8 Accuracy of the model over 200 training epochs.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_008.jpg)

**Figure 6.8** Accuracy of the model over 200 training epochs.[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f35b)

[Figure 6.9](#ch16.xhtml#f36) illustrates the loss experienced by the model across 200 training epochs. Loss represents the degree of error in the model’s predictions, with lower values indicating stronger predictive accuracy. This plot, like the accuracy graph, contains two lines: a blue line representing training loss and an orange line for validation loss. In the beginning, both loss values are fairly high, reflecting initial prediction errors as the model starts learning from the dataset. As training progresses, both the training and validation loss drop significantly, demonstrating that the model is successfully minimizing prediction errors. This rapid drop in loss during early epochs is typical in ML, as the model quickly picks up on the main patterns within the data. Eventually, the loss values stabilize, suggesting that the model has reached convergence, where further training yields minimal improvement. The close proximity of the training and validation loss curves indicates effective generalization, with the model performing well on both known and new data, avoiding overfitting. Minor spikes in validation loss may appear due to fluctuations in the validation dataset, but overall, the trend shows steady progress in reducing prediction errors.

![Figure 6.9 Loss of the model over the same 200 training epochs.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_009.jpg)

**Figure 6.9** Loss of the model over the same 200 training epochs.[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f36b)

[Figure 6.10](#ch16.xhtml#f37) presents a detailed view of the model’s classification performance across various categories. The confusion matrix is a valuable tool for evaluating classification performance, showing true positives, false positives, true negatives, and false negatives. In this normalized matrix, the values are expressed as proportions, simplifying interpretation. This matrix includes four categories: yawn, nonyawn, closed eyes, and open eyes. Each cell shows the proportion of predictions for a particular true label and predicted label combination. Diagonal cells represent correct classifications, where the predicted label matches the actual label, with higher values indicating successful identification. For example, the yawn–yawn cell has a value of 0.9820, meaning 98.20% of actual yawn cases were correctly identified as yawn. Similarly, the nonyawn−nonyawn cell has a value of 0.9837, indicating high accuracy in detecting the nonyawn category. Off-diagonal cells show misclassifications, where predictions do not match true labels. For instance, the closed eyes−open eyes cell has a value of 0.0287, suggesting 2.87% of true closed eyes cases were incorrectly labeled as open eyes. Overall, high values along the diagonal and low values in off-diagonal cells emphasize the model’s effectiveness in distinguishing between drowsiness states. The color gradient in the heatmap enhances visualization, with darker shades indicating higher proportions and lighter shades representing lower proportions. This matrix offers a clear summary of the model’s strengths and weaknesses across categories, highlighting areas of accuracy and possible improvement.

![Figure 6.10 Model’s classification performance across different categories.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C006_010.jpg)

**Figure 6.10** Model’s classification performance across different categories.[⏎](#ch16.xhtml#f37b)

In [Figure 6.9](#ch16.xhtml#f36), we see a person facing the camera with several indicators overlaid on their face. The system is running on a Raspberry Pi interface, which is evident from the desktop environment. The screen shows two main windows: one with the video feed displaying the person’s face and another terminal window displaying status messages.

*   **Blinks:** The system has detected 14 blinks, which indicates the number of times the person has blinked. Blinks are crucial indicators of drowsiness as a high blink rate can signal fatigue.
*    **EAR (Eye Aspect Ratio):** The EAR value is 0.36. EAR is a measure used to detect blinks by analyzing the ratio of distances between certain points on the eyes. A lower EAR typically indicates closed eyes, which can be used to detect drowsiness.
*   **Yawns:** The system shows 0 yawns detected. Yawning is another important indicator of drowsiness.
*   **MAR (Mouth Aspect Ratio):** The MAR value is 0.60. MAR measures the mouth’s openness and is used to detect yawning. A higher MAR indicates an open mouth, which is associated with yawning and potential drowsiness.

The terminal window on the left shows a series of messages, repeatedly displaying ”Wake up...”. This indicates that the system is alerting the driver to stay awake, likely due to detecting signs of drowsiness through blink and EAR metrics. [Figure 6.10](#ch16.xhtml#f37) depicts another instance of the system monitoring a different individual. Similar to the first image, the system overlays indicators on the person’s face and displays the terminal window with status messages.

*   **Blinks:** Here, the system has detected 17 blinks. This higher blink count may indicate an increased level of drowsiness compared to the first image.
*   **EAR:** The EAR value is 0.16, significantly lower than the first image. This lower EAR suggests that the person’s eyes are more frequently closed or partially closed, which is a strong indicator of drowsiness.
*   **Yawns:** The system has detected 1 yawn. The presence of yawns further corroborates the likelihood of the driver being drowsy.
*   **MAR:** The MAR value is 0.30, indicating the person’s mouth is less open compared to the first image, but the detection of a yawn suggests intermittent larger openings.

The terminal window in this image shows a more urgent message: ”Please Take Rest...” and ”Sleeping...”. These messages suggest that the system has determined the driver is significantly drowsy and needs to take a rest to avoid potential accidents. The system operates by continuously capturing video frames of the driver’s face and analyzing specific features that indicate drowsiness. It utilizes computer vision techniques to identify facial landmarks and ML models to interpret these landmarks into meaningful metrics like EAR and MAR. The system evaluates the driver’s alertness in real time by tracking blink and yawn frequency alongside the aspect ratios of the eyes and mouth. Upon identifying indicators of drowsiness, it activates visual and, potentially, auditory alerts to encourage the driver to stay attentive or take a break. This proactive strategy reduces the hazards linked to drowsy driving, thereby improving road safety.

## **6.5** Conclusion

In conclusion, this research represents a pivotal leap forward in bolstering road safety through the fusion of cutting-edge computer vision and ML methodologies. By leveraging the robust facial landmark detection capabilities of the dlib library alongside real-time eye aspect ratio and lip distance analysis, our system adeptly identifies early indicators of driver drowsiness and yawning. Moreover, the integration of essential hardware components like the Raspberry Pi board and camera module enables seamless image processing and feature extraction from webcam feeds. The utilization of Open CV, dlib, and imutils libraries further amplifies the system’s capabilities, facilitating rapid real-time data processing and analysis (Figures 6.11 and 6.12).

![Figure 6.11 Model’s classification performance across different categories.](html/images/9781032942346_C006_011.jpg)

**Figure 6.11** Model’s classification performance across different categories.

![Figure 6.12 Model’s classification performance across different categories.](html/images/9781032942346_C006_012.jpg)

**Figure 6.12** Model’s classification performance across different categories.

The multi-threaded architecture of our system enables simultaneous monitoring of multiple indicators, providing a holistic solution for detecting various manifestations of driver fatigue. Furthermore, our chapter underscores the significance of timely alerts, employing audible warnings to prompt drivers to take necessary rest breaks and mitigate potential accidents stemming from impaired awareness. Given the paramount importance of road safety, our innovative system presents a promising framework for integration into vehicles and transportation infrastructure. Through the strategic amalgamation of ML and advanced technologies, our chapter demonstrates its potential to significantly enhance public safety on roads. The success of this endeavor not only underscores the efficacy of ML in tackling real-world challenges but also underscores its capacity to foster a safer and more secure driving environment for all. Real-time alerts and notifications are a critical feature of our proposed system, providing immediate warnings to drivers when signs of drowsiness are detected. By analyzing facial expressions, eye movements, and other physiological indicators through AI algorithms, our system ensures that drivers receive timely notifications, prompting them to take necessary rest breaks and thereby preventing potential accidents due to impaired awareness.

Integration with existing vehicle systems is another significant aspect of our proposal. AI-driven drowsiness detection modules can be seamlessly integrated into current vehicle safety systems, ensuring compatibility and ease of adoption across various vehicle types and models. This integration is crucial for widespread implementation and adoption, enhancing the overall safety features of modern vehicles. Additionally, our research highlights the importance of data analytics and reporting tools that leverage AI insights to track and analyze driver drowsiness trends over time. These tools can provide valuable information to fleet managers, transportation authorities, and policy-makers, enabling them to make informed decisions and enhance road safety strategies. Comprehensive analytics and reporting can help identify patterns and trends in driver behavior, contributing to the development of more effective interventions and policies to improve road safety.

# Chapter 7Document classification engine to segregate multilingual PDF documents

Apeksha Aggarwal and Pawan Kumar

DOI: [10.1201/9781003569619-7](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003569619-7)

## **7.1** Introduction

The expedited digitalization of data caused increasing interest toward the paperless electronic documents; automating the process of document classification is becoming a necessity for categorizing the documents properly and knowledge discovery. Deep learning algorithms are used widely for the classification of the electronic documents such as E-mails, blogs, digital libraries, and online news to get some meaningful knowledge out of them. The large and increasing volume of the electronic documents creates the need for intelligent computational techniques to classify and organize the documents to their respective classes automatically. Most of the recent work has been done on the automatic classification of the same language documents. The present work focuses on the extraction of multi-lingual text from the portable document format (PDF) documents.

Classifying such documents from a collection of documents of different languages is an error-prone, tedious, and time-consuming process. The document classification engine can assist humans in the separation of documents of different languages into different folders. This prevents the document from getting lost if they are not properly labeled and accelerates the process of usage of these documents for proper application. The proposed classification engine can also ingest and classify the PDF documents containing the data in the form of the images. Such document classifier can find applications in various fields like text mining, information extraction systems, etc. The information extraction systems, which can work only on the documents of a specific language require these documents to be segregated before they can be applied for the extraction of the information. Therefore, the document classification engine serves as a prerequisite to classify documents to leverage extraction of information from a variety of PDF documents.

The classification of the documents \[[1](#ch17.xhtml#r168)\] aids in organization of the documents required for annotation, curation, analysis, and retrieval. A variety of methods have been studied by the researchers for the classification of documents. In general, the work focused on the information and document retrieval mainly utilizes the core technologies behind search engine such as dictionaries and indexing \[[2](#ch17.xhtml#r169)\]. Considerable work has been performed using these fundamental technologies and improvements have been performed through query reformulation and feedback \[[3](#ch17.xhtml#r170), [4](#ch17.xhtml#r171)\].

Recent work on documents classification involved the use of support vector machines (SVM) \[[5](#ch17.xhtml#r172)–[7](#ch17.xhtml#r174)\]. SVM algorithm rely on the use of kernel functions to provide separating hyperplanes. Various string kernels are used such as mismatch kernel \[[8](#ch17.xhtml#r175)\] and the spectrum kernel \[[9](#ch17.xhtml#r176)\] are used for information retrieval and document classification along with RNA and DNA sequence data.

For example, decision trees \[[3](#ch17.xhtml#r170)\] and naïve Bayes \[[10](#ch17.xhtml#r177),[11](#ch17.xhtml#r178)\] have also been used in the past. Additional methods such as topic modeling, deep learning, etc. have also been explored which can provide better accuracy \[[12](#ch17.xhtml#r179)\]. Deep learning \[[13](#ch17.xhtml#r180), [14](#ch17.xhtml#r181)\] is also used extensively these days for varied other applications as well. The basic neural network architecture is made up of an interconnected network of nonlinear nodes which are organized in the form of layers. All these layers are organized sequentially in a neural network. However, the task of automatic document classification is becoming more and more challenging with time due to the rapid growth in the size of the corpus in the digitalized era. The size increase has occurred in almost all areas \[[15](#ch17.xhtml#r182)\].

The chapter organization is given further as: [Section 7.2](#ch17.xhtml#h1-035) describes the algorithms used for comparative analysis. [Section 7.3](#ch17.xhtml#h1-036) explains in detail about the proposed architecture and methodology. [Section 7.4](#ch17.xhtml#f43) describes the experiments performed and evaluation metrices as well as the results in details. [Section 7.5](#ch17.xhtml#h1-038) concludes the work.

![Figure 7.4 Model architecture using Bi-LSTM.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C007_004.jpg)

**Figure 7.4** Model architecture using Bi-LSTM.[⏎](#ch17.xhtml#f43b)

## **7.2** Techniques used

### **7.2.1** Naïve Bayes

Naïve Bayes (NB) algorithm is a probabilistic classification algorithm based on Bayes’ theorem. The assumption of Naïve Bayes includes independence of features, where features do not interact with each other while predicting the classification results \[[16](#ch17.xhtml#r183)\]. The NB classifier is a supervised learning algorithm and can be efficiently trained using small amount of data. Since the variables or the features are assumed to be independent, thus only the calculation of the variances of each class variable is required instead of calculating the values of the entire covariance matrix. Naïve Bayes algorithm is remarkable in terms of performance because it can train on limited data as well \[[17](#ch17.xhtml#r184)–[21](#ch17.xhtml#r188)\]. Equation (7.1) describes the Bayes Rule below.

$P\\left( {c\_{i}~\\text{|}~D} \\right) = \\frac{P\\left( c\_{i} \\right)P\\text{(}D~\\text{|}~c\_{i})}{P\\left( D \\right)}$(7.1) 

where $P\\left( C\_{i} \\right) = P(C = c\_{i}) = \\frac{N\_{i}}{N}$

where *P*(*C*[*i*]) is the ratio of number of training samples of class *C*[*i*] in dataset (i.e. *N*[*i*]) over total number of samples in the dataset (i.e. *N*)

Although Naïve Bayes is based on the unrealistic assumption of independence between different features but still it delivers reasonable performance for many classification tasks. It is simple and elementary to implement. Therefore, it has been used by the researchers in different tasks \[[22](#ch17.xhtml#r189), [23](#ch17.xhtml#r190)\]. In \[[24](#ch17.xhtml#r191)\], the Naïve Bayes classifier performed exceptionally well where the probabilities computed by the model were not important. It is used for different classification applications like spam filtering \[[25](#ch17.xhtml#r192)\] in emails, spam categorization, and categorization of web content.

### **7.2.2** Long short-term memory model

Long short-term memory (LSTM) was proposed in \[[26](#ch17.xhtml#r193)\] which has proven to be extremely powerful in the past for learning sequences over standard recurrent neural networks (RNNs). RNNs are basically neural networks unfolded over multiple time steps and thus can process sequential data such as time series and sentences. With each time step having hidden state, processing inputs from the previous hidden state and current inputs. The disadvantages of RNNs are vanishing and exploding gradients. The transition functions of the LSTM \[[26](#ch17.xhtml#r193)\] are defined as per Equation (7.2)–(7.7): *i*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*i*].\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*i*])(7.2)

*f*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*f*].\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*f*])(7.3) *o*[*t*] = *σ*(*W*[*o*].\[*h*[*t* − 1],*x*[*t*]\]+*b*[*o*])(7.4)   
*c*[*t*] = *f*[*t*]⊙ *c*[*t* − 1] + *i*[*t*] ⊙ *q*[*t*]  
(7.5) *h*[*t*] = *o*[*t*] ⊙ tanh(*c*[*t*])(7.6) 

In the above equations, ⊙ signifies the element wise multiplication operation. The notations are: current time step *x*[*t*], old hidden state *h*[*t-1*], the input gate *i*[*t*], the forget gate *f*[*t*], and the output gate *o*[*t*]. Weights are denoted by *W*[*o*], *W*[*q*], and *W*[*i*] which are trainable parameters with respect to different gates. Previous and current memory cell are denoted by *c*[*t-1*] and *c*[*t*], respectively The current hidden state *h*[*t*] is modified using *c*[*t*] and is collectively decided by these gates. [Figure 7.1](#ch17.xhtml#f40) depicts the LSTM cell diagram.

![Figure 7.1 Architecture of LSTM cell.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C007_001.jpg)

**Figure 7.1** Architecture of LSTM cell.[⏎](#ch17.xhtml#f40b)

### **7.2.3** Bidirectional long short-term memory (Bi-LSTM) model

Bidirectional LSTM (Bi-LSTM) models are helpful in extracting more contextual information \[[27](#ch17.xhtml#r194)\]. The data processing is performed in the backward and forward direction simultaneously \[[28](#ch17.xhtml#r195)\] and the LSTM outputs are merged using the sum statistic \[[29](#ch17.xhtml#r196)\].

Bi-LSTM models serve as state-of-the-art models for classifying the sequential data into multiple classes and have been used extensively for speech recognition \[[30](#ch17.xhtml#r197), [31](#ch17.xhtml#r198), [32](#ch17.xhtml#r199), [33](#ch17.xhtml#r200), [34](#ch17.xhtml#r201)\]. The text in the pages of the PDF documents is also a sequence of words and multiclass criteria also exist as the documents are to be classified into the respective language class. Therefore, Bi-LSTM model is applied for the classification task in this paper.

## **7.3** Methodology

### **7.3.1** Dataset description

Dataset comprises pdf files from three languages, viz. English, Slovakian, and Czech language. Data cleaning and preprocessing is performed over the sentences. Count Vectorizer is used to encode set of sequences. Dataset is split as: 80% of data is used for training and remaining 20% is used in testing. Furthermore, cross validation is used to avoid the overfitting to a single sample set. Data are trained for 100 epochs which is selected by using grid search methodology. [Table 7.1](#ch17.xhtml#t9) shows the dataset details.

**Table 7.1** Dataset samples[⏎](#ch17.xhtml#t9b)

 
| Class || Number of samples |
| --- | --- |
| English || 5000 |
| --- | --- |
| Slovakian || 5000 |
| Czech || 5000 |

### **7.3.2** Feature extraction

Before starting with the analysis, firstly the extraction of the text from the PDF document file is performed. Tesseract OCR system is used to extract the content from a document page if the page is present in the form of a raster image \[[15](#ch17.xhtml#r182)\]. After extracting, the text passes through document classification engine, explained in detail in Section 7.3.3.

### **7.3.3** Document classification engine

The document classification engine can assist humans in the separation of documents of different languages into different folders. This prevents the documents from getting lost if they are not properly labeled and accelerates the process of usage of these documents for proper application. The proposed classification engine can also ingest and classify the PDF documents containing the data in the form of images. Document classification engine serves as a prerequisite to classify documents to leverage extraction of information from a variety of PDF documents.

[Figure 7.2](#ch17.xhtml#f41) depicts methodological steps for carrying out classification. Initially, preprocessing is done, which includes removal of stop words using NLTK library in Python. Further missing values, interpolation, and stemming are performed in the dataset. Later on feature selection is performed.

![Figure 7.2 Methodological steps.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C007_002.jpg)

**Figure 7.2** Methodological steps.[⏎](#ch17.xhtml#f41b)

Since most of the text was obtained from the image format pages of the PDF by applying the OCR on the raster scanned documents with noise, many out-of-vocabulary words were found in the extracted data. Such problem can be resolved either by running a named entity recognition (NER) \[[35](#ch17.xhtml#r202)\] system or by the use of stemming and multiple regular expressions \[[36](#ch17.xhtml#r203)\]. In this work, we have used stemming as a preprocessing step.

## **7.4** Experiments and results

### **7.4.1** Experimental details

For carrying out experiments, we have utilized Spyder 3.2.8 notebook with Python version 3.6.5. The experiments are executed on Windows 11, 64-bit OS system having 16GB RAM, with Intel(R) i5 CPU @ 2.40 GHz. Python Tensorflow and Keras API are used primarily to implement various algorithms in this work.

### **7.4.2** Baseline models

#### **7.4.2.1** DNN: deep neural network

Architectural details of DNN are defined in [Figure 7.3](#ch17.xhtml#f42).

![Figure 7.3 DNN architecture.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C007_003.jpg)

**Figure 7.3** DNN architecture.[⏎](#ch17.xhtml#f42b)

### **7.4.2.2** Lstm

Relu activation is used in all the layers except for last layer. Binary cross entropy loss is used as a loss function and for optimization, Adam is used for hyperparameter tuning across multiple epochs.

### **7.4.2.3** BiLSTM

The proposed architecture composed of embedding layer, spatial dropout layer, and a single Bi-LSTM Layer. We have used word embeddings of 128 dimensions as input layer for generating features of sparse input documents. To avoid overfitting, we have used spatial dropout of 1-dimensions with 40% dropping rate. We have further implemented Bi-LSTM layer along with further dropout rate of 20%. The learning parameters utilize grid search technique for optimization. Bi-LSTM layer utilizes 196 neurons as input. This choice of 196 neurons is selected after performing a grid search by selecting neuron range from 32 to 256 neuron sizes, and selecting the one that is giving good performance without overfitting. The final output layer utilizes Softmax function classifying multiple languages based on probabilities. Categorical cross entropy is used as a loss function. Adam is used for hyperparameter tuning as it has given good performance over other hyperparameter tuning algorithms. The output dense layer consists of three neurons for each of the classes. [Figure 7.4](#ch17.xhtml#f43) shows the architecture of the same.

### **7.4.3** Results

For evaluation of models, F-score and accuracy metrics are used in this work. [Table 7.2](#ch17.xhtml#t10) shows results for Bi-LSTM architecture.

| --- | --- |
| F- score || 0.98 |
| --- | --- |
| Accuracy || 98.73% |

[Table 7.3](#ch17.xhtml#t11) shows a comparative analysis over multiple models. Results show the best performance of our proposed architecture using Bi-LSTM. [Figure 7.5](#ch17.xhtml#f44) shows sample classifications performed by the model.

## **7.5** Conclusion

Multilingual PDF document classification is an important problem that needs to be addressed owing to the ever increasing size of the electronic documents. This paper uses and compares different algorithms for the classification of multilingual documents. The Bi-LSTM algorithm produced accuracies consistently higher than other conventional algorithms like DNN and LSTM. The advantage of using Bi-LSTM is its ability to learn context in both the directions, which makes it more accurate than LSTM. Also Bi-LSTM requires very less computation requirements, hence it is used in this work. The proposed model has shown the best accuracy with an accuracy of 98.7%. In future, we can comparatively analyze results over much newer models keeping in mind low computing requirements.

# Chapter 8FNDetectorFake news detection using combinations of various features

Yun Hwa Wong (Jenny), Amanpreet Kaur, and Sangeeta Lal

DOI: [10.1201/9781003569619-8](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003569619-8)

## **8.1** Introduction

In the explosion era of technology and the modern world, fake news is permeating our daily lives at an alarming rate. The Cambridge dictionary has defined “fake news” as “false stories that appear to be news, spread on the internet or using other media, usually created to influence political views or as a joke” \[[1](#ch18.xhtml#r204)\]. According to the world’s leading public service broadcaster, British Broadcasting Corporation (BBC), the history of fake news started 2000 years ago during a civil war in the Roman Republic. Octavian wanted to win the war, and he disseminated the “fake news” against Mark Anthony by printing the slogan on the coins and distributing it to the public. In the 19th century, after the technique of print pressed was invented. One of the newspapers in 1835, articles were published about fantastic creatures such as flying batmen and unicorns living on the moon \[[2](#ch18.xhtml#r205)\].

Before the concept of online social media came, most of the people get news via reading newspapers or listening to the broadcast channel on the radio. With the characteristic of connecting people and sharing content easily, the rate of disseminating news has increased drastically via online social media.

A study conducted by \[[3](#ch18.xhtml#r206)\] on the Twitter dataset from the year 2006 to 2017 shows that between 1000 and 100000 people were affected by the top 1% of fake news while the truth was seldom spread to more than 1000 people. The study has shown how fast fake news can be disseminated on online social media.

Fake news is prevalent in many domains, especially in the domain of politics. During 2016 US presidential nomination process, the fake news about Donald Trump was greatly favored and has been shared extensively on social media. On Facebook, there were 115 fake news stories supporting Trump and 41 supporting Clinton \[[4](#ch18.xhtml#r207)\]. The possible impact of fake news disseminated on public forum has drawn up particular attention from different researchers and the public. Fake news, however, is not just about political issues. In recent years, fake news can be disseminated widely when a disaster or pandemic happens. The best example is the SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19) virus breakout that happened in the year 2019. There are tons of fake news or false information related to information on how to get recover from the disease such as eating hot pepper can help to prevent or cure COVID-19, and drinking alcohol can protect the public against COVID-19 \[[5](#ch18.xhtml#r208)\]. For example, fake news written as a Facebook post by a user regarding steaming can kill the Coronavirus and has been shared more than 33,000 times on Facebook \[[6](#ch18.xhtml#r209)\]. Researchers have put great deal of time and effort to develop different algorithms and models to detect fake news. A study of categorizing different approaches used by various researchers has been done by de Beer and Matthee \[[7](#ch18.xhtml#r210)\]. According to de Beer and Matthee \[[7](#ch18.xhtml#r210)\], the approaches used by existing researchers can be categorized into five main categories (refer to [Table 8.1](#ch18.xhtml#t13)). An example of each of the approaches can be found in [Table 8.1](#ch18.xhtml#t13).

**Table 8.1** Example or descriptions of each approach to identify fake news[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t13b)

 
| **Approaches** || **Examples/Descriptions** |
| --- | --- |
| Language approach Topic-agnostic approach Machine learning approach || Bag of Words, Semantic Analysis, Deep Syntax Using the linguistic features and web mark-up capabilities Deep learning neural network |
| --- | --- |
| Knowledge-based approach || Expert oriented, computational oriented and crowd sourcing oriented fact checking |
| Hybrid approach || Combination of different models or techniques |

Despite the myriad of methods and techniques proposed by researchers over the years to identify fake news, it remains the case that, as of now, no approach has achieved a level of detection with complete accuracy, to the best of our understanding. There are two main reasons:

Data challenge: Researchers can only use a few publicly available data sets, as far as we are aware, to develop and test their fake news detection models. Different data sets are suitable for different tasks. It is challenging to get the correct dataset such as the LIAR dataset introduced by Wang \[[8](#ch18.xhtml#r211)\] that fulfills the research’s requirements. However, these have limitations in terms of feature set availability and are not suitable for the work proposed in this paper.

Identification of an adequate learning model: The dataset and extracted feature have a significant influence on the learning model’s performance \[[9](#ch18.xhtml#r212)\]. Hence, it is important to carefully identify the features of a given data set. We have presented the FNDetector model. We used features from four domains: linguistic-based features, sentiment-based features, name entity recognition (NER)-based features, and textual features. We trained two variants of the FNDetector model, one using the Random Forest (RF) classifier and another using the long-term short memory (LSTM) classifier. We assessed the efficiency of the FNDetector model on Horne2017FakeNews Dataset. Experimental results show that FNDetector outperforms the baseline classifier significantly. The FNDetector model using the RF classifier outperforms the baseline classifier by 9.3% (F-measure) and 9.8% (accuracy) and FNDetector using the LSTM classifier outperformed the baseline classifier by 40.2% (F-measure) and 17.7% (accuracy). Following are the main research questions that we answered using this study.

*   RQ 1: How well the baseline classifier perform?
*   RQ 2: How well does the suggested model, FNDetector, perform in comparison to the baseline classifier?
*   RQ 3: How important to extract features from both title and contents of the article?
*   RQ 4: Which features are more important in the FNDetector model?
*   RQ 5: What is the consequence on FNDetector model’s efficiency if the parameters of RF and LSTM classifiers are changed?

This is how the chapter is structured. We introduced fake news in [Section 8.1](#ch18.xhtml#h1-039). We will provide background information about fake news in [Section 8.2](#ch18.xhtml#h1-040). [Section 8.3](#ch18.xhtml#h1-041) reveals the particulars of contribution this study makes to the field of original research and will address related work. In [Section 8.4](#ch18.xhtml#h1-042), we will give detailed information about the proposed FNDetector model. In [Sections 8.5](#ch18.xhtml#h1-043), [8.6](#ch18.xhtml#h1-044), and [8.7](#ch18.xhtml#h1-045), we will provide details related to dataset extraction, experimental setup, and metrics for assessment. [Section 8.8](#ch18.xhtml#h1-046) will provide motivation, approach, and the results obtained for each Research Question (RQ). In [Section 8.9](#ch18.xhtml#h1-047), conclude and future directions will be provided.

## **8.2** Background

Various forms of fake news can be circulated in our daily life. It is circulated in different formats such as videos, URLs, articles, etc. However, they are serving the same purpose and motivation which is the creator would like to get attention and unethical benefits from the public. Here, we will discuss different types of fake news that we encounter in our daily life and how the creator gets an advantage from it.

### **8.2.1** Clickbait

The clickbait can be represented by a link or thumbnail on online social media. It has become one of the dominant forms of medium to spread fake news on social media. The headline of clickbait is always misleading and can involve a curiosity gap from the public, gossip, or sensational aspect to attract the user to click on it \[[10](#ch18.xhtml#r213)\]. A survey that has been conducted among 53 Standford students found that 96.2% of them came across clickbait stories online at least once each day \[[11](#ch18.xhtml#r214)\]. The motivation for creating clickbait from the creator is to increase the number of websites visited and advertising avenues.

### **8.2.2** Propaganda

Propaganda is another form of disseminating fake news. Propaganda is usually used to distribute biased or misleading information that is related to political aspects or personal points of view before the election or protest happens in order to influence people’s opinion. To achieve the greatest impact, propaganda employs strategies of subtle psychological and linguistic \[[12](#ch18.xhtml#r215)\]. Due to the nature of propaganda, the heinous propaganda news sources have demonstrated their ability to have a significant influence. The power of propaganda and disinformation has been illustrated in recent events such as Brexit. Propaganda propagated on a notorious bus \[[13](#ch18.xhtml#r216)\] has become a significant representation of the Brexit referendum campaign \[[14](#ch18.xhtml#r217)\]. The false claim has been believed by half of the British public which has brought a negative impact during and after the referendum.

### **8.2.3** Satire and parody

The satire and parody is a humorous commentary on a work that calls for an imitation of the work or criticism of the world. The comic effect will be applied to both styles to entertain people. Although these do not usually classify as fake news, however, the information present in satire or parody might provide false information or mislead the public and bring a negative impact on the public’s decisions. Examples of the website of satire and parody include The Onion ([www.theonion.com/](http://www.theonion.com/)), The Daily Mash ([www.thedailymash.co.uk/](http://www.thedailymash.co.uk/)), etc.

In general, online social media and websites have become one of the dominant platforms for the public to get information. However, false information from social media or websites is difficult to distinguish from the public. Hence, the researchers have invested so much effort for identifying fake news.

## **8.3** Literature survey and research contribution

### **8.3.1** Literature survey

To train a model that supports high accuracy in detecting fake news, the dataset required for training the model has played an important role. It is challenging to find a reliable dataset to train a highly efficient model. Shu et al., performed a study on extracted features from different datasets \[[15](#ch18.xhtml#r218)\]. This extracted feature is separated into two different categories, i.e., news content and social context features. Linguistic-based and visual-based are feature representations types in news content features category. User-based, post-based, and network-based are categorized under social context features. The study discussed about extracted features from some popular datasets: BuzzFeedNews \[[16](#ch18.xhtml#r219)\], LIAR \[[17](#ch18.xhtml#r220)\], BS Detector \[[18](#ch18.xhtml#r221)\], and CREDBANK \[[19](#ch18.xhtml#r222)\]. The researchers have concluded that each of the datasets mentioned above has restricted some features from being extracted, for example: Only linguistic-based features can be extracted from the BuzzFeedNews dataset. The dataset is not supporting any existing feature extraction. Hence, to defeat the downside of existing dataset, the researchers have developed a new dataset, i.e., FakeNewsNet, to include all the news information and social context features.

Bhutani et al. \[[20](#ch18.xhtml#r223)\] used three different datasets: LIAR \[[17](#ch18.xhtml#r220)\], George McIntire7 \[[21](#ch18.xhtml#r224)\], and merged dataset (the combination of the dataset from Kaggle \[[22](#ch18.xhtml#r225)\], PolitiFact \[[23](#ch18.xhtml#r226)\], and the Emergent dataset). They applied different text preprocessing techniques such as Bi-grams, Tri-grams, CountVectorizer, etc on the datasets. Additional columns such as tf-idf scores, sentiments, and cosine similarity scores have been added to the merged dataset. Therefore, by extracting the sentiment features from the false news detection model, accuracy of fake news identification is improved.

Khan et al. \[[24](#ch18.xhtml#r227)\] and Iwendi et al. \[[25](#ch18.xhtml#r228)\] carried out the investigation on the importance of feature extraction. Both of the studies extracted the feature from the text like the count of lowercase characters, average word length, positive sentiment score, etc., before passing them to advanced machine learning algorithms (RF algorithms, deep learning algorithms). The prediction precision of fake news detection before feature extraction by means of a decision tree classifier was 67.81%. However, after applying feature extraction, the result has significantly improved by 10% giving 77.58% prediction accuracy. Ajao et al. \[[26](#ch18.xhtml#r229)\] included the sentiment element as one of the extracted features. The sentiment analysis used in the study was the emotional ratio score. The authors were using the latent semantic analysis and latent Dirichlet allocation to retrieve the emotional scores. As a result, the model which included emotional ratio gave better results than the existing extracted features that were used on the same dataset. Choudhary and Arora \[[9](#ch18.xhtml#r212)\] developed three models to experiment on the prediction precision of fake news detection. The final result of the experiment shows Model 3 based on all linguistic features has achieved better accuracy over the other two models (Model 1 based on grammatical, sentimental, and syntax-based features and Model 2 based on readability features) which achieved 84.52% average accuracy.

The above studies have shown how sentiment analysis could improve the average accuracy of detecting fake news. Besides the traditional features such as textual and environment features that can be extracted to train model, Reis et al. \[[27](#ch18.xhtml#r230)\] has introduced an extra new feature named as domain localization feature to improve model. The domain localization feature can include IP, city, country, latitude, and longitude extracted from the news website URL. By including the additional feature, the fake news detection model developed by the author manage to detect nearly all the fake news in their dataset.

Although the importance of feature extraction has brought up the attention of the researchers, however, the classification approaches used to build the fake news detection model are a force to be reckoned with. Khanam et al. \[[28](#ch18.xhtml#r231)\] has performed a study on adopting different traditional classification algorithms for fake news. XGboost outperformed with an accuracy greater than 75%. Besides the traditional classification algorithm, the researchers such as \[[29](#ch18.xhtml#r232)\] applied distinct the deep learning model to the dataset for comparison. As a result, most deep learning models gave superior performance than traditional classification algorithm. Though Naive Bayes is a commonly used method, it has great potential in detecting fake news, nearly matching the efficacy of deep learning models. On top of traditional and deep learning models, Khan et al. \[[30](#ch18.xhtml#r233)\] has conducted a benchmark research on various machine learning models including the pretrained model such as BERT \[[31](#ch18.xhtml#r234)\]. Result of pretrained BERT-based model was better than other models.

Furthermore, the researcher also tried to propose the latest technology such as blockchain as a solution \[[32](#ch18.xhtml#r235)\]. There are a few challenges and limitations in adopting blockchain technology in fake news detection but the author believed that in the near future, detecting fake news would be much easier with the proposed strategy. Moreover, some existing libraries have been built, and the Flair library \[[33](#ch18.xhtml#r236)\] is one of them. Flair allows the user to combine different kinds of word embedding such as BERT embedding, ELMo transformer embedding, etc \[[34](#ch18.xhtml#r237)\]. Researcher in \[[35](#ch18.xhtml#r238)\] has used the Flair library for building model. The study found out that by using the Flair library with a deep learning algorithm, the accuracy of detecting true and false information was up to 99.8%.

The study of distinguishing between fake news, real news, and satire was performed by Horne and Adali \[[36](#ch18.xhtml#r239)\]. The researcher has used three different datasets including the Buzzfeed election data set, Burfoot and Baldwin data set, and random political news data set that was created by the researcher from different sources. Three main features: psychological, stylistic, and complexity features were extracted from the dataset and run on the SVM model with a linear kernel. Result shows that accuracy of separating fake and real news from body text of the article was 71% on the random political news dataset and 77% on the Buzzfeed election dataset.

Balshetwar et al. \[[37](#ch18.xhtml#r240)\] worked on detecting fake news and analyzing sentimental behavior present in the text. They used sentiment present in the text to cues of emotional signals. They tested the proposed model on the ISOT and LIAR datasets. The projected approach achieved precision of 99.8%.

Uppada et al. \[[38](#ch18.xhtml#r241)\] proposed a model that uses for text and images. They used a fine-tuned BERT model for text processing. Their proposed fusion model gave an accuracy of 93%.

### **8.3.2** Research contributions

We proposed the novel fake news detection model, i.e., FNDetector, using four different types of features (linguistic-based, sentiment-based, NER-based, and textual). We extracted these features from both title and content of the news. We present the results of an in-depth empirical analysis of the FNDetector model on a freely accessible dataset.

**Algorithm 1 FNDetector Algorithm**

## **8.4** Methodology

[Figure 8.1](#ch18.xhtml#f45) presents the overview diagram, and algorithms 1 and 2 present the detailed steps of the proposed FNDetector model creation. For details about the notations utilized in the Algorithm 1 and Algorithm 2 refer to the [Figure 8.2](#ch18.xhtml#f46). FNDetector comprises two phases: model building and prediction. In phase 1, we train the model using the training data (step 1 to step 4). In stage 2, we use the FNDetector model to predict fake news (step 5). Following we describe all the steps shown in [Figure 8.1](#ch18.xhtml#f45) in detail:

**Figure 8.1** Overview diagram of the proposed FNDetector model.[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#f45b)

![Figure 8.2 Notations used in the FNDetector algorithm (1) and feature extraction (algorithm 2).⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C008_002.jpg)
