### **8.4.1** Training dataset collection

The experimental dataset is split into a test dataset and a training dataset. We use 80%–20% training and test split. We create models with a training dataset, and we evaluate model’s performance with a test dataset. Prior to training any algorithm, the quality of the dataset must be guaranteed in order to develop a high-performance false news detection model. We load the dataset into a data frame with Pandas library. We shuffle the data before model creation to make sure the dataset is mixing up and not sticking to certain patterns according to the sorted order. We make sure that the dataset does not consist of any null values.

### **8.4.2** Attribute selection (step 1)

We selected the title and content of news articles for model creation (refer to step 5 in Algorithm 1). We selected both title and content of news articles because we hypothesize that both title and content consist of important information regarding the news and including both of these attributes can be beneficial for separating fake news from real news.

### **8.4.3** Preprocessing (step 2)

Lemmatization and stemming are popular preprocessing techniques. These techniques were utilized to enhance the performance of document retrieval precision \[[39](#ch18.xhtml#r242)\]. According to Manning et al. \[[40](#ch18.xhtml#r243)\], stemming is a rudimentary heuristic process that removes the end of the word and derivational affixes from the word to achieve the goal. Compared to stemming, lemmatization is the process of employing vocabulary and morphological analysis to eliminate a word’s inflectional endings and return the word to its base or dictionary form. [Table 8.2](#ch18.xhtml#t14) shows the example of stemming and lemmatization.

**Table 8.2** Example of stemming and lemmatization[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t14b)

 
| Type || Before processed || After processed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Stemming || Studies, Wander News || Studies, Wand New |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lemmatization || Studies. Running breathed || Study, run breath |

In our work, we apply lemmatization to both title and content of the news articles. We did not apply stemming techniques in our dataset as lemmatization achieves improved accuracy and result in comparison to stemming which the result has been shown in the study \[[39](#ch18.xhtml#r242)\]. However, the default behavior of lemmatization that changes the word’s base to a noun might bring the possibility of reducing the accuracy of our final result outcome. Hence, to minimize the mistake that was brought by default lemmatization, we combine the part-of-speech (POS) tagging and lemmatization in preprocessing the news title and content (refer to step 6 of Algorithm 1). The POS tag, adjective, noun, verb, and adjective are used to identify the specific text in the dataset. So, when the lemmatizer detected a word in the verb from the text, instead of changing it to a noun by default, it will return the base format of the verb. In this work, we are using the lemmatizer from Wordnet \[[41](#ch18.xhtml#r244)\]. Wordnet is a huge and publicly accessible English lexical database that is used to create organized semantic associations between words. [Table 8.3](#ch18.xhtml#t15) shows some of the examples from the dataset after lemmatization.

**Table 8.3** Example of lemmatization from dataset[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t15b)

 
| Before lemmatization || After lemmatization |
| --- | --- |
| German prosecutors have closed an investigation into suspected mass phone tapping of German citizens by British and U.S. spies after finding no concrete indication of any criminal activity, they said on Thursday || German prosecutors have closed an investigation into suspected mass phone tapping of German citizen by British and U.S. spy after finding no concrete indication of any criminal activity, they said on Thursday |
| --- | --- |

### **8.4.4** Feature extraction (step 3)

As we mentioned in Section 8.3, feature extraction is crucial to create a high-accuracy model. We extracted features from four different domains. We believe that features extracted from different domains can be beneficial in capturing different aspects of the news, and hence, can aid in improving accuracy (refer to step 7 of Algorithm 1). We extracted 32 features to train the FNDetector model.

### **8.4.5** Linguistic-based feature

The linguistic-based features are used to categorize the word or phoneme specified in particular pillars such as syntax, semantics, morphology etc. According to Mahyoob et al. \[[42](#ch18.xhtml#r245)\], words play a significant part in news and the style of writing the news’s contents brings a huge impact on the public. Conroy et al. \[[43](#ch18.xhtml#r246)\] has mentioned that even though the creator of fake news tries to keep their speech under control, there will still have some verbal “leakage” that is difficult to be controlled such as the pronoun frequencies, use of verbs, conjunction patterns etc. Hence, to detect the “leakage” or “predictive deception indicators” in message content, we need to extract the linguistic-based feature from the dataset. There are two linguistic features we will be focusing on which are grammatical feature and lexical feature:

Grammatical-based feature refers to the structure of the sentences which consists of syntax, morphology, phonology, and semantics. In this project, we extracted the count of noun, verb, adjective, pronoun, and adverb from news headline and body from the dataset. To extract the grammatical feature, we used the concept of POS tagging. POS tagging is also known as grammatical tagging which each of the word sequences in a sentence will be automatic assign or tagged with a grammatical classification which normally contains a verb, adjective, pronoun, noun, etc \[[44](#ch18.xhtml#r247)\]. Tagging each of the words with different grammar features in a large dataset is tedious and time-consuming. Hence, we took the help of the Natural Language Toolkit (NTLK) to perform the POS tagging.

Lexical-based feature is another type of linguistic-based feature. Lexical is referring the relationship between a word or vocabulary of a language. In our project, we focused on the lexical frequency where the frequencies of the text with correspondences occur in a large corpus such as the number of stopwords like “the”, “is”, etc appear in the title or contents of the article. Besides, we also calculated the count of words in each article.

[Table 8.4](#ch18.xhtml#t16) provide the details of all the 14 linguistic-based features extracted in this study.

**Table 8.4** Details of the Linguistic-based features extracted[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t16b)

 
| Feature’s name || Data type || Feature domain || Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| noun content || N || Grammatical-based || Count of nouns in content |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| noun title || N || Grammatical-based || Count of nouns in title |
| pronoun content || N || Grammatical-based || Count of pronouns in content |
| pronoun title || N || Grammatical-based || Count of pronouns in title |
| adverb content || N || Grammatical-based || Count of adverbs in content |
| adverb title || N || Grammatical-based || Count of adverbs in title |
| verb content || N || Grammatical-based || Count of verbs in content |
| verb title || N || Grammatical-based || Count of verbs in title |
| adj content || N || Grammatical-based || Count of adjectives in content |
| adj title || N || Grammatical-based || Count of adjectives in title |
| totalWord content || N || Lexical-based || Count of words in content |
| totalWord title || N || Lexical-based || Count of words in title |
| totalStopWords content || N || Lexical-based || Count of stopwords in content |
| totalStopWords title || N || Lexical-based || Count of stopwords in title |

Note: N, numeric.

### **8.4.6** Sentiment-based feature

Sentiment analysis is found to be useful in identifying suspicious accounts from online social media by extracting the element of feelings, attitudes, and opinions \[[45](#ch18.xhtml#r248)\]. It is also found useful in fake news detection when combined with other extracted features \[[26](#ch18.xhtml#r229)\]. This happens because fake news often is biased toward one type of emotion. When people are experiencing positive or neutral emotions, they are more inclined to believe a claim to be “true,” yet when they are experiencing negative emotions, they may become more skeptical \[[46](#ch18.xhtml#r249)\].

Sentiment polarity is one of the important elements in sentiment analysis. It helps to determine how the stated sentiment is oriented. For example, it can help to identify if the text is expressed in negative, neural sentiment, or negative from a statement. In this project, we extracted the polarity scores as one of the features using the sentiment analyzer from the natural language toolkit (NLTK) package \[[47](#ch18.xhtml#r250)\]. There are four different scores in this package which are positive, negative, neural, and compound score. The polarity score of each score can be ranged from −1 to +1 and the add-up of positive, negative, and neural scores will equal 1. The compound score is total of positive, negative, and neural scores with the range of most extreme negative (−1) to most extreme positive (+1). The more positive the text is, the closer the compound score is to +1. We extract 8 sentiment-based features. [Table 8.5](#ch18.xhtml#t17) shows the details of the sentiment-based features.

**Table 8.5** Details of the sentiment-based features extracted[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t17b)

 
| **Feature’sName** || **Data**  **type** || **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| neg content || N || Count of negative sentiment in content. Neg |
| --- | --- | --- |
| title || N || Count of negative sentiment in title. Pos |
| content || N || Count of positive sentiment in content. Pos |
| title || N || Count of positive sentiment in title. Neu |
| content || N || Count of neural sentiment in content. Neu |
| title || N || Count of neural sentiment in title |
| compound content || N || The compound score of sentiment in content |
| compound title || N || The compound score of sentiment in title |

Note: N, numeric.

### **8.4.7** Named entity recognition (NER)-based features

NER is a procedure used to identify and classify the designated entities that are presented in a document. According to Shelar et al. \[[48](#ch18.xhtml#r251)\], the NER tool usually scans through the whole text document and categorizes the entity such as place, organizations, date, major people and etc. It allows for easy content discovery by categorizing the articles in defined hierarchies with the known relevant tags for each text document. With the help of the NER approach, we can identify the relationship between the entities and the truth of news or articles. For example, when an election happens, fake news is tended to have major people’s names as headlines to attract the public. In this work, we are using the trained model “en core web sm” (an English pipeline that includes vocabulary, syntax, and entities with training on written web text documents such as comments, news, and blogs) from the Sparcy library. [Table 8.](#ch18.xhtml#t18)6 shows the example of entities of the NER approach in the trained model. A study from \[[3](#ch18.xhtml#r206)\] has shown that most of the fake news is related to political news as the false information or political rumors on Twitter spiked during the 2012 and 2016 US presidential elections. This has motivated us to focus on the entities that are more relevant to political news such as organization (ORG), the major person (PERSON), geopolitical (GPE), and money (MONEY) while developing the fake news detection model. We extract eight NER features. [Table 8.](#ch18.xhtml#t19)7 shows the details of all the NER-based features extracted.

**Table 8.6** Example of named entities in Sparcy library[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t18b)

 
| Entities || Examples |
| --- | --- |
| ORGANISATION || Apple, Tesla, Amazon |
| --- | --- |
| PERSON || Donald Trump, Elon Musk |
| MONEY || 1 billion dollar, 2 pound |
| GPE || India, Germany |
| LOCATION || Mount Titlis, Yellow River |
| DATE || 5 JUL, 24 January 2022 |
| TIME || two-forty am, 11.09 P.M |

**Table 8.7** Details of the NER-based features extracted[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t19b)

 
| Feature’s Name || Data  type || Feature Domain || Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| person content || N || NER || Count of person in content |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| content. person title || N || NER || Count of person in title |
| org content || N || NER || Count of organisation in content. org |
| content. org title || N || NER || Count of organisation in title. gpe |
| title. gpe content || N || NER || Count of geopolitical in content. gpe |
| content. gpe title || N || NER || Count of geopolitical in title. money |
| money content || N || NER || Count of money in content. money |
| money title || N || NER || Count of money in title |

Note: N, numeric.

### **8.4.8** Textual features

We have also used textual features in the FNDetector model. We use lemmatized title (with POS) and lemmatized content (with POS) (refer to [Table 8.8](#ch18.xhtml#t20)). However, because textual features cannot be directly fed into the classifiers. We used Term Frequency – Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF) (RF classifier) and tokenizer (LSTM classifier). Following are more details about TF-IDF and Tokenizer. TF-IDF: According to Qaiser and Ali \[[49](#ch18.xhtml#r252)\], the term frequency (TF) is referring the occurrence of a particular term in a document. To calculate the inverse document frequency of a particular term, we can use the following formula:

$\\text{TF} = \\frac{\\text{Occurrence~of~Words}}{\\text{Count~of~words~of~document}}$(8.1) 

For example: if a 2000 words document contains the word “library” 5 times. The TF of the word “library” will be 5/2000, which is 0.0025. All the terms play an important role in a document. Inverse document frequency (IDF) aims to assign a weight to a particular term. The IDF algorithm assigns a more weightage to an infrequent term and a lower weightage to a frequent term in the document. To calculate the IDF of a particular term, we can use the following formula:

  
$$\\begin{array}{l} {\\text{Inverse~Document~Frequency}\\left( \\text{IDF} \\right)} \\\\ {\\text{~~~} = \\log\_{e}\\frac{\\text{Total~number~of~document}}{\\text{Number~of~documents~with~term}}} \\\\ \\end{array}$$  
(8.2) 

For example, if we have 20 documents and the term “library” appears in three documents, the IDF of the term will be loge(20/3) = 1.897. In order to address issues like stopwords that are highly prevalent in documents but have limited significance for text mining, the researchers have suggested merging TD and IDF computations to represent a term’s relative importance to a document within the collection. The Term Frequency – Index Document Frequency (TD-IDF) will assign higher importance to the term that has higher frequency but less occurrence in the total number of documents. To calculate TD-IDF, it is just simply taken the multiplication of TD and IDF. For example, to calculate the term “library” in the collection, we will multiply 0.0025 and 1.897 which is 0.00474.

**Table 8.8** Details of the textual features extracted[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t20b)

 
| Feature’s Name || Data type || Feature Domain || Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| title\_lem || T || Textual || The lemmatized (with POS) title of articles and news |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| content\_lem || T || Textual || The lemmatized (with POS) content of articles and news |

Note: T, textual.

The concept of TD-IDF is playing important role in training the fake news detection model. By using the concept of TD-IDF, we can analyze what are the common terms being used in fake news to attract the public. In our work, the TD-IDF will be applied to the lemmatized title and the content of the articles. To implement the TD-IDF feature in the dataset, the TfidfVectorizer in the scikit-learn package \[[50](#ch18.xhtml#r253)\] (a machine learning library) is being used.

Tokenizer: The TfidfVectorizer from the scikit-learn package will return the result as a sparse matrix. However, LSTM classifier gives better performance when the sequence is used as input. Hence, for the LSTM classifier, we vectorized heading and body of news article by turning each text into a sequence of integers using Tokenizer \[[51](#ch18.xhtml#r254)\] (from the TensorFlow package).

### **8.4.9** FNDetector classifier learning (Step 4)

We combine all the features, i.e., linguistics-based, sentiment-based, NER-based, and textual features. Using this, we obtain a total of 32 features. Using these we train two versions of the FNDetector model, one using the RF classifier (FNDetector[RF]) and the other using the LSTM classifier (FNDetector[LSTM]). The configuration related details of both the algorithms are shown in [Table 8.9](#ch18.xhtml#t21). Following are the details of both of the algorithms:

**Table 8.9** The configuration details of RF and LSTM classifiers used in this work[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t21b)

 
| Method || Setting |
| --- | --- |
| RF Classifier || n estimators = 100 |
| --- | --- |
| LSTM || epochs = 10 |

Random Forest (RF) Classifier: Breiman et al. \[[52](#ch18.xhtml#r255)\] first presented the RF method, an ensemble learning approach for regression trees and classification. A collection of classifiers with tree structures make up the RF classifier, and each tree votes for the class that is most popular.

Compared to other machine learning algorithms, the random forest doesn’t consist of overfitting problems due to the use of the Strong Law of Large Numbers. The core method for the random forest to improve its accuracy was using the bagging method (also known as Bootstrap aggregating \[[53](#ch18.xhtml#r256)\]. Besides, the out-of-bag method was used to estimate the strength, correlation, and generalization error. Furthermore, based on the strength of each predictor and their correlations, the algorithm offers information about the random forest’s prediction ability.

According to Breiman \[[52](#ch18.xhtml#r255)\], the random forest provides significant improvement in classification from the concept of expanding the collection of trees and letting them choose the most popular class.

[Figure 8.3](#ch18.xhtml#f47) describes RF algorithm. Taking into account that the training dataset includes balls of various hues, including pink, green, blue, yellow, and red.

![Figure 8.3 An illustrative example of RF algorithm.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C008_003.jpg)

**Figure 8.3** An illustrative example of RF algorithm.[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#f47b)

The dataset will be divided into subsets and assigned to one decision tree in the RF algorithm. Each of the decision trees produces one output. For example, decision tree 1 and 2 produces yellow ball as their output, and tree n produces blue ball as their output. Then, the random forest algorithm will collect the majority voting and produce the final prediction. In our example, most of the tree has selected the yellow ball as their prediction output. Hence, the final prediction of RF will be the yellow ball.

Many studies such as \[[54](#ch18.xhtml#r257)\] have shown that RF is among the machine learning methods with the highest performance, particularly for prediction tasks. The RF algorithm was formed by a tree-based model. The random forest algorithm is being used widely across different areas such as stock price prediction in the financial industry \[[55](#ch18.xhtml#r258)\]. There are also lot of researchers who had used the random forest as the classifier to train fake news detection model as mentioned in Section 8.3. In our work, we tend to use different extracted features with RF algorithms to experiment with prediction accuracy.

Long-short term memory neural network (LSTM): The pioneer of the LSTM is the recurrent neural network (RNN) that utilizes consecutive or time series data. The RNN is often used in time series forecasting such as forecasting the next twelve-month energetic usage of a household. According to Hochreiter and Schmidhuber \[[56](#ch18.xhtml#r259)\], the existing RNN causes the vanishing and exploding of gradients when the error signals are flowing backward. To overcome this issue, Hochreiter and Schmidhuber \[[56](#ch18.xhtml#r259)\] has introduced the concept of LSTM. The LSTM was formed by four core elements which are forget gate, input gate, output gate, and a complex unit called memory cell \[[57](#ch18.xhtml#r260)\]. Different from other traditional deep neural networks, the memory cell in the LSTM algorithm has the center linear unit with fixed self-connection. It goes along the whole chain and makes the selection on which information needs to be stored with the help of three gates \[[56](#ch18.xhtml#r259)\]. This process allowed the LSTM network to identify long-term dependencies and memorize multiple time dependencies with different characteristics \[[58](#ch18.xhtml#r261)\].

In our work, we have created the LSTM neural network to train the model. Two embedding layers are created to store the information of the title and content of the article. Then, two LSTM layers with 128 neurons are created to hold the output of embedding layers. A concatenate layer is created to concatenate the output of the two LSTM layers. Furthermore, a dense layer (a layer of neurons that receive the input from the previous layer) is created to process the other numeric features. Another concatenate layer is created to process outcomes from previous two layers before going into another dense layer. Using the input from former layer, the final output layer produce one result as the end product.

The overall LSTM neural network model for our project can be viewed in [Figure 8.4](#ch18.xhtml#f48). We used the sigmoid function as an activation method for our final output layer. This helps network to learn which data needs to be forgotten or remembered by holding the value between 0 and 1. The information will be forgotten if the value is 0 and stays if the value is 1. Since our final result has only two outcomes which are true or false news, we used binary cross entropy function for providing better performance for the binary classification model.

![Figure 8.4 The LSTM neural network.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C008_004.jpg)

**Figure 8.4** The LSTM neural network.[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#f48b)

To perform the experiment using LSTM and answer the research questions in Section 8.8, the maximum number of most frequent words have been defined to 3000 words and the maximum number of words in each title or contents of the article to 300 words. Then, the title and contents of the article will be tokenized by Tokenizer. Three different inputs with the shape of 300 (for the title of the article), 300 (for the contents of the article), and 30 (the other 30 numeric features) will be created for model training. For all the LSTM classifiers, we have trained them with 10 epochs.

To make sure the training model in all experiments presented in Section 8.8 are consistent, we have defined the basic setting for the random forest and LSTM neural network classifier which is shown in the following table:

### **8.4.10** Prediction phase (step 5)

In this phase, we applied the FNDetector model to guess the label of news articles available in the test dataset. In order to accomplish this, we extract all 32 characteristics after extracting the news items’ titles and contents from the test dataset. To determine whether or not the news article is phony, we feed these features into the FNDetector model.

## **8.5** Dataset extraction

In this work, we use Horne201 FakeNews Data \[[36](#ch18.xhtml#r239)\]. The particulars about given dataset are shown in [Table 8.10](#ch18.xhtml#t22). There are two popular news datasets: Buzzfeed news and random political news. The first Buzzfeed news dataset contains 48 fake news and 53 real news, whereas the random political news dataset contains 75 fake news and 75 real news, respectively. These datasets contain two pieces of information which are the title and content of news. We downloaded the dataset from the repository [https://github.com/rpitrust/fakenewsdata1](https://github.com/rpitrust/fakenewsdata1).

## **8.6** Experimental setup

We use Python \[[59](#ch18.xhtml#r262)\] for programming. The libraries and their associated process applied for our work are mentioned below:

*   Framework: The algorithm was modeled using Jupyter Notebook for Python 3.
*   Preprocessing: wordnet from nltk library, tf-idf vectorizer from sklearn library, pandas for data frame.
*   Machine learning models: Random Forest classifier from sklearn library.
*   Deep learning model: LSTM neural network from Keras.
*   Features extraction: sklearn, nltk, spacy library.
*   NLP: nltk library.
*   Visualization: matplotlib, seaborn libraries.

## **8.7** Evaluation metrics

To make sure the classification or prediction model is behaving correctly and achieving the goal, a set of checklists will be applied to it to perform the measurement which is known as evaluation metrics. The performance and efficacy of classifiers can be assessed using evaluation metrics \[[60](#ch18.xhtml#r263)\]. Several factors need to be considered when choosing the correct evaluation metric such as less complexity and less computational cost, the issue of multiclass problems, informativeness, distinctiveness, and discriminable \[[61](#ch18.xhtml#r264)\]. To ensure a classification model has high efficiency and high performance, it needs to make sure the predicted outcome is the same as the true outcome. The problem discussed in this paper is a binary classification problem. The evaluation metric applied for binary classification problems is the confusion metric which also belongs to the threshold types of evaluation metrics \[[61](#ch18.xhtml#r264)\].

### **8.7.1** Confusion metric

The confusion metric is presented in a tabular format. It contains information about the actual and predicted class that is done by a classification model. There are four types of indicators in the confusion metric which are True Positive (TP), True Negative (TN), False Positive (FP), and False Negative (FN) \[[61](#ch18.xhtml#r264)\].

The confusion metric provides the visualization to the researchers on the indicators so they can take further action on improving the classification model if necessary. [Figure 8.5](#ch18.xhtml#f49) shows how a confusion metric of random forest classifier. The *x*\-axis and *y*\-axis signify predicted values and real values, respectively. The darker the color in the box of the confusion metric, the higher the values.

![Figure 8.5 The example of confusion metric of Random Forest classifier.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C008_005.jpg)

**Figure 8.5** The example of confusion metric of Random Forest classifier.[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#f49b)

### **8.7.2** Accuracy

The range of accuracy scores is between 0 and 1. In general, the accuracy score calculated the percentage of exact predictions over the total number of samples that are assessed \[[61](#ch18.xhtml#r264)\]. The equation is listed below:

$\\text{Accuracy} = \\frac{\\text{TP} + \\text{TN}}{\\text{TP} + \\text{TN} + \\text{FP} + \\text{FN}}$(8.3) 

### **8.7.3** Precision, Recall, and F-Measure

Accuracy score is not a great evaluation metric to judge the performance of a classification model, as it provides a less distinctive value \[[61](#ch18.xhtml#r264)\]. The accuracy score might not be accurate when the dataset is imbalanced. To overcome the limitation of the accuracy score, we can calculate the precision score of the classification model. The concept of calculating a precision score is using the sample that is precisely predicted from the entire predicted samples in a positive class \[[61](#ch18.xhtml#r264)\]. Different from precision, the recall score can also be used to calculate the number of positive classes that are correctly classified out of all positive examples in the dataset. The harmonic mean of precision and recall is the f-measure (F1 score). Joshi \[[62](#ch18.xhtml#r265)\] has mentioned that although the f-measure is less popular being used as the performance metric; however, it performs better than the accuracy score when come to optimizing the classifier for binary classification problems. The range of f-measure can be between 0 and 1. The formulas for computing these measures are listed below: $\\text{Precision}\\left( p \\right) = \\frac{\\text{TP}}{\\text{TP} + \\text{FP}}$(8.4)

$\\text{Recall}\\left( r \\right) = \\frac{\\text{TP}}{\\text{TP} + \\text{TN}}$(8.5) $F\\text{~measure} = \\frac{2\\text{\*}p\\text{\*}r}{p + r}$(8.6) 

### **8.7.4** Receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve and area under ROC (AUC)

The receiver operating characteristic (ROC) has been widely used as a performance measurement for evaluating medical diagnostic systems \[[63](#ch18.xhtml#r266)\]. According to Park et al. \[[64](#ch18.xhtml#r267)\], the ROC is represented as a graph that consists of two key components which are sensitivity (or true positive rate) and specificity (or false positive rate), and it can differentiate the signal from the noise. In a ROC graph, the sensitivity will be plotted on the *y*\-axis and specificity on the *x*\-axis, respectively. The ROC graph can clearly show the relationship between the false positive rate and the true positive rate. The extension of the ROC curve, area under the ROC curve (AUC) has been proved as a useful metric for summarizing the efficacy of the test \[[65](#ch18.xhtml#r268)\]. Mandrekar \[[65](#ch18.xhtml#r268)\] also mentioned that the value of AUC can be ranged from 0 to 1, where 0 indicates the test was perfectly inaccurate and 1 indicates that test is perfectly accurate. In general, when the AUC is greater than 0.5, there is a high chance that the classifier can differentiate the positive class value from the negative class value. However, there are some cases where the AUC value is equal to 0.5, and the ROC curve is presented as the diagonal line on the graph. In this case, the classifier will not have the ability to differentiate between the positive and negative classes.

## **8.8** Experimental results

Here, we provide the details of experimental results.

RQ1: What is the performance of the baseline classifier?

Motivation: We worked on building a fake news detection model using different types of features. It is significant to recognize how the fake news detection model performs without using any of the external feature extraction. Hence, in this RQ, we built our baseline classifier using the title and the content of news articles.

Approach: We used the textual data, i.e., the lemmatized title and content of the news articles to build the baseline classifiers. We then vectorize the dataset by using TfidfVectorizer before training the RF classifier model. For the LSTM classifier, we tokenize the textual data and insert it into an embedding layer before going to the LSTM layer. For this experiment, we use 100 n estimators for RF and 10 epochs for LSTM.

Results: [Figure 8.6](#ch18.xhtml#f50) and [Table 8.11](#ch18.xhtml#t23) show the results of this RQ. We obtained an accuracy value of 60.7% and 78.4% for RF and LSTM classifiers, respectively, and an F-measure of 79.2% and 41.1% for RF and LSTM classifiers, respectively (refer to Table 8.10). These results indicate that the baseline classifier gives very low accuracy and F-measure with respect to fake news detection, and hence, there is a need to improve these models. The baseline classifiers perform poorly for fake news detection tasks with RF and LSTM classifiers giving an accuracy of 60.7% and 78.4%, respectively. The performance of the LSTM classifier is much lower than the RF classifier both in terms of accuracy and F-measure.

![Figure 8.6 The performance of the baseline classifier.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C008_006.jpg)

**Figure 8.6** The performance of the baseline classifier.[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#f50b)

**Table 8.11** The performance of baseline classifiers[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t23b)

 
| Method || Accuracy (%) || Precision (%) || Recall (%) || F-Measure (%) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Baseline[RF] || 78.4 || 80.8 || 77.8 || 79.2 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Baseline[LSTM] || 60.7 || 100 || 25.9 || 41.1 |

RQ 2: What is the performance of the proposed model, i.e., FNDetector, when contrasted to the baseline classifier?

Motivation: In this RQ, we investigated the performance of the proposed FNDetector model. To build this model, we combined different types of features. Additionally, we extracted features from both the title and content of news articles. This RQ will help in providing insights into the effectiveness of the FNDetector model.

Approach: To answer this RQ, we trained the FNDetector model using RF and LSTM classifiers to build two variants of the FNDetector model, i.e., FNDetector[RF] and FNDetector[LSTM] . We tested the performance of these two models on the test dataset and computed various metrics. We also performed a comparison of these two models with the baseline classifiers.

Result: [Table 8.12](#ch18.xhtml#t24) and [Figure 8.7](#ch18.xhtml#f51) show the prediction results of the FNDetector[RF] and FNDetector[LSTM] models. [Table 8.12](#ch18.xhtml#t24) shows that FNDetector[RF] gives an accuracy of 88.2% and an F-measure of 88.5%, whereas FNDetector[LSTM] gives an accuracy of 78.4% and an F-measure of 81.3%. [Figures 8.8](#ch18.xhtml#f52) and [8.10](#ch18.xhtml#f54) show the comparison of the performance of the baseline and FNDetector classifiers. [Figures 8.9](#ch18.xhtml#f53) and [8.10](#ch18.xhtml#f54) show that both FNDetector[RF] and FNDetector[LSTM] models outperform the baseline classifiers in both accuracy and F-measure. We compute the improvements and present the results in the form of bar graphs (refer to [Figures 8.10](#ch18.xhtml#f54) and [8.11](#ch18.xhtml#f55)). We notice an improvement of 9.8% (accuracy) and 9.3% (F-measure) using FNDetector[RF] model and an improvement of 17.7% (accuracy) and 40.2% (F-measure) using the FNDetector[LSTM] model. These results indicate that feature extraction is an important step in fake news model creation. From the result, we can conclude that feature extraction is useful in fake news model building for both random forest and LSTM classifiers. We obtained an improvement of 9.8% (FNDetector[RF]) and 17.7% (FNDetector[LSTM]) in terms of accuracy and an improvement of 9.3% (FNDetector[RF]) and 40.2% (FNDetector[LSTM]) in F-measure.

RQ 3: How important to extract features from both the title and contents of the article?

Motivation: Most of the researchers have focused on analyzing the headlines of the news articles for fake news model creation such as \[[66](#ch18.xhtml#r269)–[68](#ch18.xhtml#r271)\]. Some of the datasets that are used for building a fake news detection model such as the LIAR dataset \[[69](#ch18.xhtml#r272)\]) do not contain both the title and contents of the news articles in the dataset. The dataset only contains the statement extracted from both the title and contents of the article. We believe that the content of news can provide important information for fake news detection. Thus, in this RQ, we work on analyzing the importance of both titles and contents for detecting fake news. The result of this RQ can help us understand which part of the news is more important in fake news detection. Approach: To answer this RQ, we build three variants of the FNDetector model: FNDetector (title), FNDetector (content) and FNDetector (title+content). FNDetector (title), model is trained using only the title of the news articles. FNDetector (content), is trained using the content of the news articles. FNDetector (title+content) is trained using both title and content of the news articles which is the original FNDetector model is proposed in this work. We train all these variants using both RF and LSTM classifiers, hence, create a total of six variants of the FNDetector model.

Results: [Tables 8.13](#ch18.xhtml#t25), [8.14](#ch18.xhtml#t26), and [8.15](#ch18.xhtml#t27) show the results of this RQ using both RF and LSTM classifiers. [Figures 8.12](#ch18.xhtml#f56) and [8.13](#ch18.xhtml#f57) show the comparison of the performance between different variants for FNDetector[RF] and FNDetector[LSTM] models, respectively. [Table 8.13](#ch18.xhtml#t25) shows that the accuracy and F-measure using FNDetector[RF] (title) model is 70.6% and 72.7%, respectively. The FNDetector[LSTM] (title) gives accuracy and F-measure of 74.5% and 76.4%, respectively. We observe from [Table 8.16](#ch18.xhtml#t28) that the performance of the FNDetector[RF] model is improved when we use “content” of news article as compared to when we use “title” for training the model. We observe an improvement of 11.8% (accuracy) and 10.3% (F-measure) when using FNDetector[RF] (content) as compared to the FNDetectorRF (title) model. For the LSTM classifier, we observe a reduction in the performance in both accuracy and F-measure when the “content” of news articles is used to train the classifier (refer to [Table 8.17](#ch18.xhtml#t29)).

This is also the original FNDetector model proposed in the work. [Figures 8.12](#ch18.xhtml#f56) and [8.13](#ch18.xhtml#f57) show a comparison of the performances of the three variants for FNDetector[RF] and FNDetector[LSTM] models, respectively. We notice an improvement in the performances of both FNDetector[RF] and FNDetector[LSTM] models. Using FNDetector[RF] (title + content), we notice an improvement of 17.6% (in comparison to FNDetector[RF] (title)) and 5.8% (in comparison to FNDetector[RF] (content)) in accuracy. Similarly, using FNDetector[LSTM] (title+content), we notice an improvement of 3.93% (in comparison to FNDetector[LSTM] (title)) and 19.63% (in comparison to FNDetector[LSTM] (content)) in accuracy. The improvements are also shown in [Tables 8.16](#ch18.xhtml#t28) and [8.17](#ch18.xhtml#t29). Results indicate that both the title and content of news articles are important in fake news detection. For both FNDetector[RF] and FNDetector[LSTM], we observed a significant improvement in the prediction accuracy when both title and content are used to train the model.

RQ 4: Which features are more important to detect fake news in the FNDetector model?Motivation: We use 32 features to train the FNDetector model. These features belong to different domains and have different types, i.e., linguistic-based features (grammatical and lexical features), sentiment-based features, named entity recognition features, and textual features. The FNDetector model outdid the baseline line classifier significantly in both accuracy and F-measure. An analysis of the performance of the proposed FNDetector model with incremental feature class addition can provide insights into the importance of different feature classes.

Approach: To answer this RQ, we perform two types of experiments. First, we created four variants of FNDetector models (for both RF and LSTM classifiers) and added features incrementally to observe the effect of each feature class on the performance of the FNDetector model. We create the following variants:

*   FNDetector[A]^(F[S]): FNDetector model trained using sentiment-based features.
*   FNDetector[A]^(F[S] + F[L]): FNDetector model trained using sentiment-based and linguistics-based features
*   FNDetector[A]^(F[S] + F[L] + F[N]): FNDetector model trained using sentiment-based, linguistics-based features, and NER-based features.
*   FNDetector[A]^(F[S] + F[L] + F[N] + F[T]): FNDetector model trained using all the 32 features extracted.

In the above, A refers to the classifier. A can take value RF or LSTM. Hence, we compute the performances of all the above variants with both RF and LSTM classifiers. Second, we compute the feature importance of all the numerical features using an RF classifier. [Table 8.18](#ch18.xhtml#t30) shows the notation for each feature set.

**Table 8.18** Notation for each feature set[⏎](#ch18.xhtml#t30b)

 
| Feature name || Feature symbol |
| --- | --- |
| Linguistic-based feature || FL |
| --- | --- |
| Sentiment-based feature || FS |
| Named entity recognition || FN |
| Textual features || FT |

We added features from different features incrementally. [Table 8.19](#ch18.xhtml#t31) shows a consistent improvement in the performance (for both accuracy and F-measure) of the FNDetector model with the addition of each feature set. In the next experiment, we calculate each numerical feature’s relevance using the RF classifier.

RQ 5: What is the effect on the performance of FNDetector model by changing parameters of RF and LSTM classifiers?

Motivation: The parameters of an algorithm play an important role as they can impact the performance of an algorithm. It’s critical to adjust an algorithm’s settings., in order to build a high-accuracy model. In this RQ, we will present what is the effect on the performance of the FNDetector model by changing the parameters of RF and LSTM classifiers. We also compute the time it requires to train the models as well as the time required to make the predictions. We believe that time can be an important factor in choosing the appropriate classifier in resource-constrained applications like mobile devices.

Approach: To answer the RQ, we perform several experiments with the FNDetector model using RF and LSTM variants. We create a total of seven variants of the FNDetector model, three using FNDetcetor[RF] and four using FNDetector[LSTM]. For FNDetector[RF], we tested the performance of the model for three different values of the estimator, i.e., 50, 100, and 150. For FNDetector[LSTM,] we tested the performance of the model by changing epochs and batch size. We use 2 different epochs, i.e., 5 and 10 epochs, and 2 different batch sizes, i.e., 128 and 256 respectively. [Table 8.20](#ch18.xhtml#t32) shows the parameters and values that we use to perform the experiments.

The time required to train the model and generate a prediction was then calculated. We compute the time it takes to build the model using the training dataset for both FNDetector[RF] and FNDetector[LSTM] classifiers for different parameters (number of estimators (for RF classifier) and epoch & batch size (for LSTM classifier)) as discussed above.

Results: [Figures 8.17](#ch18.xhtml#f61), [8.18](#ch18.xhtml#f62), and [8.19](#ch18.xhtml#f63) show the result of FNDetector[RF] classifier with 50, 100, and 150 n estimators. From the result, we can observe count of FPs and FNs decreases when the n\_estimators increase. Also, the AUC value is increased when the n\_estimators are increased. These results show that increasing the number of estimators can aid in enhancing the performance of FNDetector[RF] (please refer to [Table 8.21](#ch18.xhtml#t33)). For FNDetector[LSTM] classifier, we demonstrated the model accuracy and loss of the model by plotting it in [Figures 8.20](#ch18.xhtml#f64) and [8.21](#ch18.xhtml#f65). The results show that the FNDetector[LSTM] classifier attained the best result with epoch 10 and batch size 128. We can observe that the loss is decreasing and the accuracy of the model is increasing consistently as the number of epochs increases. This makes the FNDetector[LSTM] model with 10 epochs and 128 batch sizes have the highest accuracy in detecting fake news compared to other parameters.

[Table 8.21](#ch18.xhtml#t33) shows that for FNDetector[RF] classifier the training and prediction time increases as we increase the number of estimators. As we are increasing the number of estimators the RF algorithm needs to create more trees and hence, the time increases. For the FNDetector[LSTM] classifier, we observe a similar pattern, i.e., training time increase as we intensify the number of epochs. As we increase, batch size the training time decrease for the FNDetector[LSTM] classifier. This happened because with increases in batch size the algorithm can take big chunks of the training dataset and can learn faster. Overall, we observe that the FNDetector[RF] classifier took much less time in both training and testing as compared to the FNDetector[LSTM] classifier. Experimental results show the parameter tuning is very important as it can have a big influence FNDetector model’s effectiveness. In our experiment, the RF classifier achieved its best performance when the n estimator was set to 150 and the LSTM classifier achieve its best performance when epochs value is 10 and the batch size value is 128. FNDetector[RF] took much less training and testing time as compared to the FNDetector[LSTM] classifier.

## **8.9** Concluding remarks and future scope

The amount of news items shared on social networking websites has skyrocketed. Because so much false information circulates on social media, people are less likely to believe news articles published on these channels. It’s challenging to confirm the veracity of news articles on social media. The identification of fake news has become more significant as a way to get around this problem and as a motivator for related research. We provide FNDetector, a fake news detection model, in this paper. FNDetector uses a combination of various feature sets. We train the FNDetector using two classifiers, i.e., RF and LSTM. The FNDetector model outperformed the baseline classifier significantly. FNDetector[RF] and FNDetector[LSTM] give an accuracy of 88.2% and 78.4%, respectively. Our investigation related to feature importance shows that all the feature sets used in this paper are important and help in increasing the accuracy. Also, ‘noun count’ and ‘total word count’ in both the title and content of news articles are found to be the top four important features. We intend to use ensemble learning to expand the model in the future. The ensemble learning model combines different classifiers to improve training models. The ensemble learning model working has been shown in a few studies such as \[[69](#ch18.xhtml#r272)\] and \[[45](#ch18.xhtml#r248)\] to improve the outcomes of the models. Additionally, we plan to use more advanced methods such as BERT to enhance the effectiveness of fake news detection.

# Chapter 9Survey of visual deepfake detection methods

Nikhil Rathoure, R. K. Pateriya, Deepak Singh Tomar, Ankit Vidyarthi, Nitesh Bharot, and Priyanka Verma

DOI: [10.1201/9781003569619-9](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003569619-9)

## **9.1** Introduction

With the recent advancements in deep learning techniques, the world of a generation of hyper-realistic media generation and manipulation has seen unprecedented growth, and the impact it brought along with itself is tremendous and far-reaching.

Over the last few years, the use of AI-generated content has substantially increased on the internet. Especially during and after the COVID-19 pandemic period, the dependence of the public on digital media has increased multifold, with people spending more and more time in the virtual world. As the dependence on digital media has increased, misinformation through fake media has also increased significantly. One of the biggest sources of misinformation on the internet is “Deepfake”, which derives its name from “deep learning” and “fake”, referring to media synthesized and/or manipulated by deep learning methods. Deepfakes are hyper-realistic audio, video, or image that easily deceive humans. Deepfake is one of the most bothersome applications of AI in recent years in terms of its potential use for cybercrimes and has been called the most serious AI crime threat \[[1](#ch19.xhtml#r273)\].

Damages resulting from the spread of misinformation through deepfakes encompass damage to financial resources, psychological/mental health harm, and damage to the reputation and trust of companies/celebrities/politicians, which is often irrecoverable, causing trust issues and chaos and unrest in society. According to a report by Sumsub \[[2](#ch19.xhtml#r274)\], there has been an increase of 245% year on year in deepfakes worldwide from Q1 2023 till Q1 2024, with multifold growth in countries where elections are being held or scheduled in 2024. It reported YOY increase of deepfake incidents in India (+280%), USA (+303%), Mexico (+500%), Indonesia (+1550%), and South Korea (+1625%). The magnitude and spread of deepfakes through the increasing pervasiveness of digital media in our personal and professional lives have made deepfakes a de facto tool for misinformation in today’s time. As a result, the field of deepfake detection has gathered the attention of researchers across industries, academics, and the government. Thus, it has become important to analyze deepfake and related advances in deepfake generation methods and development in detection methods. In this review, we will focus on visual deepfake detection methods.

![Figure 9.1 Country-wise Year on Year increase % of Deepfakes from Q1 2023 till Q2 2024 \[2\].](html/images/9781032942346_C009_001.jpg)

**Figure 9.1** Country-wise Year on Year increase % of Deepfakes from Q1 2023 till Q2 2024 \[[2](#ch19.xhtml#r274)\].

This article is organized as follows: [Section 9.1](#ch19.xhtml#h1-048) provides the introduction of deepfakes. [Section 9.2](#ch19.xhtml#h1-049) discusses the various datasets available for deepfake detection methods for training and testing. [Section 9.3](#ch19.xhtml#h1-050) gives in-depth detail of various existing deepfake detection methods. Metrics used for performance assessment are discussed in [Section 9.4](#ch19.xhtml#h1-051). [Section 9.5](#ch19.xhtml#h1-052) presents the various challenges related to deepfakes detection that still exist. [Section 9.6](#ch19.xhtml#h1-053) summarizes and concludes the article.

### **9.1.1** Types of Deepfakes

Deepfakes can be divided into two broad categories: Visual Deepfakes and audio Deepfakes.

Visual Deepfakes refer to images or videos that are created by synthesizing fake images/videos or manipulating real images or videos. These visual Deepfakes involve images or videos in which the original face in the source has been replaced with someone else face (Face Swap). Source facial expressions have been changed (Face Reenactment) or certain characteristics or attributes of the face have been changed (Facial attribute manipulation) or complete fake has been generated (Entire Face Synthesis) or movement of the subject in the video has been changed in ways that actually never happened (puppet).

Audio Deepfakes refer to audio or audiovisual media instances in which the complete fake voice has been synthesized (voice synthesis using techniques such as text-to-speech), or lip-syncing has been done by changing the voice of the subject in a video with another voice recording (voice swapping).

### **9.1.2** Categories of visual Deepfake generation methods

![Figure 9.2 Types of deepfakes and deepfake generation methods.](html/images/9781032942346_C009_002.jpg)

**Figure 9.2** Types of deepfakes and deepfake generation methods.

Visual Deepfake algorithms can be divided into four major categories (Figure 9.2): (1) face swap, (2) face reenactment, (3) entire face synthesis, and (4) facial attributes manipulation. These major categories of Visual deepfake algorithms are discussed below:

*   Face swap: In face-swapping methods, the identity of the person in the source video/image is replaced with that of the person in the target video. Here, the expression of the face in the source video or image is an important characteristic of this category of deepfakes. Methods such as FaceSwap, Deepfakes, and FaceShifter of FF++ dataset belong to this category. Recent methods of this category generate highly realistic deepfake videos.
*   Face reenactment: In face reenactment methods, a portion of the face is modified to change the expression or say or do something that was never done or said. Although face reenactment methods often produce high-quality and realistic fake images/frames, they lack temporal consistency in the case of videos. Methods such as Face2Face of FaceForensics++ (FF++) \[[3](#ch19.xhtml#r275)\] is an example face reenactment method. In this category, the aim of the deepfake generation methods is to reconstruct facial expressions in the source video or image of the face present in the target video while preserving the illumination, head pose, identity, and other parameters of the target video/image.
*   Entire face synthesis: In entire face synthesis methods, a complete face, which is fake, is synthesized after training the generating model to learn the complex latent representations and features from the face dataset. Here, the synthesized face does not belong to any real person. ProGAN \[[4](#ch19.xhtml#r276)\] belongs to this category of deepfake generation methods.
*   Facial attributes manipulation: This category of deepfake generation methods includes methods which manipulate certain characteristics of the face, such as skin or eye or hair color, hairstyle, gender, age, etc. An example of this category of methods is StarGAN \[[5](#ch19.xhtml#r277)\], which can perform image-to-image translation across multiple domains (or attributes of the face).

### **9.1.3** Deepfake generation process

Key technology used for creating deepfakes is generative adversarial network (GAN) \[[6](#ch19.xhtml#r278)\] which was proposed in 2014. GAN as shown in [Figure 9.3](#ch19.xhtml#f68) consists of two networks which make it possible to generate new synthetic fake images. These comprising networks are (1) “generator”, whose purpose is to generate fake images, and (2) “discriminator”, whose task is to classify if the image generated by the generator is from a dataset of real images.

![Figure 9.3 GAN architecture.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C009_003.jpg)

**Figure 9.3** GAN architecture.[⏎](#ch19.xhtml#f68b)

The discriminator is trained like a binary classifier to maximize the probability of associating the correct label to both training samples as well as samples from the generator. The cost incurred by both networks is attempted to be minimized, and the back-propagation algorithm uses the derivatives of the discriminator’s output with respect to the discriminator’s input to train the generator to improve its output.

The first case of deepfake occurred in November 2017, when a Reddit user “deepfakes” published a pornographic video with the face of the porn actor replaced by the face of a celebrity using deep learning. Since then, there has been a rapid development in the field of deepfake such that celebrities, politicians, friends, and colleagues, and almost every person in society have been affected by it.

Since then, there has been an exponential rise in GAN methods, which are used in combination with other methods for generating deepfakes. ExprGAN \[[7](#ch19.xhtml#r279)\] introduced facial expression manipulation with expression control ability. ProGAN \[[4](#ch19.xhtml#r276)\] described a new methodology of GAN by progressively training generator and discriminator starting from low-resolution fake images and, as training progresses, adding new layers to synthesize finer quality images. StarGAN \[[5](#ch19.xhtml#r277)\] proposed image-to-image translations on multiple domains using a single model. GANimation \[[8](#ch19.xhtml#r280)\] allowed facial expression manipulation in the continuous manifold. StyleGAN \[[9](#ch19.xhtml#r281)\] made it possible to synthesize highly varied and high-quality face images with scale control variation of synthesis of fake images. StyleGAN2 \[[10](#ch19.xhtml#r282)\] is an improved version of StyleGAN. HomoInterpGAN \[[11](#ch19.xhtml#r283)\] generates high-quality facial expression translation. AttGAN \[[12](#ch19.xhtml#r284)\] provided realistic attribute manipulation.

Various other deepfake generation methods in the category of Face swap methods such as RSGAN \[[13](#ch19.xhtml#r285)\], FSGAN \[[14](#ch19.xhtml#r286)\], SimSwap \[[15](#ch19.xhtml#r287)\], HiFiFace \[[16](#ch19.xhtml#r288), [17](#ch19.xhtml#r289)\], and face reenactment methods such as ReenactGAN \[[18](#ch19.xhtml#r290), [19](#ch19.xhtml#r291)\], ICface \[[20](#ch19.xhtml#r292), [21](#ch19.xhtml#r293)\] etc have also emerged.

Comparable to GANs, diffusion models \[[22](#ch19.xhtml#r294)\] can synthesize sophisticated and good quality images \[[23](#ch19.xhtml#r295)\].

### **9.1.4** Opportunities

*   Marketing industry: Deepfakes provide new avenues for the marketing industry with the potential to revolutionize the industry by creating more personalized marketing and multilingual and cost-effective content creation. Under ethical and legal guidelines, digital marketing can use deepfakes to advertise a product using an individual’s personal favorite celebrity using his/her mother tongue to increase customer engagement. Dalí Museum, Florida used deepfake Salvador Dalí to greet visitors \[[24](#ch19.xhtml#r296)\]
*   Entertainment industry: Deepfakes has various exciting, revolutionary, and desirable offerings to the entertainment industry. Deepfakes show the ability to create cost-effective, fast, and automatic solutions for re-ageing actors or replacing stunt doubles. Existing facial reageing and replacement methods are costly and time-consuming processes as they require manual frame-by-frame work, which often takes a lot of days even by skilled workers and requires costly CGI or Makeup work. With Deepfakes, facial reageing and replacement tasks can be made cost-effective and faster than existing methods. In 2022, researchers at Disney \[[25](#ch19.xhtml#r297)\] presented a neural network-based production and fully automatic technique for reageing facial images in videos. It has the potential to make the arduous task of dubbing in different languages easier and cost-effective.
*   Healthcare industry: Deepfake has the potential of helping people suffering from Alzheimer’s disease to interact with people by creating their younger face, which can help patients to recall some memory \[[26](#ch19.xhtml#r298)\]. Deepfake also has the potential to help and cure mental health patients by creating realistic simulations of people interacting with them, providing an environment which they can find relatable and help create more interactive sessions between doctors and patients.

### **9.1.5** Threats

Due to the general availability of fake videos and images generating apps \[[27](#ch19.xhtml#r299), [28](#ch19.xhtml#r300)\], \[[29](#ch19.xhtml#r301)\] and source code, various threats are posed by deepfakes such as:

1.  Scammers using deepfakes to scam people by exploiting their socially available videos and images
2.  Forging media and spoofing the identity of public figures or celebrities to damage their reputation as well as create chaos and unrest in society
3.  Manipulating publicly available information present on the internet to influence the public in events such as elections in democracies to cause political unrest
4.  Using deepfake for bypassing biometric security systems, identity theft, cyberbullying, or harming the reputation of individuals by creating fake pornographic material, thus posing a threat to both personal and social security.
5.  Corporate sabotage using deepfake, which may result in a drastic drop in stock prices, malign the company’s reputation and monetary losses.
6.  Producing deepfake as false evidence in courts of law in the absence of a robust deepfake detection method presents a major threat which needs significant attention of law enforcement
7.  People’s natural inclination to believe what they see also poses a major threat resulting from deepfakes.

## **9.2** Datasets

In this section, we discuss the various popular deepfake video datasets. [Figure 9.4](#ch19.xhtml#f69) and [Table 9.1](#ch19.xhtml#t34) show the various three generations of deepfake datasets \[[30](#ch19.xhtml#r302), [31](#ch19.xhtml#r303)\].

**Figure 9.4** Generations of Deepfake datasets.[⏎](#ch19.xhtml#f69b)

| **Dataset** || **Release Year** || **Real/Fake** || **Source** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| UADFV || 2018 || 49/49 || YouTube |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Deepfake-TIMIT || 2018 || \-/620 || VidTIMIT \[[32](#ch19.xhtml#r304)\] |
