### **9.2.1** Uadfv

This is one of the early datasets which contains 49 real videos obtained from YouTube and 49 fake videos generated using DNN model using FakeAPP \[[33](#ch19.xhtml#r305)\].

### **9.2.2** Deepfake-TIMIT

It is also one of the early-generation datasets that contain 690 Deepfake videos created using FaceSwap GAN on the VID-TIMIT dataset. It contains an equal number of high-quality videos (DF-TIMIT-HQ) and low-quality videos (DF-TIMIT-LQ) \[[34](#ch19.xhtml#r306)\].

### **9.2.3** FaceForensics++ (FF++)

FaceForensics++ is a widely used large-scale dataset which originally contained a total of 5000 videos, of which 1000 videos are original videos obtained from YouTube and 4000 fake videos created using four different manipulation techniques, with 1000 fake videos created using each manipulation method. Later, a new manipulation technique, FaceShifter, was added to FaceForensics++ to add 1000 more manipulated videos, resulting in a total of 6000 videos. It is one of the benchmark datasets in which original videos have unoccluded frontal faces on which the following five manipulation methods have been applied \[[3](#ch19.xhtml#r275)\]:

*   Deepfakes \[[35](#ch19.xhtml#r307)\]: It is a based method that is trained using pairs of subject images to compute a mapping between them and produces an image by face swapping the given face in the source image with the target face.
*   FaceSwap \[[36](#ch19.xhtml#r308)\]: It is a 3D face-swapping method that uses the traditional graphics pipeline and blending to create a forged image by swapping the face in the given image with that of the target.
*   Face2Face \[[37](#ch19.xhtml#r309)\]: It is a facial reenactment method that is based on computer graphics based approach. It uses expression manipulation of a target video from the source video while maintaining the facial identity of the target video.
*   NeuralTextures \[[38](#ch19.xhtml#r310)\]: It is a forgery method which learns a face neural texture from a target video and then uses it to create a realistic face reenactment while preserving the identity by only modifying the mouth region of the face.
*   FaceShifter \[[39](#ch19.xhtml#r311)\]: This method is added latest in the original FF++ dataset, and it is a two-stage face swapping method for producing high fidelity and occlusion aware face swapping by reconstructing attributes that are hard to replicate.

Face2Face and FaceSwap are computer graphics-based approaches, while Deepfakes and NeuralTextures are learning-based approaches. These videos are available in three types of compression quality: c23 quality videos (high-quality videos with low compression ratio), c40 quality videos (low-quality videos with high compression ratio), and raw videos.

### **9.2.4** Dfd

DeepFake detection dataset is a dataset provided by Google and Jigsaw, which contains 3068 manipulated videos from 363 original videos obtained with the consent of actors of different genders, ages and ethnicities \[[40](#ch19.xhtml#r312)\].

### **9.2.5** DFDC \[[31](#ch19.xhtml#r303)\] and DFDC preview \[[41](#ch19.xhtml#r313)\]

DeepFake Detection Challenge Dataset (DFDC) \[[31](#ch19.xhtml#r303)\] is one of the largest datasets provided by Facebook which contains total 128,154 videos comprising of 104500 fake videos and 23654 real videos. These videos are created using 3426 paid actors of various ages, gender, skin-tone etc, and fake videos are generated using eight different Deepfake, GAN-based methods and non-learning-based methods. DFDC preview \[[41](#ch19.xhtml#r313)\] comprises 4119 fake videos created and 1131 original videos with total videos of 5K videos of paid actors with fake videos created using two face manipulation methods.

### **9.2.6** Celeb-DF v2

Celeb-DF is one of the most challenging large-scale datasets, which contains 5639 deepfake videos generated from 590 original videos. The source of these videos is publicly available YouTube videos of 59 celebrities of diverse ages, ethnicity, and gender. It is one of the most difficult data set for existing Deepfake detection algorithms \[[30](#ch19.xhtml#r302)\].

### **9.2.7** WildDeepfake

WildDeepfake is one the recent datasets consisting of 707 total videos sourced from the internet providing 3805 real face sequences and 3509 fake face sequences representing a wide variety of wild deepfakes present in the internet \[[42](#ch19.xhtml#r314)\].

### **9.2.8** DeeperForensics-1.0 (DF-1.0)

It is one of the large datasets containing a total of 60000 videos with real and fake videos in the ratio of 5:1. Videos of 100 paid actors are used, which are created using a controlled indoor environment, and fake videos are created by applying seven different types of perturbations at five different intensity levels \[[43](#ch19.xhtml#r315)\].

### **9.2.9** KoDF

It is the largest dataset, containing a total of 237,942 video clips of 403 subjects comprising 175,776 fake videos and 62,166 real videos. Fake videos are synthesized using six different varieties of face-swap and face-reenactment methods \[[44](#ch19.xhtml#r316)\].

## **9.3** Visual Deepfake detection methods

### **9.3.1** Stages used in Deepfake video detection studies

[Figure 9.5](#ch19.xhtml#f70) shows the overall steps used in the deepfake video detection studies. These steps are summarized below:

![Figure 9.5 Steps used in Deepfake detection.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C009_005.jpg)

**Figure 9.5** Steps used in Deepfake detection.[⏎](#ch19.xhtml#f70b)

*   The Initial phase consists of the creation of a dataset by either collecting and organizing deepfake videos or using available datasets.
*    From the dataset, videos are preprocessed by extracting the video frames and cropping and resizing as needed is performed on the frames to prepare for improved feature selection.
*   Feature extraction is performed and useful features are selected to be used for detection
*   The model to be used for detection is then either a newly created or a suitable available model.
*   The selected model is then trained on the dataset to learn key features for distinguishing real and fake videos.
*   Finally model evaluation is done using various performance metrics to assess the performance of the model.

### **9.3.2** Classification of detection methods

*   Biological/Physiological signal based methods: use biological/physiological signals such as abnormalities in eye blinking, heart rate, skin color, etc., for detection of deepfakes.
*   Statistical feature-based methods: employ statistical measures such as PRNU, cross-correlation scores, etc, for forgery identification.
*   Machine learning based methods: work by defining features and the feature vector is fed to machine learning algorithms such as support vector machine, logistic regression, random forest, etc to train the classifier for detection of deepfakes.
*   Frequency domain feature-based methods: are based on the identification of forgery clues in the frequency domain and help in the discovery of latent features which provide crucial clues for deepfake detection.
*   Temporal domain feature-based methods: use temporal inconsistencies in video frames for deepfake detection.
*    Deep learning-based methods: are widely used deepfake detection methods as they can extract complex latent patterns and learn features to provide high detection performance using deep learning models such as CNN, RNN, etc.
*   Transformer-based methods: use transformers such as Vision Transformer (ViT) \[[45](#ch19.xhtml#r317)\] for improved generalized deepfake detection by leveraging the self-attention mechanism to capture complex spatial and temporal dependencies within videos.

### **9.3.3** Early Deepfake detection methods

Early deepfake detection methods employed hand-crafted artefacts or CNN-based feature extraction methods to extract the visual features, which are different for manipulated and real images, and then train classifiers on the visual features to detect deepfake images.

1.  *Biological signal based methods:* Early hand-crafted artefact methods included physiological or biological signals-based deepfake detection methods that use physiological/biological signs such as eye blinking or heart rate as signs for classifying videos as either fake or real. Li et al. \[[46](#ch19.xhtml#r318)\] used eye blinking abnormalities in fake videos to detect deepfake by using Long term Recurrent CNN (LRCN) model to capture eye states. Each frame is cropped to a rectangular shape around the eye area after face alignment and passed to the LRCN model to learn discriminative temporal features, predict the eye states, and calculate blink frequency. The model shows good performance in detecting deepfake videos on the evaluation of self-made datasets. Tackhyun et al. \[[47](#ch19.xhtml#r319)\] proposed an integrity verification algorithm that uses an eye-blinking pattern to classify a video as fake or real. The authors established an initial DB containing natural values of various factors that affect the eye blinking pattern such as age, gender, time of the day, and the activity of the subject in the video and corresponding information like blink count, period, elapsed time, etc. The proposed algorithm is used to compare the blinking information from the database with that extracted from the video to classify the video as fake or real. The method is evaluated on a self-made dataset of deepfake videos and achieves an accuracy of little over 87%. FakeCatcher \[[48](#ch19.xhtml#r320)\], proposed by Aybars et al., is a biological signals-based deepfake detection model for fake portrait videos and uses color-based remote photoplethysmography (rPPG \[[49](#ch19.xhtml#r321)\]) to extract heart rate signals by observing subtle changes in the skin color. The PPG is a technique that can detect changes in skin color resulting from blood circulation. The model is evaluated using datasets FF++, UADFV, FF, and Celeb-DF in addition to their own dataset and achieving accuracies in the range 91%–96% for different datasets. Similarly, Hernandez-Ortega et al. \[[50](#ch19.xhtml#r322)\] is also a biological signals-based model that uses a convolutional attention network (CAN) trained for heart rate estimation using rPPG to extract spatial and temporal information from video frames and analyze both the extracted information to detect fake videos. This model is evaluated on Celeb-DF and DFDC preview datasets and demonstrated AUC performance of 99.9 % and 98.2% on the datasets, respectively. However, biological/physiological signals-based methods are no longer efficient as the latest deepfake generation algorithms are able to reconstruct such signals with more efficiency and with good enough quality that such methods cannot efficiently detect abnormalities associated with biological or physiological signals.
2.  *Early CNN-based methods:* Early learning-based approaches used CNN-based methods such as \[[51](#ch19.xhtml#r323)\], which is based on the computation of co-occurrence matrix on the RGB channels of an image and used the computed vector as input to deep CNN model to detect deepfakes.

### 9.3.4 Statistical feature-based methods

Alongside these methods, statistics-based deepfake fingerprint identification methods mentioned in \[[52](#ch19.xhtml#r324)\] and \[[53](#ch19.xhtml#r325)\] used photo response nonuniformity (PRNU), noise pattern resulting by light-sensitive sensors of digital cameras for detection of deepfakes.

Luca et al. \[[54](#ch19.xhtml#r326)\] proposed deepfake detection approach that is based on an expectation and maximization algorithm to create a mathematical model to represent the local correlations between each pixel and its neighborhood and capture this relationship in the form of a vector called convolutional trace (CT) that characterizes the traces left by GAN process used for creating deepfake images. The method extracts CT and uses it as a feature vector as input to naïve classifier to discriminate between real and fake images. Experimentations were conducted on images generated by 10 different GANs which involved with and without face manipulation and using different classifiers such as SVM, LDA, K-NN etc. The Random forest classifier provided the best classification result with an overall accuracy of over 98%, and when tested on deepfakes generated by FACEAPP, a test accuracy of 93% was achieved. However, accuracy decreases to as low as 56.96% on the application of different postprocessing techniques such as blurring, rotation, compression, etc.

### **9.3.5** Frequency domain feature-based methods

Frequency in Face Forgery Network (*F*³ net) proposed by Qian et al. \[[55](#ch19.xhtml#r327)\] used frequency-aware forgery clues for face forgery detection. The proposed method consists of two branches: (1) frequency aware decomposition branch, which partitions input image in the frequency domain according to learnable filters, and then decomposed frequency components are transformed back into spatial domain image components, which are stacked together and fed to CNN to fine forgery clues. (2) The local frequency statistics branch mines discrepancies in local frequency statistics between real and fake images of faces by employing sliding window DCT on the input image and calculating the mean frequency values within a set of learnable frequency bands. The two branches are then fused together using a cross-attention fusion module. The features of the two branches are then augmented using cross attention fusion module for feature interaction between the two branches and FF++ dataset is used for method evaluation and results of which show good performance in different qualities of images of FF++ with a minimum of 93.2% accuracy and 0.958 AUC score for low quality and a maximum of 99.99% accuracy and 0.9999 AUC score for raw FF++ videos.

Another frequency-based deepfake detection method, hierarchical frequency-assisted interactive networks (HFI-Net) proposed by Changtao et al. \[[56](#ch19.xhtml#r328)\] is dual branch network that explores mining face forgery clues of deepfakes in the frequency domain. It consists of two branches: (1) transformer branch, which consists of four stages of transformer blocks (each stage has three ViT transformer blocks), captures Global forgery clues (2) CNN branch, for extracting local forgery artefacts, also has four stages as transformer branch with each stage containing three separable convolution blocks and a residual connection between input and output of the stage. At each stage of HFI-Net a global–local interaction (GLI) module fuses, global and local information and allows interaction between the two branches of the network and combines them to provide complementary features and improve generalization ability. Intradataset experimentation is done on FaceForensics++ (FF++) dataset, and cross-dataset evaluation is done on Celeb-DF, TIMIT, DFDC, and UADFV datasets. and also use GID-DF and GID-FF protocols \[[57](#ch19.xhtml#r329)\] for unseen manipulations experimentation and DeeperForensics-1.0 for unseen perturbations experiments. The method shows impressive performance in different experiments.

### 9.3.6 Temporal domain feature-based methods

The recurrent neural network method proposed by Xi et al. \[[58](#ch19.xhtml#r330)\] is based on extracting spatial, steganalysis, and temporal features. For spatial features, XceptionNet is used, and for extracting Steganalysis features, constrained convolutional filter is used on simplified XceptionNet. Both of these features are fused together and temporal features are extracted from the fused features of the consecutive frames using LSTM. The proposed method is evaluated using FF++ and GAN-based Deepfakes \[[59](#ch19.xhtml#r331)\] dataset. The method achieves accuracy % of 98.57% (FF++ HQ) and 90.11% (FF++ LQ) in the intradataset evaluation, and cross-dataset evaluation on GAN-based Deepfakes achieves accuracy comparatively lower than that of intra-dataset evaluation.

Xi et al. \[[60](#ch19.xhtml#r332)\] proposed a deepfake video detection method that uses both spatial and noise features of the spatial and temporal domain of video frame to detect deepfake. Of the k-selected video frames, each cropped facial regions are sent to two streams: RGB stream and SRM (Spatial Rich Model) stream. RGB stream derives the RGB features and SRM stream is used for extracting noise features by applying learnable SRM filters whose values are defined manually. The fusion of both segment-based and stream-based predictions provides the final detection result. Both streams use Xception as a backbone network, and the method is evaluated on the FF++ dataset and achieves the performance of 90.36% accuracy on evaluation.

### **9.3.7** Deep learning-based methods

Yuval et al. \[[61](#ch19.xhtml#r333)\] presented a method which aimed at identifying deepfake videos based on comparing inner and outer context regions, and the method is based on the assumption that the manipulation method only manipulates the inner part of the face and the outer context regions such as hairs, neck, ears etc are not easy to regenerate by the manipulation methods. During the preprocessing step, the face region is detected and cropped, and the inner face region and context region are obtained and fed into different Xception \[[62](#ch19.xhtml#r334)\]-based networks. *E*[*f*] learns on the facial region, and *E*[*c*] learns on the context region. Extracted vectors are then the difference of the two extracted vectors is computed and used as discrepancy vector which is concatenated with vectors obtained from the other two networks which map face regions from face swap and face reenactment methods. The concatenated vector is then passed to the final classifier for the final detection task. The method uses multiple datasets such as VGGFace2, Labeled Faces in the Wild (LFW), FaceForensics++, DFDC, Celeb-DFDC, and FaceForensics++ datasets for training and testing. This method achieves AUC % performance of as low as 66% (Celeb-DF v2) and as high as 99.7% (FF-DF) in images created by face swapping.

Framework for mining generalized features for the detection of AI-manipulated faces proposed by Yang et al. \[[63](#ch19.xhtml#r335)\] focused on mining intrinsic features in the form of channel difference image (CDI) and spectrum image (SI), which are indispensable characteristics of the camera imaging process and AI manipulation process. The framework uses the Octave convolution for mining the features as both the features are related to the frequency domain and uses an attention-based fusion module to effectively combine the extracted information and adopt a domain alignment module to improve generalizability. The dataset used for experiments is constructed by four categories (face entire synthesis, facial expression manipulation, facial attribute manipulation, and identity manipulation), and the dataset contains 10 state-of-the-art and popular manipulation techniques such as StyleGAN, StarGAN, FF++ (Deepfakes), DFDC, etc. The model outperforms other existing methods in both the same dataset analysis as well as cross dataset analysis and demonstrates good performance for different postprocessing methods such as compression, resizing, median filtering, noise addition, etc. For unseen categories, the method achieves an accuracy performance of 86.79% for entire synthesis, 89.26% for expression manipulation, 89.74% for attribute manipulation, and 81.92% for identity manipulation.

Based on the observation that deepfake generation methods generate inconsistent lighting, sharpness, noise, blur, etc, which results in loss of depth information in fake images as compared to real images, Liang et al. \[[64](#ch19.xhtml#r336)\] proposed a depth map prediction network and depth map guided triplet network. depth map prediction network evaluates depth information and generates a depth map. The triplet feature extraction network has an input of the original face, fake face and target face each concatenated by a depth map extracted from the depth prediction network which forms triplet input and consists of three swin transformer-based feature extractors which are trained on triplet and use the triplet loss to constraint the feature distance between two real images as close as possible and the distance between real and fake images as far as possible. Finally, these extracted features are combined and fed as input into the classifier for classification. Biwi Kinect Head Pose Database, FF++, and Celeb-DF dataset are used for training and testing of the proposed method and achieve a minimum of 91.51% accuracy (in case of low-quality FF++ dataset) and AUC % of 72.30 in case of Celeb-DF dataset in cross-dataset evaluation scenario.

Liang et al. \[[65](#ch19.xhtml#r337)\] proposed a framework called masked relation learning for video-based Deepfake detection which consisted of two components: (1) SpatioTemporal Attention (STA) module which produces attention maps to extract features from various facial regions and (2) masked relation learner (MRL) module for learning the irregularity in the relation between facial regions from deepfakes. Firstly, a graph is constructed in which vertices denote features of facial regions and the edge represents feature correlation between two regions. In the training procedure, MRL masks the partial edges to reduce the redundancy of relations and learn the critical structure of faces and uses the temporal convolution network (TGCN) to capture the irregularities of relations to expose the deepfakes. FF++, Celeb-DF, and DFDC are used as datasets for evaluation of the proposed method and achieve competitive performance of AUC% performance of 96.18% in FF++ (c40) dataset, 83.58% in Celeb-DF, and 71.53% in DFDC dataset.

Multiclassification framework FCD-Net of deepfake images to detect multiple types of deepfakes proposed by Ruidong et al. \[[66](#ch19.xhtml#r338)\] consists of three modules to detect deepfake images and to classify them. These three modules are: (1) Face Synaptic Saliency Module (FSS) module which consists of three parallel branches of depth separable convolution layers is used for extracting better face features than standard convolution layers. (2) Contour detail feature extraction (CDFE) Module is used for extracting key discriminating facial features and reduce impact of background noise. (3) Distinguishing feature fusion (DFF) Module is an improved pooling layer used before fully connected layer to extract multiscale information by concatenating multiple max pooling layers of different kernel sizes. In addition, they proposed HDFD dataset of homologous deepfake images which consists of four categories of images: real face images (obtained from FFHQ dataset), face swap synthesis, entire face synthesis, and facial attribute manipulation, with each category containing 8000 images where each manipulated image is constructed from the real ones through StyleGAN, FaceAPP, and Deepfakes(FaceSwap). The proposed method is then trained and tested on the HDFD dataset with AUC % performance reaching nearly 100%.

1.  *Transformer based detection methods:* Han et al. \[[67](#ch19.xhtml#r339)\] proposed a method to detect deepfakes by learning distinctive features of intraconsistency within classes and inter-diversity between classes. Firstly, a Vision Transformer (ViT) \[[45](#ch19.xhtml#r317)\]-based encoder is trained by using patch-wise masking and prediction scheme where the encoder is trained to predict masked image patches, which help in mining hidden features in an image without using deepfake labels in a self-supervised learning way. For better generalizability and improved latent discriminative feature extraction, the pretrained encoder is fine-tuned with labelled data for deepfake detection. To develop a more general and robust detection method, a Discrete Wavelet Transformation (DWT)-based Adjustable Forgery Synthesizer (AFS) module is introduced, which augments the data to generate diverse quality and diverse similarities between images to facilitate better representation learning. Datasets used for evaluation are: FF++, CelebDF, DFDC, UADFV, DF-TIMIT, DFR, and WildDeepfake and demonstrate competitive performance. The method shows the highest AUC % of 99.55% in the case of the FF++ (c23) dataset and the lowest of 81.44% in the case of the WildDeepfake dataset (model trained on FF++ DF (c23)).

Tianyi et al. \[[68](#ch19.xhtml#r340)\] proposed a keyframe-based deep convolutional transformer model, which jointly leveraged convolutional pooling and reattention approaches to study the discriminative image features. Local features of the image are extracted using a stack of CNN layers, and global features are analyzed using ViT together with convolution pooling and reattention technique on keyframes or I-frames, which contain complete information than using P-frames and B-frames which suffer from information loss as a result of compression. Datasets used for evaluation are FF++, Celeb-DF, DFDC, and DF-1.0 and results show that the method has good performance in the same dataset scenario of however cross-dataset performance is relatively lower.

[Table 9.2](#ch19.xhtml#t35) provides a summary of the methods in an intradataset scenario where training and testing are done on the same dataset. [Table 9.3](#ch19.xhtml#t36) summarizes the performance of the deepfake detection methods in cross-dataset scenarios where training and testing are done on different datasets.

**Table 9.2** Summary of performance of detection methods in intradataset scenario[⏎](#ch19.xhtml#t35b)

**Table 9.4** Performance metrics used by various studies for Deepfake detection[⏎](#ch19.xhtml#t37b)

 
| **Metrics** || **Definition** |
| --- | --- |
| Accuracy (ACC) || Accuracy is the measure of total number of correct predictions out of all predictions. ACC = (TN + TP)/ (TP+FP+TN+FN) |
| --- | --- |
| ROC curve || Receiver operating characteristic curve or ROC curve is a graphical plot to represent performance graphically by showcasing trade-off between true positive rate (TPR) and false positive rate (FPR) at different thresholds and separates signal from noise |
| Area Under Curve (AUC) || The AUC represents the area under ROC curve. The greater the AUC, the better the performance of the model. If AUC is equal to 1, the classifier is able to perfectly distinguish between all the Positive and Negative instances. |
| Precision || Precision refers to the proportion of correct positive predictions by the model out of all positive predictions. Precision = TP/ (TP+FP) |
| Recall || Recall, also known as sensitivity, provides a measure of the proportion of all correct positive predictions out of all the actual positive instances. Recall = TP / (TP + FN) |
| F1 score || F1 scores measure the harmonic mean of precision and recall to provide a comprehensive evaluation of the model. F1 = 2 \* Recall \* Precision / (Recall + Precision) |
| log loss || log loss assess the performance of a classification model and important metrics used for classification |
| Error Rate || Error rate assess the performance by providing the number of instances the classification model predicts wrongly. Error Rate = (FP + FN)/ (TP + TN + FP + FN) |
| MCC || Matthews correlation coefficient or MCC provides a single value summarizing the confusion matrix |

To assess the performance of various methods, various metrics used for evaluation of deepfake detection methods are shown in [Table 9.4](#ch19.xhtml#t37). It has been observed that the most often used metrics are accuracy, receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve, and AUC. While error rate, recall, and F1-score are comparatively not popular in deepfake detection methods, accuracy and AUC are the most popular metrics used for performance evaluation.

## **9.4** Emerging challenges and open issues

With the rise of deepfakes, new challenges are emerging in the way of deepfake detection methods. These challenges can be summarized as follows:

*   Decreased cost of creating doctored media as deep learning has made it easy for anyone to perform manipulation in less time and effort unlike earlier times when an individual needed to be skilled in some image processing tool to create realistic morphed images
*   Continuous evolution of powerful deepfake generation methods and new methods such as variational autoencoders (VAEs) \[[69](#ch19.xhtml#r341)\] and diffusion models \[[22](#ch19.xhtml#r294)\] that create very realistic doctored media
*   Increasing computing capabilities of devices and easy availability of powerful hardware like GPUs have increased generation of hyper-realistic deepfakes
*   Public availability of the source code and apps for generating deepfakes such that anyone can generate deepfakes easily with just access to the internet

## **9.5** Metrics

### **9.5.1** Open issues

In addition to the emerging challenges, there are several open issues which need the attention of future researches in the field of deepfake detection.

*   High computational cost: Most of the existing methods require high computations and significant time to perform deepfake detection. This prevents deepfake detection on real-time data. This creates a gap that needs to be addressed by future researchers. to develop detection methods with lesser computations and time consumption.
*   Lack of good generalized methods: An analysis of various existing methods shows the need to improve the generalization ability of detection methods as well as the need for performance improvement in postprocessed videos and requires immediate attention of researchers of both industry and academics for developing tools and techniques that can detect the deepfakes with high efficiency and robustness and reliability to create a check on misinformation by providing public in general the ways to be able to distinguish between fake and real.
*   Need for balanced quality dataset: Most of the existing deepfake datasets are imbalanced, and as new variants of deepfakes are emerging and evolving continuously, there is a need for a balanced dataset that contains a collection of real and fake data created using diversified manipulation methods and perturbation methods.

## **9.6** Conclusion

In this survey, we presented a detailed survey of deepfakes from their origin and generation process to various deepfake detection methods developed over the years. We found that the generation process of deepfakes is continuously evolving, and there has been a steep rise in deepfakes circulation on the internet over the last few years. FF++ is the most popular deepfake dataset that has been used in the majority of deepfake detection methods, with Accuracy being the most popular evaluation metric. We also observed that CNN-based methods have been the most widely used techniques used for deepfake detection.

With the rapid rise in the circulation of deepfakes on the internet and the continuous evolution of the deepfake generation process, deepfake detection methods still face a wide variety of challenges which need increased attention from researchers. This article covers a wide range of topics related to deepfakes such as categories of deepfakes, datasets available on the internet for deepfake detection methods’ training and testing, various existing deepfake detection methods as well as threats and challenges related to deepfakes etc so that this article can serve as a valuable resource and hope will help the researchers in developing effective deepfake detection methods.

# Chapter 10Empowering educators Leveraging large language models for lecture preparation material development

Harshita Chandwani, Anuja Arora, Adwitiya Sinha, Neetu Sardana, and Deepika Varshney

DOI: [10.1201/9781003569619-10](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003569619-10)

## **10.1** Introduction

Large language models (LLM) have the potential to create or enhance existing solutions across various domains, and the current educational framework is one of them. In this domain, LLMs can be applied for tasks such as generation of quiz \[[1](#ch20.xhtml#r342)\], planning of lecture \[[2](#ch20.xhtml#r343)\], and suggestion for content \[[3](#ch20.xhtml#r344)\]. In this same direction, there remains a notable gap in research concerning the generation of lecture preparation material specific to programming computer science courses. Literature suggests exploration of effective prompt engineering techniques to guide the language model specific to programming course-related lecture generation for neophyte coder.

Kojima et al. \[[4](#ch20.xhtml#r345)\] mentioned in their work the success of LLMs is attributed to few-shot or zero-shot learning. Several studies have explored the use of large language models and attributed these models through prompt engineering using zero shot, few shot, conditioning few shots \[[5](#ch20.xhtml#r346)\], instructional zero shot \[[6](#ch20.xhtml#r347), [7](#ch20.xhtml#r348)\], etc. The primarily used zero-shot learning works without any explicit training data and generate a generalize outcome of tasks. On the other hand, few shots use a very limited amount of data to achieve the desired outcome. The developer implements prompt tuning as well to generate task-specific data during training \[[8](#ch20.xhtml#r349)\]. A significant concern in preparing lecture materials for coding courses is ensuring consistency. Literature recommends utilization of template prompts \[[9](#ch20.xhtml#r350)\] to assist coding learners in grasping various algorithms and problem-solving concepts in a uniform and coherent manner. This research work is a notable effort towards generation of lecture preparation material using template-based prompting and aims to address the gap in various prompt engineering techniques by investigating the effectiveness of template-based prompting. The research objectives of this study are as follows:

*   To generate lecture preparation material for programming/coding courses to serve as study material for professors.
*   To explore the feasibility of template prompting for educational content generation
*    To evaluate performance of template prompting over conventional zero-shot and few-shot prompting for generating lecture preparation material by implementing a three-phased evaluation process.
*   To explore utilization of Gemini pro for lecture preparation material quality assessment.

To achieve these objectives, comprehensive was developed involving the exploration of various queries, categorization of the queries, generation of lecture preparation material using various prompting techniques, and evaluation of the generated material using Gemini Pro which to our best of the knowledge has not been explored before. This research has made various contributions to the contemporary literature. First, by exploring the application of template-based prompting for generating lecture preparation material expanded the scope for use of large language models in education. Second, it renders educators and researchers with meaningful insights into the effectiveness of various prompting techniques and an understanding of the most suitable approach for generating high-quality lecture preparation material specifically for programming courses. Examining the quality and relevance of generated lecture preparation material using Gemini Pro demonstrates its capabilities as a content evaluator which is also an unexplored domain. Our work discusses about the literature studied, throwing light upon how the applications of LLMs are being explored in the education domain, the framework for generation of lecture preparation material, various prompting techniques explored, utilization of Gemini Pro with RAG for ranking, the experimental setup and results obtained from the three-phased evaluation in the following sections.

## **10.2** Existing literature survey

The language models have already been used in education domain for diverse objectives such as to facilitate teachers by generating quizzes \[[1](#ch20.xhtml#r342)\], defining lecture objectives \[[10](#ch20.xhtml#r351)\], preparing lecture content \[3, 11, and 12\], providing suggestions based on the feedback of students \[[13](#ch20.xhtml#r354)\], etc. Several research papers are studied in education domain specifically utilizing large language models and a few relevant research papers are summarized in this section.

One recently published most relevant research work by Spasić et al. \[[2](#ch20.xhtml#r343)\] focuses on the development and testing of prompting techniques to enhance the generation of lecture plan for teaching programming to preschoolers. The authors employed three prompting techniques: (1) standard prompting, (2) standard prompting with role prompt, and (3) standard prompting with role, instructions, and seed words. The results demonstrate the capacity of the model to generate human-like responses aligned with the given task. However, there were notable differences among the generated lecture plans, particularly in terms of adaptability to preschoolers and adherence to unplugged programming principles. The findings suggest that carefully tailored prompts, incorporating role definitions and seed words, can significantly influence the quality and suitability of the generated lecture plans. Sridhar et al. \[[10](#ch20.xhtml#r351)\] have used language model GPT-4 to automatically design learning objectives (LOs) for artificial intelligence courses. This study explores the efficiency of GPT-4 to produce high-quality LOs aligned with Bloom’s Taxonomy. The result shows that GPT-4 can generate sensible and well aligned LOs with Bloom’s taxonomy.

Another research work introduces an initiative to incorporate generative AI by applying large language model into online higher education classes at large public university in the USA. The implemented LLM extracts the rich metadata from lecture videos, segment lecture into coherent units, extract key definitions, examples, and procedural knowledge of taught topics. Even, questions to test student understanding of the taught topic using LLM are also generated. This research paper outlines several research directions to explore the full potential of LLMs for online education \[[13](#ch20.xhtml#r354)\]. For quiz generation, EvalQuiz was introduced by Meißner et al. to automatically generate self-assessment quizzes in software engineering education using LLMs \[[1](#ch20.xhtml#r342)\]. Traditional quiz creation is time-consuming process that require expertise and manual review of questions. EvalQuiz aims to alleviate this burden by leveraging LLMs, specifically GPT-4, to automate quiz generation based on lecture materials. The process involves several stages: topic modeling and keyword extraction, prompt engineering and question generation, assembling and validation of quizzes, and evaluation. Lecture material is processed to extract relevant keywords and generate prompts for the LLM. The generated quizzes are then assembled and validated, ensuring compliance with specified formats. Finally, the quality of the quizzes is evaluated, considering factors like relevance, correctness, and originality \[[1](#ch20.xhtml#r342)\]. A personalized lecture summarization tool, HumSum, is developed by Kolagar et al. \[[14](#ch20.xhtml#r355)\] for humanities students. HumSum collects user preferences through a scenario-driven survey to guide the implementation of the tool. Utilizing Streamlit for the user interface and map reduce function OF Langchain with GPT-4 model of OpenAI enabled effective summarization without collecting personal user data \[[14](#ch20.xhtml#r355)\]. A survey published in a conference discusses the use of large language models specifically in the education domain \[[15](#ch20.xhtml#r356)\]. It delves into the various stakeholders involved in education, including teachers, students, parents, educational institutions, government, and society, highlighting their roles and interactions within the educational ecosystem. Several applications of LLM in education domain are briefed in the manuscript and future implications of LLM in education are discussed to address the challenges in building a landscape where smart education becomes more personalized, diverse, and digitally enhanced \[[15](#ch20.xhtml#r356)\]. Leiker and his group conducted experiments for the creation of course using an LLM under the guidance of prompt engineering and human oversight \[[11](#ch20.xhtml#r352)\]. They meticulously examine both the advantages and hurdles associated with employing LLMs in the asynchronous creation of courses for adult education, training, and upskilling. Despite the immense potential of LLMs in accessing and synthesizing extensive human knowledge, reservations persist regarding their accuracy and reliability. Consequently, the study advocates for a balanced approach, suggesting the integration of human oversight to ensure the quality and clarity of the generated output \[[11](#ch20.xhtml#r352)\]. Another research direction in this same domain is towards conversational tutoring system such as Ruffle&Riley \[[16](#ch20.xhtml#r357)\]. This system automates scripting and orchestration for learning-by-teaching interactions and LLM agents engage users in free-form conversations. A user study comparing Ruffle&Riley to simpler QA chatbots and reading activities have shown no significant difference in post-test scores, but users rated Ruffle&Riley higher in understanding, remembering, helpfulness, and coherence \[[16](#ch20.xhtml#r357)\]. Jauhiainen and Guerra research focuses on utilization of generative AI in primary school education. This theoretical study is done on 110 pupils of 8–14 years of age and studying in classes 4–6 in two different schools as participants in test lectures \[[12](#ch20.xhtml#r353)\]. The results of the study demonstrated the feasibility of utilizing ChatGPT-3.5 to personalize learning materials to meet the knowledge and learning skills of pupils with varying levels of knowledge. Importantly, a clear majority of pupils reported enjoying the generative AI-modified material, indicating the potential for generative AI to support motivated learning and skills development in school education \[[12](#ch20.xhtml#r353)\].

The studied research papers are usually used for theoretical subjects whereas lecture preparation material generation for professors using large language model for programming subject using a template-based prompting is still an unsolved and less explored issue.

## **10.3** Lecture preparation and generation procedure

The methodology involves using template-based prompt techniques \[[17](#ch20.xhtml#r358)\] for generation of lecture preparation material using ChatGPT-3.5 for two important computer science fundamental subjects – Data Structure & Algorithm (DSA) and Object-oriented programming System (OOPS). The seven types of queries for which lecture preparation material could be generated and used by a professor were explored. Then, these queries were given as an input to ChatGPT-3.5 along with a fixed template respective to the type of query defining the structure of the material generated. Further from this pool of queries, 50 queries were extracted, and lecture preparation materials were generated for them using zero-shot prompting \[[18](#ch20.xhtml#r359)\] and few-shot prompting \[[19](#ch20.xhtml#r360)\] on ChatGPT-3.5. The materials generated using each type of prompt technique, for their respective query, were scored and ranked based on the structure and content quality using Gemini Pro and rule-based evaluation, respectively, followed by human evaluation. The framework of the proposed methodology has been visualized in [Figure 10.1](#ch20.xhtml#f71). In order to conduct this study, a set of 274 queries were explored in which DSA and OOPS topics taught in a classroom are included at least once. The queries were further categorized based on the type of content being asked and according to the type. A prompt structure was developed.

![Figure 10.1 Illustration of our proposed lecture preparation process.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_001.jpg)

**Figure 10.1** Illustration of our proposed lecture preparation process.[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f71b)

The considered five types of queries are as follows:

*   **Single Topic Queries (ST):** Collection of 138 queries to provide lecture preparation material on a single topic such as Arrays, Linked Lists, Stack etc.
*   **Topic Combination Queries (TC):** Collection of 70 queries to give lecture material on combination of topics, for example, explain a topic say A for topic B or explain a topic A for topic B in topic C or discuss topic A in topic B or illustrate topic A to topic B i.e. “give lecture preparation material on implementing stacks using linked list.”
*   **Comparison Queries (CQ):** Collection of 11 queries to arrange lecture material on comparison of various algorithm/techniques/methodology like prime query. For example, “Provide lecture preparation material comparing various sorting techniques like selection sort, bubble sort or insertion sort.”
*    **Programming Question Queries (PQ):** Collection of 48 queries to provide lecture material on a programming problem. For instance, “Share lecture preparation material on the programming problem fractional.”
*   **OOPs and Complexity Queries (OCQ):** Collection of seven queries to provide lecture material on OOPs and Complexity related topics. For example, “Share lecture preparation material on inheritance.”

The lecture preparation material, for all the 274 queries, generated using template-based prompting were incorporated in a dataset called as the demonstration dataset.

## **10.4** Prompt techniques

There are several prompting techniques in large language models that are considered essential for successfully directing the model to generate the intended results. In our study, we have incorporated few shots, zero shots, and template-based prompting techniques.

### **10.4.1** Template-based prompting

Template-based prompting is a method used in natural language processing (NLP) where predefined templates or structures are provided to guide the generation of text. In the context of our study, template-based prompting has been used to craft different types of prompts tailored to different query types. The prompt consists of pointers defining the various headings a professor would expect in a lecture preparation material, thus guiding ChatGPT-3.5 in defining the structure of the lecture preparation material. The task of ChatGPT-3.5 is to fill in the content under these headings from its known knowledge. The various headings defined by the pointers in the prompt are as follows:

*   **Topic**: The pointer extracts the topic from the query on which the lecture preparation material should be generated.
*   **Objectives**: The pointer for this heading is to write objectives for the topic.
*   **Theory**: The pointer provides an elaborate theory on the topic present in the query.
*   **Code implementation**: Provides coding implementation of the topic and even though not specified in the prompt. All the codes are in python language in the output. For programming question queries, this heading asks for various ways a programming problem can be solved. For example, Knapsack problem can be solved using greedy algorithm and dynamic programming. The explanation and complexity analysis of each approach is also asked, as shown in [Figure 10.2](#ch20.xhtml#f72).
    
    ![Figure 10.2 Template for each query type.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_002.jpg)
    
    **Figure 10.2** Template for each query type.[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f72b)
    
*   **Explanation of the code**: Provides proper explanation of the code written in code implementation section.
*   **Complexity analysis**: this pointer details the time and space complexity of the code written in the code implementation section.
*   **Beginner Programming Problem**: The pointer for this heading asks ChatGPT-3.5 to provide a beginner level programming question on the topic asked in the query. This exists only for single topic prompts.
*   **The application of the topic**: The pointer for this heading asks ChatGPT-3.5 to provide application of the topic in real life.
*   **Conclusion**: The pointer for this heading asks ChatGPT-3.5 to provide a conclusion of all that that has been discussed in the material above.
*   **Short quiz**: The pointer for this heading asks ChatGPT-3.5 to provide a short quiz along with answers on the topic and what has been discussed in the lecture material.
*   **References**: The pointer for this heading asks ChatGPT-3.5 to provide references to study the topic further. It is also mentioned to give links which are properly working.

Since there are different types of queries, the structure of prompts for the respective queries are also different, even though there are common headings in all types of prompts. The pointer differences in all the prompts are depicted in Figure 10.2.

### **10.4.2** Zero-shot prompting

In this prompting, ChatGPT-3.5 generates lecture preparation material for the given query without any additional illustrations. As shown in [Figure 10.3](#ch20.xhtml#f73), the zero-shot prompt \[[18](#ch20.xhtml#r359)\] consist of the query enclosed within triple backticks and a brief instruction guiding the model to generate lecture preparation material for the mentioned. Zero-shot prompting, even though offers simplicity and flexibility, can lead to distinct responses depending on the interpretation of the model interpretation of the query and the complexity of the requested topic, which is not helpful in the problem area this study targets wherein a proper and consistent structure is required.

![Figure 10.3 Zero-shot prompt generated lecture.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_003.jpg)

**Figure 10.3** Zero-shot prompt generated lecture.[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f73b)

### **10.4.3** Few-shot prompting

In this prompting, ChatGPT-3.5 is provided with a few instances of queries and their respective lecture preparation material extracted from the demonstration dataset, referred to as “shots,” to boost the accuracy and relevance of response. As shown in [Figure 10.4](#ch20.xhtml#f74), the few-shot prompt \[[19](#ch20.xhtml#r360)\] consist of the query, natural language instructing the model to use the demonstrations to understand the structure and shots which may or may not be related to the topic, providing additional context and guidance for the model to generate a material on similar lines. Leveraging these instances, the model obtains a better understanding of what is expected and can generate more customized and thorough responses. The model learns from these examples, leading to more focused and informative outputs as compared to zero-shot prompting.

![Figure 10.4 Few-shot prompting generated lecture.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_004.jpg)

**Figure 10.4** Few-shot prompting generated lecture.[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f74b)

### **10.4.4** Gemini pro with RAG for ranking

Gemini Pro is an advanced natural language processing tool and, in this study, it has been given the task of determining the ranking of lecture preparation material generated using each prompting technique for each query on the basis of the content quality using the context consisting of data related to the topic asked in the query retrieved using retrieval augmented generation (RAG). According to the Google, DeepMind report titled ‘Gemini: A Family of Highly Capable Multimodal Models’ \[[20](#ch20.xhtml#r361)\], the pretraining dataset for Gemini included programming data which makes it a suitable choice for a content evaluator of computer science related material. Further it states that during training of Gemini models, a sequence length of 32,786 tokens were used, and it was observed that they effectively utilize their context length. The report also explored the performance of Gemini models for handling complex prompts, that is, prompts with multiple instructions and the per-instruction accuracy of Gemini Pro was 77.8 ± 2.0% making it adequate for the evaluation phase of this study as the prompt for Gemini Pro consists of the query and its respective lecture materials generated using each prompt techniques as discussed above, a context retrieved using RAG, which is explained in Section 10.4.4.1, natural language instructing the model to analyze and rank the content of lecture material generated by each prompting technique based on the context provided, resulting in a lengthy and a complex prompt as shown in [Figure 10.5](#ch20.xhtml#f75). The insights gained from implementing this technique is discussed in Section 10.5.

![Figure 10.5 Prompt given to Gemini Pro.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_005.jpg)

**Figure 10.5** Prompt given to Gemini Pro.[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f75b)

#### **10.4.4.1** Context retrieval using retrieval augmented generation

According to Ref \[[21](#ch20.xhtml#r362)\], retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) refers to the retrieval of relevant information from external knowledge bases before answering questions with LLMs. RAG has been showcased to significantly enhance model performance for knowledge intensive task Since ranking lecture material is a knowledge intensive task, Gemini Pro is provided with context retrieved using RAG from a pdf containing topics present in DSA and OOPs coursework, stored in chunks in Chroma DB (a vector database). The context obtain has content related to the topic asked in query, and Gemini Pro is prompted to use this context to judge the lecture material in terms of context. This choice of exploring Gemini Pro with RAG as a content evaluator is backed by the attribution aspect of factuality of Gemini API models, that is, if grounded to a given context. The Gemini API models generate a response with the highest degree of faithfulness to the context as mentioned in \[[20](#ch20.xhtml#r361)\].

## **10.5** Experimental setup and results analysis

In this section, the experimental setup and outcomes of the study, which aimed to compare the effectiveness of template-based prompting (TB) with zero-shot prompting (ZS) and few-shot prompting (FS) in generating lecture preparation material for a diverse set of queries, is represented. Additionally, development and utilization of a front-end application was incorporated to facilitate the experimental process.

### **10.5.1** Front-end development

The front-end application uses React.js, providing an intuitive user interface for query submission and result visualization. The front-end allowed researchers to input queries of various types and select the prompting method for lecture generation, streamlining the experimental process. The screenshot of LPM system is show in [Figure 10.6](#ch20.xhtml#f76).

![Figure 10.6 LPM system for students to get study material, and for faculty to prepare study material.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_006.jpg)

**Figure 10.6** LPM system for students to get study material, and for faculty to prepare study material.[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f76b)

### **10.5.2** Test dataset composition and query distribution

Test set has been generated using demonstration data set (see Section 10.3). As discussed, the demonstration dataset consists of five query types: ST, TC, PQ, CQ, and OCQ. Test set is not chosen randomly to ensure a balanced representation of each query type. An equation-based approach is employed to determine the number of queries for each category as shown in Equation (10.1).

*C**Q* \* *x* + *O**C**Q* \* *x* + *T**C* \* *x* + *P**Q* \* *x* = 50(10.1) 

Count of queries in the demonstration dataset is fixed. Using those same count of queries of each category in the demonstration dataset (see Section 10.2 and [Table 10.1](#ch20.xhtml#t38)), Equation (10.1) is transformed to Equation (10.2).

11*x* + 7*x* + 70*x* + 138*x* + 48*x* = 50  (10.2) 

In above Equation (10.2), *x* represents the scaling factor, and their respective constants represent the count of queries belonging to a particular query type in demonstration dataset. For example, in the term 11*x*, 11 represents the count of comparison queries in the demonstration dataset.

**Table 10.1** Dataset details for prompt technique ranking through Gemini[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#t38b)

 
| Query type || \# of queries in demonstration dataset || \# of queries in test dataset |
| --- | --- | --- |
| CQ || 11 || 2 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| OCQ || 7 || 1 |
| TC || 70 || 13 |
| ST || 138 || 25 |
| PQ || 48 || 9 |

The test dataset of all the lecture material generated using prompt engineering techniques into consideration is combined into a list of dictionaries. This dictionary contains the query and the lecture material generated using zero shot (ZS), few shot (FS), and template-based (TB). These data are stored in a JSON file upon which evaluation is performed.

### **10.5.3** Evaluation

This section highlights the evaluation of our proposed technique through rule-based and human evaluation methods. Rule-based evaluation utilizes predefined rules and metrics to measure the performance of LLMs. Human evaluation relies on human judges to assess the quality of the LLM outputs. Combining both rule-based and human evaluation methods allows for a more comprehensive and precise assessment of LLM performance.

#### **10.5.3.1** Rule-based evaluation

In this phase, the generated LPM are evaluated on the basis of a predefined structural criteria which focuses on finding specific keywords, within the generated content, like topic identification, objectives, theory explanation, code implementation, complexity analysis, applications, conclusion, quiz, and references which should be a header in an ideal LPM. For the presence of each component, the generated material is rewarded a score of 1 and conversely, the absence results in a score of 0. Finally, all scores are added and since there are total 10 headings, a score out of 10 is assigned to each lecture material. The results obtained depicts that for the 50 queries in test dataset, the score of LPM generated using template-based prompting (TB\_score) ranges between 9 and 10, for the LPM generated using few-shot, the score (FS\_score) ranges between 6 and 10, and for the LPM generated using zero shot (ZS\_score), the score ranges between 0 and 5, as shown in [Figure 10.7a](#ch20.xhtml#f77). It can also be observed, in [Figure 10.7b](#ch20.xhtml#f77), that count of value of TB score and FS score as 10 is similar but TB score is not below 9 for any query which is the case in zero shot and few shot. Therefore, it can be concluded that according to rule-based evaluation technique, TB prompt technique performed as good as FS prompt technique or better in terms of structural criteria.

![Figure 10.7 (a) Line graph representing the scores given to each of the prompt techniques. (b) Multibar graph visualizing the number of times a particular score is given to each of the prompt techniques for 50 queries on the basis of rule-based evaluation (structural criteria).⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_007.jpg)

**Figure 10.7** (a) Line graph representing the scores given to each of the prompt techniques. (b) Multibar graph visualizing the number of times a particular score is given to each of the prompt techniques for 50 queries on the basis of rule-based evaluation (structural criteria).[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f77b)

#### **10.5.3.2** Gemini Pro with RAG evaluation

In this phase, a prompt is developed for Gemini Pro as discussed in Section 4.4. Gemini Pro with RAG is used to rank the lectures based on content quality of LPM of test set. The ranked results were stored in a CSV file, with columns representing the query and the ranks assigned to ZS, FS, and TB lectures as presented in [Figures 10.8](#ch20.xhtml#f78) and 10.9. The left-side visualization in [Figure 10.8](#ch20.xhtml#f78) represents the number of times a particular type of prompt was ranked first. It is observed that out of 50 queries TB was ranked first maximum time. The right-side visualization in [Figure 10.8](#ch20.xhtml#f78) depicts the distributions of various ranks over each prompt technique that clearly depicts that TB was given rank 1 for most of the queries. For very few queries, it was ranked second but was ranked third way more times than it was ranked second. FS was majorly given second rank, and ZS was majorly given third rank. From the observation, it can be concluded that according to Gemini Pro, with respect to the context retrieved using RAG, even though a TB prompt technique performed better than other techniques for maximum queries in terms of content quality of LPM but for about 25% of the queries it was not the best prompting technique in terms of content.

![Figure 10.8 Generated lecture ranking comparison of template-based (TB), few shot (FS), and zero shot (ZS) using Gemini Pro With RAG.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_008.jpg)

**Figure 10.8** Generated lecture ranking comparison of template-based (TB), few shot (FS), and zero shot (ZS) using Gemini Pro With RAG.[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f78b)

[Figure 10.9](#ch20.xhtml#f79) represents the ranking of TB for each query type by plotting the number of times TB has attained ranks 1, 2, and 3. It can be observed that for each query type, it has been ranked first more than it has been ranked second or third and for ST queries it has been ranked third for about eight queries.

![Figure 10.9 Template-based rankings order for different query type (a) TC, (b) ST, (c) PQ.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_009.jpg)

**Figure 10.9** Template-based rankings order for different query type (a) TC, (b) ST, (c) PQ.[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f79b)

The applied template-based prompt engineering result for test set are shown in [Table 10.2](#ch20.xhtml#t39). It can be observed in results that 48% times TB has received first rank, 18% times TB ranked second, and remaining 26% times TB ranked third in comparison to zero-shot and few-shot prompt engineering technique specifically for LPM generation.

### **10.5.3.3** Human evaluation

During this phase, from the test dataset a query of each type, which is total of five queries, along with their respective LPM generated using the three prompting techniques, that are, TB, FS, and ZS are shared with professors as a survey form. The survey required the participants to define their preferences as first, second, and third for the LPM generated using different techniques according with respect to the queries. The prompting techniques with which a particular LPM is generated were not revealed to the participants, and their ability to give similar preferences were enabled. The results obtained depicted that the LPM generated using FS prompt technique was majorly the first preference of the participants, followed by TB and then ZS. The LPM generated using TB was majorly a second preference, and the one generated using ZS was majorly given the third preference as visualized in [Figure 10.10](#ch20.xhtml#f80). This result also throws light on the gap between the sense of judgement of a human and LLMs like Gemini Pro as content evaluator.

![Figure 10.10 Multibar graph visualizing the count of all the prompt techniques as first, second, and third preference by a human evaluator.⏎](html/images/9781032942346_C010_010.jpg)

**Figure 10.10** Multibar graph visualizing the count of all the prompt techniques as first, second, and third preference by a human evaluator.[⏎](#ch20.xhtml#f80b)

## **10.6** Conclusion

This study involved leveraging advanced natural language processing techniques to generate education content. By employing various prompting techniques like template-based prompting, zero-shot prompting, and few-shot prompting techniques on Chat-GPT-3.5, lecture preparation materials were generated on topics related DSA and OOPs fundamentals and finally the performance of each prompting technique was compared by implementing a three phased evaluation. By carrying evaluation in three phases, we discerned the nuances in structure and content quality among the generated materials. The first two phases of evaluations, that are rule-based (structural criteria) where in for more than 55% of the queries in test dataset, TB has score of 10 out of 10 and for the remaining ones a score of 9 out of 10 which depicts structural completeness and Gemini Pro with RAG (content criteria) where in for about 48 % queries, the LPM generated using TB prompt technique are ranked first depicting content completeness, highlight the efficacy of template-based prompting in consistently producing well-structured and informative lecture content compared to zero-shot and few-shot prompting techniques for majority of the queries, but according to the third phase, that is human evaluation, the material generated using template-based prompting is majorly a second preference. Further, for evaluating generated education content, leveraging Gemini emphasizes on its value as a content evaluator as well as revealing areas where there is a scope of improvement to match human level evaluation. In conclusion, this study contributes to the ongoing discussion about the role of AI in education by throwing light on how content can be generated in educational systems by leveraging AI.
